## D: Dig–double unit house

### Dig
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Be shrewd, sweet Dig, and let those whowill die working. <i>(Jun 21, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/221</i>)

### dig
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  I would not go and dig for any man under [pounds]3 per day. It is killing work. <i>(Aug 14, 1861,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Aug 14 letter from Tuapeka in 1867 Hochstetter NZ 110fn]



 
 <i>n.</i> innings [cricket (not in SOED, ODE)]

>  [Hdg] Astle's dig among the game's greatest. <i>(Mar 19, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),23</i>)

### dig deep
 
 <i>phr.</i> to draw on all one's reserves, make an intense effort [not in ODE, SOED]

### dig down
 
 <i>v.</i> usu. constr. for, to pay out of one's own pocket. Compare dip south.

>  Kia Ora [pounds] 5000 Art Union./ Dig down for that half-crown right now. Be in on the last 5000 [pounds] Art Union without fail. <i>(Sep 15, 1926,Aussie (NZ Section),XV</i>)

### dig-in
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an action caused by poorly aligned points on a buckrake.

>  How frequently a 'dig-in' could have been avoided .. if the tines had been correctly aligned. After a 'dig-in' the points should be always kept within at least 1 in. of their true line. <i>(Nov 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

### dig-out
 
 <i>n.</i> =digger n2.. [(obs. prisoners')]

>  Prisoners [in the Terrace Gaol, Wellington] frequently complain that if the chief gaoler gets a snout on them they go into the dig-out on the slightest provocation. <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9/3/5</i>)

### digger
 
 <i>n.</i> prison detention unit/cell

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> With the key in our possession it became possible to supply any friend of ours who was in the digger with as much tobacco, matches, food, and other delights as he wanted. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,136</i>)

>  Seven days in the Digger on number two diet... They let you have a choice from a selection of books, most of them insulting nursery-rhyme stuff deliberately chosen by Mr Stone, the unhappy screw on day duty in charge of the Digger. <i>(Aug 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  <b>FOOTE</b> Rangipo Prison layout was the usual rectangular plan, two sets of cells facing each other across a central grassed area with one special cell called 'the digger' or 'dummy' to isolate a troublesome inmate. <i>(2000,Bread and Water,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> miner

>  A hundred and one pubs and danceshanties, and them fine girls shakinga leg with the diggers and all goingstrong till daylight. <i>(Nov 7, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,14</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Many of the diggers and storekeepers whohad brought loaded waggons from Otago..were now obliged to leave them behind andtake their stores on with pack-horses. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,68</i>)

>  I am happy to say that the fossickers and mica diggers have "cut it." <i>(Nov 7, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)

>  These "specimens" as they are called by the diggers, show no - or very little sign of being water-worn. <i>(Jul 8, 1859,Auckland Provincial Gazette,VIII(14) : 90</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> Dick Dickson had been a good deal at sea,but for a "digger" was well educated. <i>(1884,Poems & Other Literary Remains,265</i>)

>  I saw a shepherd's wife take her child out of a cradle and sell the latter to a digger for a GBP5 note. <i>(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  These Irish Diggers (they hated to be called miners) made the place as bad or worse than Donnybrook. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,53</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>McNEISH</b> Fred Smith of the Digger's Rest introduced a sport which topped the bill - rat-baiting. <i>(1957,Tavern in the Town,234</i>) [NOTES: 1966 edition]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Mining statutes and digger licenses [sic] were issued, gold commissioners were installed, and escorts were established, who conveyed the gold produce [sic] every fortnight under guard toDunedin. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),112</i>)

>  The knowledge that he has beenrecommended by his C.O. for recognitionhas met with the warm approval of all his"digger" cobbers. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/49 : 8</i>)

### Digger
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Zealand soldier

>  We.. pulled up to announce our arrival to a rather officious-looking officer who wasn't very impressed by a few Kiwi Diggers. <i>(Dec 20, 1944,Hutt & Petone Chronicle,12</i>)

>  Some of the Mounted Rifles who have recently returned to Christchurch are objecting to being called Diggers... It appears that the term was practically unheard of in Palestine and Egypt. <i>(Oct 1919,Quick March,61</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> And splendid with medals and ribbons,/ Old Diggers - the country's cream - Boyd from the days of the Crimea,/ And some from the Boer War,/ <i>(1988,Southlanders at Heart,17</i>)

>  The common method of addressing a New Zealander is to call him "digger," which, from being an abusive term, has been readily and universally adopted and approved by the Pig Islanders themselves. We, the N.Z.R.B., prefer to be addressed as "diggers" rather than "dinks" which was the original mode of address. <i>(Apr 30, 1918,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>) [NOTES: From a letter by Rifleman C A Palmer of Wanganui (nb: date entered in NZDE database was 31/4/1918)]

>  "Digger" is now the common form of salutation between privates. It applied to the Maoris only at one time. They do a lot of pioneer work, such as digging trenches, but as we all do more or less navvy work, the term is universally used among the New Zealanders. Really the meaning is "Friend" now. <i>(Aug 13, 1917,Wanganui Herald,6</i>) [NOTES: This is from a letter from an Auckland soldier;]

>  Tommies and overseas Kangaroos andDiggers enter into conversation withpeasants, which turn often intoconferences. <i>(Jan 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,III/36 : 286</i>)

>  In the colloquial parlance of the"Digger," they came a gentle "gutzer". <i>(Jul 19, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/48 : 274</i>)

### Digger Dick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Who is better equipped.. than "Digger Dick," practical miner, Empire builder, and the very active personality behind "Seddon, New Zealand?" <i>(Feb 6, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

### digger plough
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [now used elsewhere, but first and widely in NZ] a winged plough that breaks down the furrow slice. [also digging plough below]

>  Digging-ploughs are now coming into general use. <i>(Jul 1892,NZ Farmer,Supplement 1</i>)

>  The digger plough, especially on clay land, is to be recommended for preparing land for wheat. Care must be taken, however, to turn up too much clay at the first time of ploughing with a digger. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,353</i>)

>  The lea land should be turned over with the digger plough ... [digging plough] <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,923</i>)

### digger's delight
 
 <i>n.</i> a large felt hat; = digger's hat (WW2) (AND 1940)

>  <b>BAKER</b> New Zealand terms, recorded during the closing twenty years of last century, [include] ... digger's delight, a large felt hat worn by gold-diggers in New Zealand. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,46</i>)

>  digger's delight n. large felt hat worn by N.Z. gum-diggers; <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

### digger's hat
 
 <i>n.</i> a wide-brimmed felt hat [(goldfields and early rural)] (See [also digger's delight](dig-dou.md#diggers-delight))

>  <b>BARKER</b> I must say, [the bushmen] were the most picturesque of the party, being ... dressed in red flannel shirts and leathern knickerbockers and gaiters; they ... wore `diggers' hats,' a head-dress of American origin - a sort of wide-awake made of plush, capable of being crushed into any shape,and very becoming. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,182,1870 ed.</i>)

### digger's lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> a bottle lantern (= bush lantern, colonial lantern, miner's lantern)

>  <b>LUSH</b> The evening was very dark and I had taken up as I left home, a `digger's lantern'; this is usually made out of a brandy [68] bottle with the bottom broken off and a candle stuck in the neck and which, held by the neck, makes a capital `rough and ready' lantern. <i>(Sep 8, 1869,Thames Journals,67,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> ... the other went off in the dark to search the creek bed, using a digger's lantern which was a glass bottle with the bottom removed and a candle stuck in the neck. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,11</i>)

### digger's shirt
 
 <i>n.</i> a blue (? Crimean) woollen shirt.

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> Men ... dress fine and do the grand/ For the age of digger's shirts has passed away. [cf 15. The moleskin and the Crimean shirt are growing scarce apace.] <i>(1894,Voices of Wave and Tree,11</i>)

### digger's weed
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also Californian stinkweed](c-chi.md#californian-stinkweed))

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> STINK WEED ([<i>Navarettia] squarrosa</i>), [formerly named <i>Gilis squarrosa</i>] and called Californian stink weed, digger's weed and yeast plant, occurs chiefly [in river beds] on dry pastures and fields in Marlborough and Otago, where it sometimes becomes dominant over all other herbage. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,139,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>digger's weed [=] Californian stinkweed [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)

>  Californian Stinkweed, Digger's Weed (Navarettia (Gilia) squarrosa) . . . It is sometimes erroneously called "Bathurst burr." <i>(1895,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,III/121</i>)

>  Californian Stinkweed, Diggers' Weed (Navarettia (Gilia) squarrosa) . <i>Ibid.</i> 2 It is sometimes erroneously called "Bathurst burr." <i>(1895,Leaflets for Farmers,No. 22 : 1</i>)

### digger-mania
 
 <i>n.</i> gold-fever.

>  <b>COOPER</b> It is called by some digger-mania, by others digger-phobia. This dire disease is usually developed about a month after the victim has commenced digging; its symptoms are very varied and curious, and are anything but agreeable to those who are unfortunately connected with the poor afflicted one. <i>(Dec 19, 1867,Digger's Diary,23,1978 ed.</i>)

### Diggerland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Other miscellaneous historical (obsolete) names from the early 20th century include.. </i>Diggerland</i>, coined in World War I. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,4 : 2</i>)

>  [Title] "Diggerland"... Can you see thewaving tussock grass that yellows in thesun, To the soft blue haze of thedistant hills that quiver into one?.. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,77</i>) [NOTES: Title of the poem is "Diggerland". The word does not seem to be used anywhere else and not in the poem.]

### Diggers, the
 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ concert party (WW1 and after)

>  <b>HURST</b> Another aspect of the influence of the Great War was evident in 1919, with the appearance of the Diggers. <i>(1944,Music & the Stage in NZ,60</i>)

>  <b>DOWNES</b> The New Zealand Pierrots were at a loose end by this time [sc. 1919] and, more or less as a body, they became the principal members of the new group - the Diggers... For the next twelve years the name of Pat Hanna's Diggers became a byword throughout both countries [sc. Australia & NZ]. <i>(1979,Top of the Bill,58</i>)

>  <b>WOLFE</b> Formed in 1917, this group eventually evolved into the Diggers and were led by Pat Hanna. <i>(1991,Kiwi,34</i>)

### Diggerville
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WOLFE</b> These birds were less of a permanent troupe, although they once converted a barn into a theatre, and named it Diggerville. <i>(1991,Kiwi,34</i>)

>  Just a stone's throw from H.Q. and A.Coy. is what used to be a big farm, butwhich is now known to the boys as"Diggerville". <i>(Aug 30, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/51 : 57</i>)

### digging
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining; incl attrib]

>  In the meantime Mr. C. Ring found it necessary to abandon the first digging, owing to the obstruction offered to his reaching bed rock by the presence of large Quartz Boulders, ... <i>(Nov 9, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/26/165</i>) [NOTES: (Report of Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... I went amongst them every day, and we talked of their digging experiences; ... <i>(Dec 20, 1864,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,79,1914 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> pertaining to a diggings; mining. [(obs.)]

>  <b>FITTON</b> The digging population is not now what it used to be. Diggers are now hard-fisted in more senses of the word than one. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),344</i>)

>  ... while her decks were cleared, to allow of a search for stowaways, and of those other processes known to Custom-house officers and immigration commissioners, her 700 and odd passengers, laden with their swags, digging utensils, and bright tin pannikins and dishes. <i>(Sep 20, 1861,NZ Goldfields,5,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 20 in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 5]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> There are always a few of these unsocial miners [hatters] about every digging. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,138</i>)

>  <b>WASHBOURN</b> The term billy ... originated on the [Collingwood] digging and is still in general use. <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,5</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> He replied that as he had to hold a service at Antonio's Flat (a digging on the Grey Valley) next morning, ... <i>(1930,Reminiscences,137</i>)

>  <b>DILKE</b> ... a gang of diggers and sailors, dressed in the clothes which every one must wear in a digging town unless he wishes to be stared at by every passer-by. <i>(1868,Greater Britain,1/335</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> [Prospective diggers] had only arrived to find the whole thing what is called in digging parlance a `duffer' - i.e., that there was comparatively `nothing' to repay them for even the trouble of digging a single hole. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,121</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... at dinner we had the pleasure of the company ... of a publican from Nokomai, a digging township on the road we were going. <i>Ibid.</i> 197 Pinkerton and Lady came board. Pinkerton is a publican who has made a lot of money in the digging time and his lady or rather as he calls her `My old woman' has ringlets. <i>(Jan 23, 1873,Knocking About in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 23 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 146]

>  <b>HARPER</b> [The West Coast] has become, from a digging point of view, a field of far less importance. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,30</i>)

>  Lyell is a picturesque little digging township ... <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,163</i>)

>  <b>WASHBOURN</b> ... the gold ... [was] so much of a gold-leaf character ... I have frequently [c1860s] seen large specks floating in and out of the dish when panning out there, and these in digging terms are `floaters.' <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,4</i>)

### digging plough
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a style of plough with an extra projecting wing. (See [also digger plough](dig-dou.md#digger-plough))

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Howards Digging Plough .. turns over the furrow slices most perfectly and breaks what is left at the surface by means of a projecting wing or continuation of the mould-board. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,272</i>)

>  Digging-ploughs are now coming into general use. [digging-plough] <i>(Jul 1892,NZ Farmer,Supplement 1</i>)

### diggings
 
 <i>n.</i> a goldfield usu. where surface or alluvial mining in registered claims is being carried out (1851-1853 usu. with reference to Australia or California, often in quotation marks, and prefixed with gold). [Early spg (or var. form) diggins, occas. digins. [Spec. use of digging(s) applied to a goldfield in California, then Australia and NZ.] [1853 Adams Spring Canterbury Settlement 56-57 diggins.] Common pronunciation was `diggins' [check] (Griffiths Collection). 1. [AND 1851.] (Collect. pl. or pl. regarded as a sg., the diggings.)]

>  <b>STEPHENS</b> Thousands of people had already flocked to the diggings from Sydney. <i>Ibid.</i> 546 ... the diggins ' <i>(1851,Letters & Journals,514</i>)

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> diggings ... <i>(1851,Papers,1/94</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Stafford Papers [TS] 1/94 (Featherston) ...]

>  <b>LUSH</b> The excitement respecting the gold `diggings' in Australia rising to fever heat in Auckland. <i>(Jun 23, 1851,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1851 June 23 Lush Auckland Journal (1971) 79]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... gold diggings ... <i>(1852,Journal,7/278,1833 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1852 R. Taylor Journals (1833-) 7/278]

>  <b>FITTON</b> If there be one point on which all those who have visited the diggings agree, it is this - <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),355</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec J.R.Godley's Address in 1856 Fitton NZ 355]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> ... the public was again reassured [of gold discovery] by the appearance of the following noveladvertisment:- sale of/ AUCKLAND GOLD/ Connell & Ridings/ Will sell by Public Auction, at their Mart, Queen-Street,/ This day, Saturday, 11th December,/ ... / A Quantity of Gold Dust, and Gold-bearing Quartz,/ From the `Wynyard Diggings,' Coromandel. <i>(1853,Auckland,105</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 11 Advertisement in 1853 Swainson Auckland 105]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> Some very fine samples of gold from the Waiau diggings ... were brought into town today ... thequantity about five ounces. -->1853 Rochfort Adventures of a Surveyor 20 [He] was always in debt, and shortly after he ran away to the diggings, leaving his salary heavily mortgaged. <i>(1853,Auckland,107</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 13 Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 107]

>  Never mind - the diggings will not always draw people away. In a short time the overflowings of disappointed diggers will bring Ships and labour back and the tide of Immigration will flow again. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/123 Many houses to let in consequence of some persons having gone to the diggings - others over the hill (as they call it) into the Plains. <i>(1853,Journal of Henry Sewell,1980,1/144 ed.</i>)

>  ... the annexed tabular report of the work at this Gold-field since my arrival. ... wherever I and the Messrs. Ring have `prospected' on the Kapanga ..., we have found gold, and that the greatest number of flakes in the prospecting dish, appeared upon washing some earth yesterday at a bar about half a mile lower down the stream ...; and below all the present diggings. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report) Kapanga River Diggings,/ Coromandel harbour,/ 4th November, 1852.]

>  <b>EARP</b> ... emigration to New Zealand has given way to the mania for the Australian gold diggings. <i>Ibid.</i> 248 They have their `Long Tom' at the junction of a small tributary with the Kapenga, below all the other diggers. Neither of the men have ever been at any other diggings. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),98</i>)

>  On the resumed diggings, the parties ... washed [7 1/4oz of dust] ... the quantity set forth, and are continuing their work with every prospect of finding eventually the matrix Gold, which the nuggets now obtained indicate to be in the vicinity of the workings. <i>(Jan 12, 1853,NZ Government Gazette,6/1/2</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report from Coromandel)]

>  The gold seems a little more promising according to reports. Bearded losels wandermore and more up and down the beach [at Nelson] - to and fro the diggings. <i>(May 5, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/263,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A. Domett)]

>  The character of the diggings is improving; the yield of gold is much better. <i>(Jul 4, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  ... at the end of three months (his nose having got exceedingly red in the meantime and smelling worse that ever of bad tobacco), he goes to that refuge for the destitute, the Nelson diggings. <i>(Aug 12, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> I ...am now at the Manokaiou where the diggins are. <i>(Dec 1, 1865,My Dear Bannie,81,1958 ed.</i>)

>  Nothing but one loaf of bread left, could hardly get firewood enough to boil one billy full of tea. Met some mourays who told us of the new digins. <i>(May 9, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,37,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> The diggings [at Dunstan] extend 80 miles one way and fifteen another. If you want to boil a pot you have to hunt about for dead `Wild Irishmen' a nasty prickly little shrub. The high lands abounded with `Spaniards' I suppose from their cut-throat propensities. A kind of very thick grass, grows in a little lump. <i>(Jan 7, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> We travelled to ... the Grey, and back to the Buller, calling at Charleston and Waite's Pakihi's; all familiar diggings to any west-coast digger, and very rowdy diggings too. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,76</i>)

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> Bullen ... owned a clothing store in the principal `diggins' of the day. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,111</i>)

>  diggings: place where gold is mined. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  diggings ... A place where gold-mining is carried on. b. on the diggings, the idiom (rather than in) within the precincts of a goldfield. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),122,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... I slept for the first time `on the diggings;' and ... I confess I would have preferred a warm bed ... to the slab table with which I was compelled to content myself, every bunk and bed-place being preoccupied, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,10</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> He'd a mart on the diggings here, and in addition,/ He did a great deal selling things on commission. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.5/49</i>) [NOTES: Life of an Auctioneer]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLEAN</b> Someone found a `diggins' at the Hutt. <i>(1857,Papers,14/272</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The men were only diggers who had caught sight of the tent, and thought that Ernest and Harold had a good `diggings' there in a quiet way. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,184</i>)

### diggings, what the
 
 <i>exclam.</i> what the dickens!

>  <b>SMYTH</b> What the `diggings' do you think you're doing? <i>(1930,Wooden Rails,144</i>)

### diggler
 
 <i>n.</i> an egg

>  <b>McGILL</b> digglers eggs. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,25</i>)

### diggly bones
 
 <i>n.</i> [(children's game) ca1922 Pukekohe R. Mason]

### dilberry
 
 <i>n.</i> a foolish or naive person.

>  Any man who thinks and reads beyond the immediate requirements of getting a good job is a fool - `wet', `gormless', `dilberry', etc. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23/221</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

### dill
 
 <i>n.</i> stupid person; fool; dupe (occas.), awkward, clumsy person. [ca1941 St Patrick's Silverstream]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> A dupe; an easy victim: Australian, esp. confidence tricksters': since the 1920s. In, e.g. Baker, 1942. Perhaps silly billy>Billy>Billy?)> Bill> by `rhyme', dill; but prob. ex daffydill, illiterate for daffodil ... (cf pill @n with similar, though harsher, connotation) perhaps dialectal relation to dull (OE dyle), or perhaps a NZ shortening from dilly (aj) (though the reverse may be true, dilly (aj) from dill @n; dill is a more friendly term than dool (aj doolish) which is applied in anger or disgust with somebody's stupidity. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,186</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the [slang] expressions listed by children in two schools are: `Up the shoot, going eyes out, bullswool, go jump in the lake, ... shivery dick, shiver me timbers, ... you're telling I, got iton the brain, like smoke in a wheelbarrow' (Kaitaia), `Sheila, crow, bud, frig it, grub, holy moses, blast it, mole, balls to you, shut your face, ... nerts to you, go bite your back, have a roll, fluff off, scram, ants in your pants, rag bag, ... you're bats, dronk, drongo, dill, dip, dippy, ya big lug, greetings gruesome, who dented your beak. (South Wellington). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/677</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Yeah. The others are proper dills. Except Arthur's side-kick.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,261</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> [c1920-1950] there are vigorous Slanging Contests in which the contestants try to outdo each other by using phrases like the following: `You're up the shoot.' ... `Dronk!' `Drongo!' `Dill!' `Dippy!' `Drip!' <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,138</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> You're a dill to do things like that with a sheila like that. Don't get tied up with her. <i>(1973,NZ Jack,124</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Besides I'd have looked a bit of a dill reporting something like that to the police.' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,85</i>)

>  dill n. Austral. inf. a fool [C20: < DILLY 2] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,313</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 209]]

>  dillbrain: idiot <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dill, dillbrain silly or incompetent person, possibly from daffodil, where `daffy' means daft; eg `Wear ear-muffs with that weedeater, dillbrain.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,36</i>)

### dillbrain
 
 <i>n.</i> a fool (an elaboration of dill).

>  The Nats have had so many absolute political gifts lately, but the minute they get the Government on the ropes, the dill-brains start slogging themselves on the chops instead. <i>(Jul 19, 1987,Sunday News,37</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dill, dillbrain silly or incompetent person, possibly from daffodil, where `daffy' means daft; eg `Wear ear-muffs with that weedeater, dillbrain.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,36</i>)

### dilly
 
 <i>adj.</i> silly; cranky; foolish. (See [also dill n](dig-dou.md#dill))

>  His Worship: `Was the accused sober?' Witness: `Well, he looked muzzy.' His Worship: `What do you mean by that?' Witness: `He looked either drunk or a shingle short.' His Worship: `A what?' Witness: `A screw loose.' His Worship: `I don't understand you.' Witness: `He seemed to have a rat in his garret.' His Worship: `Oh, speak English!' Witness: `Off his dot - balmy [sic] - dilly.' Sergeant: `He says the man was mad, your Worship.' Witness: `No, I didn't. I said he was either drunk or ratty.' <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/1/6</i>)

>  dilly a. NZ & Austral. stupid, silly. [daft and silly] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,209</i>)

>  dilly adj. Austral. sl. silly [C20: < E dialect, ? < SILLY] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,313</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 209]]

### dim as a Toc H lamp
 
 <i>phr.</i> very dim

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Dim as a Toc H lamp he was - This country would be in the cart properly but for us Scandies - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

>  dim as a Toc H lamp <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

### dim-bulb
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Best character so far: Calvin, Ben and Frank's dim-bulb brother. The elevator may not go all the way to the top floor, but on this show he fits right in. <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),71</i>)

### dimple
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an underground depression in land, caused by ploughing of native land unsuited to cultivation.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> The dimples usually occupy individually an area of 2 to 5 square yards. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,100</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> Subcutaneous dimples developed on deep loess near the foot of the Port Hills ... <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,116</i>)

### dimpling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) underground soil erosion caused by ploughing, that results in concave depressions on land surface.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> Subcutaneous dimpling appears to be limited to land which, in the course of European occupance, has been ploughed at least once. Under these circumstances dimpling replaces sub-surface sheet wash. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,100</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> From Marlborough to Southland, short steep slopes ploughed out of tussock or ploughed after bush burns, exhibit dimpling in various stages. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,101</i>)

### ding
 
 <i>v.</i> to dent or damage

>  [Hdg] New jet dinged by snow plough... The first of two identical second-hand aircraft bought to replace the air force's troubled Boeing 727s was damaged when a snow-clearing vehicle backed into it and left a gash under its nose. <i>(Apr 16, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a (small) dent (in a car's bodywork etc.)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Dylan barges past, putting a ding in my aging bodywork as he goes, releasing the ball to Rajiv. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,107</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> money? [NZism?]

>  Since rather more than half the voters were too stupid to vote for the far-sighted Dr Brash..the politicians are going to have to do something with the ding. <i>(Dec 18, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  Of course, people are not going sentimentally to put their ding into something just because it has a proletariat or patriotic political flavour. <i>(Jul 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### ding-a-ling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station cook.

>  station cook - a ding-a-ling. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### ding-dong
 
 <i>adj.</i> the gen. Eng. intens. (1864), used for dinkum; related to dinkum cf 1944.

>  [Aunt Ada'] ... Ding-dong New Zealander, that's me `a ding-dong New Zealander' for the catchphrase `a dinkum New Zealander' <i>(Aug 11, 1941,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### dingbat
 
 <i>n.</i> a stupid or fooish person

>  <b>RENEE</b> And Gertrude snorts. 'Another bloody dingbat,' she says. <i>(1997,Snowball Waltz, The,183</i>)

>  Why on earth do you employ such a dingbat columnist as Frank Haden? <i>(May 26, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  I'm not suggesting that there was any malice involved. There were one or two dingbats, but that's life. <i>(Apr 21, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cobbler, or sheep that is difficult to shear.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> 'I don't know what the hell I'm doing up here shearing these f-- dingbats,' he would say .. Look at these dingbats - wool all over their eyes ... <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,147</i>)

>  dingbat - a cobbler. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,3</i>)

>  A sheep that is difficult to shear: a dingbat <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a halfwit

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Drongos,' Max hisses. `Wart-heads. Dingbats. Halfwits.' <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,32</i>)

>  dingbat n. Austral. sl. a crazy or stupid person. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,314</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 209; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 135]]

>  dingbat n. an eccentric; (Coll.) officer's servant in World War 1. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dingbat odd or silly person; plural form means madness, either of rage or delirium tremens; singular was an Anzac officer's servant in WWI, said to be a merger of dingo and batman. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,36</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(transf.)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> `I don't know what the hell I'm doing up here shearing these f---- dingbats,' he would say. `... Look at these dingbats - wool all over their eyes; ... A man must be bloody mad to shear them? <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,147</i>)

### dingbatitis
 
 <i>n.</i> drunkenness or excessive drinking.

>  <b>LORD</b> `Dingbatitis', `drinking', `doped', `dizzy', or on `the drunk' <i>(1921,Ballads of Bung 'Stunology',1976 ed.</i>)

### dingbats
 
 <i>n.</i> delirium tremens, a mad fit (esp. in phr. have the dingbats)

>  'Ave you got the dingbats. <i>(Sep 27, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,109</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> George, 'e 'ad the dingbats. <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,96</i>)

>  Dingbats. - Slang, of Australian origin, for delirium tremens. The dingbats, I believe, are really the snakes, weasels, etc., which a sufferer sees. ([1890-1910], but since when I am not sure.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> 2. the dingbats. Delirium tremens: Australians' and New Zealanders': C.20; ob. - 3. Hence, madness: id.: from ca 1905; ob. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,221</i>)

>  dingbats n. (Sl.) delirium tremens; have the dingbats, to be off one's head. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> (Ac) ... dingbats, delirium tremens <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... dingbats: delirium tremens <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Boozin' again! You'll end up with the dingbats, you will. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,42</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> He had lain for a month in a native hut visited by pubescent girls who supplied him with home comforts and African beer. ... `I had the dingbats for a while,' he said. ...@Well, when I had the dingbats there was a bloody great Negro's face an inch away from me all the time. It must have been the bad grog.' <i>(1985,Horse,25</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 25]

>  dingbats n. (Sl.) delirium tremens; have the dingbats, to be off one's head. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> GRADY: It could be the dingbats. [of an alcoholic who has died] <i>(1982,Collected Plays,124</i>) [NOTES: (1968) 1982 Baxter Collected Plays (Band Rotunda)124]



 
 <i>adj.</i> mad; crazy. [ca1941 Havelock e.g. Go dingbats, `go mad']

>  [Children yell] Your mother's dingbats. <i>(1949,Landfall,11/146</i>) [NOTES: [?check ?3]]

### dinginess
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [wool handling] dust, sand or soil in fleecewool.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> .. great differences in dinginess can be found among fleeces from a flock of sheep that have all the same opportunities to become affected. Quite apart from a certain amount of dinginess which might come about through particular living habits, for example, the habitual grazing locations of individuals on a block ... <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,81</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Depending on the source and kind of dinginess some reasonable means of control can be suggested. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,82</i>)

### dingle stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HELMER</b> `This little hori's damned hungry,' Crump said, lifting the tea billy from the dingle stick. `I could scoff a real binder, just quietly.' <i>(1964,Stag Party,80</i>)

### dingles
 
 <i>n.</i> [pl. diggles, taws. ca1957-1963 Horowhenua The marbles your put in the centre of the ring.]

### dingo
 
 <i>n.</i> [Australian]

>  What's worse; what r-u-u-l-l-y hurts, is that far too often they have been beaten by those they call the dingoes - Australians like Nicole Livingstone, Phil Adams, Martin Vinnicombe and Sean Carlin <i>(Jan 29, 1990,Sydney Herald</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural, informal) a Merino sheep.

>  Merinos are generally known as dingoes. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,4</i>)

### dingy
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of wool) discoloured by condition or dust. [(woolgrowers')]

>  Dingy: Discoloration due to condition and dust. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] [of fleece-wool] carrying excessive amounts of sand, earth and dust.

>  dingy - a yellow discoloured wool, heavy in condition. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Dingy wool - in practice this kind of wool is described with a variety of names. In New Zealand the most common names are 'dingy' .. There [in Australia] the term 'dingy' is most often used to describe discolored wool in general. The following discussion is confined to wool bearing excessive amounts of sand, earth and dust and for simplicity it is called dingy wool. It is the rule rather than the exception to find very weak back wool in dingy fleeces. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,81</i>)

### dining room
 
 <i>n.</i> in a NZ house often a name for the `best' room, the `front' room; lounge.

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> He said he wouldn't mind [a cup of tea], so she went out to the kitchen ... When she returned to the dining-room he was sitting on the chesterfield. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,159,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARTLETT</b> On the mantelpiece in the dining room, among the ornaments and family photographs, stood an especially fine piece of kauri gum the colour of clear honey. It had probably been dug by Bob on his farm or on a gum-digging `holiday'. <i>(1947,Emigrants, The,102</i>)

### Dink
 
 <i>n.</i> with various descriptive epithets, from shape of shoulder patches. Arse-up Dinks, Fourth Battalion[an upside-down triangle??]; Diamond Dinks, First Battalion; Square Dinks; Triangle Dinks, Second Battalion; see (Square) Dinkums.

>  <b>McKINLAY</b> See Square Dinks/ Fair Dinks. <i>(1939,Ways & Byways,24</i>)

>  In the Great War there were Square Dinks, the Triangle Dinks and I won't tell you what we called the other ones but their triangle was upside down - you know what soldiers mean. <i>(Feb 12, 1953,2ZB</i>) [NOTES: (Lux Money-Go-Round Programme)]

>  <b>AITKEN</b> Next came the New Zealand Rifle Brigade from Mersa Matruh, first the 2nd Battalion, nicknamed the `Square Dinks' (a `square dinkum bloke' being idiomatic Australian for a genuine fellow) from the distinctive black patch on the upper sleeve; later the `Diamond Dinks', who wore a diamond patch and had been in action with British and Sikhs in the defeat of the Senussi on Christmas Day. The alternative name of the Rifle Brigade, the E.L.O. (the Earl of Liverpool's Own) had been quietly allowed to lapse. <i>(1963,Gallipoli to the Somme,46</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Triangle Dinks Third Battalion, NZ Rifle Brigade, WWI, from shoulder flash. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,114</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... a few days after Messines, D Company 4 Battalion, NZRB walked out of Plocgsteert Wood. A complete mystery. Can any old Dink throw any light on it? <i>(1978,Soldier Country,123,1990, p. 82 ed.</i>)

>  [Hdg] Trophy of "The Dinks". How Christchurch's much discussed howitzer was captured. <i>(Jul 4, 1928,NZ Free Lance,11</i>)

>  Williams was an "Ak Emma" and Henrys was a "Dink", a fair dinkum "digger". <i>(Nov 27, 1926,NZ Observer,Xmas Annual : 28</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Sometimes even Dinkums proved too long and was shortened to "Dinks". <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,10</i>)

>  The common method of addressing a New Zealander is to call him "digger," which, from being an abusive term, has been readily and universally adopted and approved by the Pig Islanders themselves. We, the N.Z.R.B., prefer to be addressed as "diggers" rather than "dinks" which was the original mode of address. <i>(May 31, 1918,Wanganui Chronicle,4</i>) [NOTES: From a letter by Rifleman C A Palmer of Wanganui]

>  We were talking with the 'Dinks' thatevening. <i>(Mar 14, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/14 : 41</i>)

>  Originally there were two canteens inthis camp, but since the "Dinks" camehere in September both have beenenlarged. <i>(Jan 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,III/34-35 : 24</i>)

>  That Dink now gets quite mad when theboys taunt him about a certain pigskinH.E. <i>(Jul 19, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/48 : 272</i>)

>  The Drill Hall barely accommodated 100"dinks" from all over the Dominion whoattended the reunion of veterans of theNew Zealand Rifle Brigade on Saturdaynight. <i>(Jun 7, 1948,Dominion,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the Dinkums [(WW1)]

>  <b>ANON</b> I have seen a number of university men of the Rifle Brigade, generally known to us as the Dinks - a name they have cheerfully accepted and try to live up to. [fn] The newspapers urging enlistment in 1915 declared that the later men, joining the forces with motives of reasoned patriotism, would be more praiseworthy than the `impetuous spirits' of the Main Body. Hence thesardonic nickname of `the Dinks' - dinkum soldiers - attached to the new brigade. <i>(Mar 11, 1916,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1916 March 11 Letter from Egypt in 1965 Malthus ANZAC 156]

>  <b>INGRAM</b> This evening the C.O. ... imparted to them the information that the Divison with the exception of the Rifle Brigade (`The Dinks') was going back ... <i>(Sep 1, 1917,Anzac Diary,40,1987 ed.</i>)

>  <b>102</b> Our Brigade (2nd) and the Dinks were to advance with the Australians. <i>(Oct 28, 1917,Great Adventure,1988</i>) [NOTES: (Gray)]

>  The Dinks were laying cable in the forward areas, ... <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,171</i>) [NOTES: [WW1]]

>  <b>STEWART</b> Colloquially the Rifle Brigade Battalions were known as `Dinks', a term applied to them on their arrival in Egypt, 1916, and alluding to the special patronage which they enjoyed of the Governor-General of the Dominion, and to their peculiarities of drill and dress. <i>(1921,NZ Division,vi fn</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The Dinks - the New Zealand Rifle Brigade - had been getting it in the neck here with every sort of music the opposition could turn on. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,204</i>)

>  The New Zealand Rifle Brigade, perhaps better known as the `Dinks', will hold a reunion in Wellington ... (How did they get their name? The black buttons, badges, and blazes, and theunusual drill of the New Zealand Rifle Brigade led to their being dubbed `the dinkum soldiers.' Shortened to `The Dinks' it was accepted as an honourable title.) <i>(Mar 13, 1955,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... he spent most of his time ... playing poker and drinking beer with ... his old cobbers who were in the Dinks in the first war. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,153</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> pertaining to the Rifle Brigade, or a member of it. [(WW1)]

>  <b>MILLER</b> A Dink officer got one in the arm. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,159</i>) [NOTES: >[1917 Oct 12] 1939 Miller Camps, Tramps, and Trenches 159]

>  <b>INGRAM</b> At about 9.30 last night we started off in [112] the pouring rain to relieve the Fourth Dink Battalion. <i>(Sep 13, 1918,Anzac Day,111,1987 ed.</i>)

### dink
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [double-bank](dou-dyn.md#double-bank))



 
 <i>n.</i> ducking movement

>  Tidy cover defending, clever dink through for Gear's first try. <i>(Nov 28, 2005,NZ Herald,C3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> money, cash. Compare jink [(obs., ?children's) ca1930 Dunedin schoolchildren's p.c. A. Campbell. ca1939 Havelock c. (schoolboys' obs.) [?F.?a different root for something worthless: or ?ironic use; nf OED2.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a cheap clay marble; dinky [1931 p.c. George Turner Diary (Dannevirke]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> Usu. in collocations fair dink(s), true dink to express or imply doubt or assert the truth of a statement.

 <i>adj.</i> [aka dinky-di]

>  That is beyond me, dinky di. <i>(Nov 15, 1934,Evening Post,12</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [?F. dinkie Sc and Us something small and neat.]

>  <b>LELAND</b> Dinkie. A beautician's metal clip is used for holding back HAIR. It is similar in shape to an alligator CLIP. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A</i>)

### dinkum
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Perhaps his band was not of the "dinkum"sort! <i>(Sep 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/2 : 48</i>)

>  I haven't done anything - dinkum I haven't. <i>(Aug 19, 1924,Otago Witness,73</i>)

>  I'm a dinkum saw-doctor, too, though I sez it meself. <i>(Nov 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,96</i>)

>  I've dinkum swallowed it this time. <i>(Jul 5, 1943,NZEF Times,III/106</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

>  It's the real dinkum Kiwi heritage. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Up to muddy Trentham came the Dinkumitesone day. <i>(Mar 14, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/14 : 41</i>)

### Dinkums
 
 <i>n.</i> [When my eye first fleetingly caught this par, I thought it referred to NZers. This actually states that it refers to Australians but the similarity of names is such that I wonder if this is a sub-editor's misinterpretation of the review or his precis of another paragraph.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Sometimes even Dinkums proved too long and was shortened to "Dinks". <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,10</i>)

>  A reviewer of Captain Bean's book "Letters from France," quotes the nicknames of the successive drafts of Australian forces to fight for the cause of the Old Country. "The Tourists" came first, - people out to see what the adventure was like. The next shipment were the "Dinkums", the true men; followed by the "Super-Dinkums". Then arrived the "War Babies" and the "Chocolate Soldiers", and after them the "Hard Thinkers" who thought very hard before they came, and last of all the "Neutrals". <i>(Oct 9, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  The "Dinkums" have been tested, and tested in a manner even comparable to what our most early arrivals had to stand on that fateful day, April 25th, 1915. <i>(Sep 29, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,52</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a formal suit (full tuxedo)

### Dinnergate
 
 <i>n.</i> [The media hype surrounding Mrs Shipley's dinner with Kevin Roberts, the man from Saatchi and Saatchi.]

>  [Hdg] Dinnergate <i>Ibid.</i> 21 However, Dinnergate has not been the only recent occasion when the outpouring of the Roberts charisma and gusto has been a mixed blessing. <i>(Mar 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  "Dinnergate" showed Shipley is perilously accident-prone under heavy fire, and Labour will be searching for the issues that probe the Prime Minister's weaknesses. <i>(Mar 7, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Mr McCarten said "Dinnergate" had checked National's upward surge at the start of the year... Mr Munro conceded that the public was still struggling to work out what "Dinnergate" had been about. <i>(Mar 16, 1999,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Check his new website and.. You can read that the nastiest thing anyone's ever done to him is "skewering by assorted media commentators on dinnergate." <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Evening Post,10</i>)

### DINZ
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Deer Industry New Zealand [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Deer Industry New Zealand (DINZ) has taken the unprecedented step of teaming up with the British Deer Farmers Association (BDFA) in order to stimulate public demand for farm-raised venison. <i>(Jun 2005,Deer Industry News,20</i>)

### dip
 
 <i>n.</i> mine entrance

>  <b>HUNT</b> They'd have to put in another "dip" - another entrance [sc. to the mine]. <i>(1981,Southern Monthly Magazine,132</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bath in which sheep are washed, to kill parasites in skin and wool.

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. leading to the dip, an enclosed floor, resting on a pivot, which, when filled with sheep tilted up ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> At the end of the dip are two draining-pens, used alternately. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,88</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  After sheep dipping, the dip is used for soaking the droppers in. <i>(Aug 1900,NZ Farmer,331</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is wise to build a dip. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The yards consist of two essential parts: a) crush pens leading to the dip; and b) drainage pens leading from the dip. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,358</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> An alternative to the swim dip or race type was the 'pot.' <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,213</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> There are various forms of dip such as race, well, pot and shower dip. [dip-wash, walk-through, tub-dip etc] <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,257</i>)

>  Now I flung it, flung it into the poisonous dip, the trough full of sheep piss and Cooper's yellow chemical to kill the parasites. <i>(Oct 20, 1991,Dominion Sunday Times,25</i>)

>  The Provincial Government have set off a piece of ground at Wanganui as a sheep dip. <i>(Jul 8, 1873,Wellington Independent,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> put (sheep) through a dip



 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep dip (the bath)



 
 <i>n.</i> sheep dip (the chemical liquid) [aka sheep dip]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to rid sheep and cattle of lice, keds, other parasites and fungi by placing in a spray, race, swim, pot or swim-through dip or by using pour-on.

>  To dip a thousand sheep in tobacco water costs, in material alone, GBP13 10s.; while with sulphur and soda the material would only cost GBP4 18s. <i>(Aug 22, 1862,Colonist (Nelson),4</i>)

>  These sheep, after being landed, must be dipped. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> .. make use of this as soon as your sheep arrive, and are fit to bear it, dipping them in a strong decoction of tobacco ... <i>(1860,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,26</i>)

>  Sheep have to be dipped three months before they quit Nelson ... <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,43</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  .. and if a man's sheep trespass on to his neighbour's run he has to dip his neighbour's also. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,44</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As the sheep are slightly infected with scab, we have to dip them periodically. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,87</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  Dipped ram hoggets. <i>(Jul 9, 1878,Johnston Diary</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Drafted off the woollies, dipped the rest. <i>(Jan 27, 1917,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  Hoggets that weren't dipped when they were shorn in October, or lambs not dipped at weaning may need dipping with an effective pour-on off-shears in autumn. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Autumn 7</i>)

>  In New Zealand she sheep farmer is required to dip by law. <i>(1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,100</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> All told, with wethers, some dry ewes and the hoggets, there were just on 5,000 to dip. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,19</i>)

>  Accused also said that his sheep had already been dipped once, and that he intended to dip again next week. <i>(Jun 22, 1859,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a powder, paste or liquid that is mixed with water for the dipping of sheep.

>  <b>TIFFEN</b> The dip was mixed in four-hundred-gallon tanks ... <i>(1845,Journal,108</i>) [NOTES: in Cresswell (1949) Early New Zealand Families]

>  It would be decidedly a good plan to dip both ewes and lambs off the shears with a good non-poisonous dip. <i>(Nov 20, 1915,NZ Journal of Agriculture,456</i>)

>  There are several kinds of dips, such as fluid, powder and paste. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,13</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> I think that the most unpleasant part of the job was scrubbing the posts and rails and gates of the sheep yards with 'dip' but it had to be done to stamp out the disease [scab]. <i>(1922,Reminiscences of a New Chum in Otago,18</i>)

>  The best feature about these dips, however, is the beneficial results so noticeable in the wool at shearing time. Each of these dips is ideal for its own particular purpose. <i>(Jan 1, 1929,NZ Farmer,23</i>)

>  It is obvious that the different dips which are now available are all produced for a specific purpose. <i>Ibid.</i> Oct. 25 [It] should give protection against maggot fly for 2 to 3 months when used in plunge or shower dips. <i>(Oct 1972,NZ Journal of Agriculture,24</i>)

>  The basic ingredient used in all poisonous dips is the powerful poison arsenic.<i>Ibid.</i> 199 Sheep should not be dumped into the dip head first. <i>(1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60/105</i>)

>  The dip manufacturers.. are reluctant for trade reasons to recommend any definitive number of sheep after which the wash should be discarded. <i>(1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60/201</i>)

>  Please forward to Waihoa Downs at your earliest convenience another five cases of "Puritas Sheep Dip." <i>(Nov 27, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  There are many who use a solution of arsenic as a dip for sheep, which has undoubtedly the recommendation of economy of cost, and I believe, when judiciously applied, to be quite as efficacious as tobacco, as far as curing the scab goes. <i>(Dec 2, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

### dip one's lid
 
 <i>phr.</i> to raise one's hat [ca1935 (Havelock) -->19 - Partridge D.S. [?p] Dip one's lid ... Aust: -1916 Dennis]

### dip paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a field or paddock containing a sheep dip.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Out in the dip paddock a few enthusiasts were busy marking out the pitch and planning boundaries. <i>(1949,High Country Days,58</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Sharp on twelve, all hands trooped to the dip paddock ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,59</i>)

### dip post
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a specially footed and stayed post used in a dip or incline on a fenceline.

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Dip posts in soft boggy spots to be securely anchored by fastening two strands of No 8 wire ... <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,414</i>)

>  Hunter fence is particularly suitable for hilly country as it eliminates 'dip posts.' <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,30</i>)

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> We had one dip-post being stapled, ready to force the wire under ... <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,55</i>)

>  Fence posts on a bend (angle posts) or in a hollow (dip posts) need special attention in fence construction ... [dip-post] <i>(Feb 10, 2002,RD1.com,Farm Management Agfact.</i>)

### dip south/tip south
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put one's hand in one's pocket for money

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>South, dip </i>. To put one's hand in one's pocket for money, esp. if it is running low: New Zealanders': C. 20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,803</i>)

>  `To dip South' is to spend much needed money; ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Terms originated in the first Great War [include] ...<i> to tip south </i>, to put one's hand into one's pocket <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

### dip yard
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Gordon Edge had built a first-class set of drafting yards.. and alongside these the old dip yards were a real eyesore. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,112</i>)

### dip-hand
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a worker at dipping yards and sheep dip.

>  The first dip-hand was the yarder up, whose duty it was to keep working the sheep forward as the final small pen and the yard leading into it were emptied. <i>(1888,Textile Educator I,261</i>)

>  .. the third dip-hand, an old soldier, had the responsibility of keeping up the supply of hot water and soap. <i>(1888,Textile Educator I,262</i>)

### dip-yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheepyard adjoining a sheep dip.

>  <b>BRABAZON</b> Worked at dip-yard fence. <i>(Feb 27, 1908,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> As soon as the dip-yard gate was opened, they knew what to expect, and fought every inch of the way to the water's edge. <i>(1954,On the Hill,23</i>)

### dipped
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] bathed or sprayed [in dipping fluid to kill parasites etc.]

>  <b>BISLEY</b> .. we have received into our own stores wool from the same station, and from the same class of sheep, dipped and undipped ... <i>(1897,Practical Treatise on Wool and Sheep Breeding,51</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> The dipped sheep had to be confined to a certain part of the Run ... <i>(1958,Sturdy Sons,128</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> On the way back to Te Rata I met Mick Williams the head shepherd from Waipaoa Station with three shepherds taking a very large mob of dipped lambs up the river. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,107</i>)

### dipper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He says locals in the know will be heading to "the dipper" for a prime view of cars sliding their way around the hill. <i>(May 27, 2004,Wellington Contact,4</i>)

>  He says locals in the know will be heading to "the dipper" for a prime view of cars sliding their way around the hill. <i>(May 27, 2004,Wellington Contact,4</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed at the sheep dip and dipyards.

>  <b>FISHER</b> Ralph's attitude towards the 'drifters' - gangs of scrubcutters, shearers, crutchers, footrotters, dippers, fencers and dockers was quite different. <i>(1978,Dolphins and Killer Whales,45</i>)

>  . . we have in this country persons who go from farm to farm and find all the apparatus at so much per score, and get a good living at the business, and the farmer runs no risk afterwards by using poisoned tubes, as these are brought by the dipper. <i>(Mar 21, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,10</i>)

>  The slovenly manner in which sheep are sometimes dipped leads, with the former variety , to an imperfect solution and unequal distribution of the remedial agent, with, as a result, death of some of the last to be dipped, or loss of fleece, besides the poisoning of grass and impregnatioin of water courses, while the so-called non-poisonous dips are ineffectual, unless more time and care is bestowed than the average dipper cares to take. <i>(Mar 6, 1894,Bruce Herald,4</i>)

### dipping
 
 <i>n.</i> sheep dipping

>  Dipping would be over in a week or two followed a month or so later by crutching, <i>(Dec 9, 1924,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  One single important error at the time of dipping can seriously affect the end result. <i>(Oct 1972,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)

>  We have finished dipping our sheep. <i>(Apr 8, 1924,Otago Witness,72</i>)

>  By systematic dipping this particular disease [<i>sc.</i> scab] has been eradicated from New Zealand flocks. <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Agriculture,57/477</i>)

>  Careless dipping is a waste of money. The early pastoralists were faced with the necessity of treating their ever-increasing flocks in an expeditious manner - and so were developed the swim dips and draining pens and other arrangements with which every sheep farmer is now all too familiar. <i>(1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60/101</i>)

>  If you started chipping men after a few hours' dipping, you'd soon be without any men at all. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,20</i>)

>  Control [<i>sc.</i> of green blowfly] is by dipping, spraying or jetting with organophosphates. <i>(Feb 1972,NZ Journal of Agriculture,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McRAE</b> Tobacco was grown mainly for dipping scabby sheep in the juices, although scab was not at that time a major problem. <i>(1963,By the Braes of Balquether,32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Government seem to be in part alive, by.. erecting a dipping apparatus nearly adjoining the cattle sale-yards at Remuera. <i>(Sep 20, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the washing of sheep to rid them of parasites, insects, fungi etc.

>  <b>WELD</b> .. an apparatus for dipping in case of disease, should at once be erected. <i>(1860,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,26</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> If a single infected sheep is left undipped on the run, it will infect all others, in which case all the trouble and cost of dipping is wasted. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,87</i>) [NOTES: (1935)]

>  In the case of sheep affected with lice or ticks the Inspector can order an immediate dipping. The dipping of long-woolled and crossbred sheep is made compulsory once a year. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,223</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Although dipping is compulsory, the dipping material, providing it is effective, is left to the choice of the owner ... <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,358</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> Dipping time was hard work in the old swim-through type of dip, especially if the sheep had 'ergot'. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,11</i>)

>  .. the practice of dipping is less than 200 years old and was developed in Australia and New Zealand. <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Summer 32</i>)

### dipping crutch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long pole with an S-shaped hook on the end, used to push under sheep's heads in a swim, pot, or plunge dip. (See [crutch](con-cyp.md#crutch),[dipping pole](dig-dou.md#dipping-pole))

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> My task was on the edge of the dip, armed with a dipping crutch to plunge each sheep under, for lice swarm up on to the heads of affected sheep as they swim. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,35</i>)

>  <b>COATES</b> .. the older sheep did all they could to hold back from that cold plunge and the following push to the bottom of the dip by the man on the dipping crutch. <i>(1995,Dining With the Cats,67</i>)

### dipping muster
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gathering in of sheep for dipping or spraying.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> On high or rough country there are always straggler sheep which dodge the dipping muster. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,74</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> In those days there were three annual musters - for shearing, dipping and autumn muster. <i>(1965,High Endeavour,188</i>)

### dipping pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small enclosure at sheep dipping yards, where sheep are drafted into small numbers appropriate for a dipping bath. (See [dipping yard](dig-dou.md#dipping-yard))

>  .. sheep entering the dipping pen will run much easier if they see the decoys before they see the dip itself. The dipping pen will hold 30 sheep. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Then with a gesture typical of him, Andrew pulled his cloth hat down over his forehead and made for the dipping pens. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,191</i>)

### dipping season
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A step in the right direction would be to have the dipping season shortened to one month for all travelling sheep. <i>(1898,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VI/47</i>)

### dipping tank
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep dip for washing sheep prior to shearing.

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> The necessary features of a station are a dwelling house for the manager, as store for the supply of rations, clothing and horse ands bullock gear, a woolshed with dipping tank etc and pens for the sheep in waiting ... <i>(Nov 4, 1859,Letter</i>)

### dipping yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a yard in proximity to a sheep dip [often a draining yard.]

>  The sheep should be paddocked conveniently to the dipping yards. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1481</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> And in about the dipping yard and round the shearing shed ... <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,12</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> It [a forcing pen] differs from a crush pen in as much one of these is needed only where physical force may be required, eg in the dipping yards. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)

### dipping-pole
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a crutch with an S-hook used to push sheep under dipping fluid in a dip-bath. (See [crutch](con-cyp.md#crutch),[dipping crutch](dig-dou.md#dipping-crutch))

>  <b>PORTER</b> .. the yell of a musterer whose dipping pole slipped off a sheep's head and he fell into the dip. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,33</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> Long dipping poles each topped with a horizontal S-hook were used. Mr Know called these poles crummachs. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,66</i>)

### dippy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a student studying for Diploma in Agriculture at Massey University. [also at Lincoln University (TD)]

>  Dippies, aggies, Redbands and Swanndris - forever tied to Massey University ... <i>(Mar 23, 2002,Midweek,24</i>)

### dippy sheep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He had been contacted by North Islandshearers who have suffered blurredvision, dizziness and nausea afterhandling sheep that had been recentlydipped [with organophosphates]. The riskyanimals were known in the trade as "dippysheep"... A Rangiora sheep dipcontractor.. said he had heard reports ofshearers being affected by dippy sheep inhot wool sheds. <i>(Jun 4, 1995,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

### direct report
 
 <i>n.</i> someone who reports directly to a superior

>  After becoming aware of the relationship, organisational development manager Joe Guthrie wrote to [him] saying the company would not condone a personal relationship between a manager and a "direct report"... The authority held that [he] had been unjustifiably dismissed as "no fair and reasonable employer could have concluded that he had committed serious misconduct simply for failing to acknowledge that he had exercised poor judgment in entering into a relationship with a direct report, particularly after that direct report had resigned." <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Dominion,7</i>)

### direct-drill
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to oversow pasture or crop with seed.

>  Establishment data from the experiment on direct-drilling Ruanui or Nui into existing swards indicate that Ruanui was not as suited to this method of introduction as the more vigorous Nui. <i>(Mar 1979,NZ Journal of Agriculture,57</i>)

>  He has a variety of special purpose pastures. We saw one of his latest which had been direct drilled with Nui 28 kg, Manawa 6 kg... <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)

>  After the cereals we direct-drill greenfeed into the stubble. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  When we direct-drilled one of our poorest paddocks with that mix, it went from 26 to 27 days to 21 to 22 days between grazings. <i>(Oct 1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,48</i>)

### direct-drilling
 
 <i>n.</i> [verb as well as noun in the citation]

>  By September and October we'll be direct-drilling summer crops, as well as working ground from peas and sweet corn."... Many Hawke's Bay farmers have taken on the concept of direct-drilling in the past few years... <i>(Aug 26, 2004,Dominion Post,Farming Supplement p. 9</i>)

### dirt-scratcher
 
 <i>n.</i> the driver of large earth-moving machinery

>  For their part, underground miners speak condescendingly of the "dirt-scratchers" who drive the big diggers and trucks of an opencast operation. <i>(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/44</i>)

### dirt-tracker
 
 <i>n.</i> a rugby football name for those members of a touring team who play midweek rather than the choice weekend games

>  One: "In the last week, before the third test, the management said they didn't want to see us dirt-trackers around the hotel, just to keep out of the way." <i>(Jun 19, 2005,Dominion Post,7,Lions on Tour Supplement ed.</i>)

>  He stood by me all the way, and when he came back from the meeting he ordered the dirt-trackers [the others were all playing the next day] all to be rounded up. <i>(Jul 19, 2001,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <b>ROMANOS</b> I might be pencilled in as one of the dirt-trackers, but you never know what can happen during World Cup month. <i>(1997,Black,133</i>)

### dirtsurfer
 
 <i>n.</i> scooter with BMX wheels

>  Tagged the skateboard on steroids, the dirtsurfer - built for careering down mountain sides or ripping up a mellow grassy slope - has the same fluid turning motion that has hooked every surfer, snowboarder and kiteboarder... Dirt surfers have an "aircraft aluminium" frame, a flexible deck for suspension, two knobby BMZX tyres and calf-operated brakes. <i>(Aug 8, 2002,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

### dirtsurfing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) sport using a type of scooter with BMX tyres

>  Dirtsurfing is becoming the new craze in Australia, Switzerland and America. <i>(Aug 8, 2002,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

### dirty
 
 <i>n.</i> a dirt-tracker, one who mainly plays in secondary games on tour

>  We were initially hoping to take 26 All Blacks and a smaller NZ A squad and play some of the All Black 'dirties' with NZ A. <i>(Sep 27, 2000,Evening Post,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that requires dagging prior to shearing.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> After an hour's riding a block is mustered off and 2000 sheep driven to nearby drafting yards. There the 'dirties' - the sheep in need of dagging - and the rams are drafted out. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,141</i>)

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> Head shepherd Tom Fleming drafting in the Bottom Six yards. He separates out the rams, "dirties" and strays from the ewes. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,151</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farm working conditions in exposed areas] exceedingly wet and windy.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of the dirtiest days I've had on the hill was on that block. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,18</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. I had only just hit the high ridge when a dirty driving sou'west storm hit us. .. the storm was one of those quick dirty busters and it was over in about two hours. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,90</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> But Jim Clews had a theory that we did more harm than good in riding round the ewes in dirty weather. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,112</i>)

### dirty burn
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an incomplete or black burn where there is no remnant white ash.

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> This [scrubcutting] was so that all the undergrowth and vines would dry and help carry the fire when the bush was burned. Otherwise there would not be a satisfactory burn - commonly known as a 'dirty burn.' <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,164</i>)

### dirty dairying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the flowing of dairy shed effluent into waterways.

>  Fish and Game New Zealand backed up its "dirty dairying" campaign rhetoric yesterday with scientific research by soil and water experts that claims a firm link between intensive farming and waterway pollution... The institute's report says waterways are in poor condition mainly because of "dirty dairying" and are likely to get worse. <i>Ibid.</i> 12 Mr Polson claims (June 19) that Federated Farmers is doing something about "dirty dairying" and he lists a series of schemes. <i>(Jun 26, 2002,Dominion,9</i>)

>  Something has to be done about dirty dairying and our polluted streams and lakes, and the lack of biodiversity in a country with so much grass, the terrible impact of introduced pests on our forests. <i>(Feb 20, 2011,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,3</i>) [NOTES: Paul Callaghan]

>  Fonterra has promised the Government and regional councils to wipe out dirty dairying. In an accord signed at Parliament yesterday, the industry giant spelled out how it would make farmers fence streams, bridge or culvert water crossings and stop fertiliser runoff into waterways. <i>(May 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  Dirty dairying debate will not go away. Mr Lodge's comments at the annual meeting of Federated Farmers' dairy section in Wellington came in the wake of a year's debate about so-called 'dirty dairying' degrading water quality in waterways and in ground water. <i>(Jun 18, 2002,Dominion,23</i>)

>  Waikite Valley farm company fined for dirty dairying: A North Island dairy farm has been fined $11,000 after cowshed effluent was discharged on to land. <i>(Jun 25, 2002,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  Agriculture minister Jim Sutton has apparently shrugged off the 'dirty dairying' campaign and is continuing to back the dairy industry to regulate its own environmental issues. <i>(Sep 12, 2002,Wanganui Chronicle,16</i>)

>  Fish and Game, a non-profit organisation representing the country's anglers and hunters was to assume the unpopular role of whistle-blower on dirty dairying. <i>(Oct 2002,North & South,10</i>)

>  Mr Johnson, leader of Fish and Game's "dirty dairying" campaign, says instead of attacking the Government, Federated Farmers ought to be trying to earn public respect by doing something about farming's pollution of waterways. <i>(Nov 27, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  Mr Johnson is the first to declare that he doesn't consider all dairying as dirty, and the Fish and Game Council has suggested that the "dirty dairying" phrase should not be used... The article also quoted the chairman of Federated Farmers' dairy section who said there was not a problem, and that dirty dairying was a myth. <i>(Jun 19, 2002,Dominion,15</i>)

### dirty dirty
 
 <i>n.</i> a rugby player outside the selected players

>  "...that was the first time I was a Dirty Dirty for a team, but I think I'm a better person for it... It's made me think back to the guys who were the Dirty Dirties in those teams and the jobs they did." Being Dirty Dirty is a thankless job. During the week the unwanted players are used as cannon fodder for the team, running in defence at training and trying to simulate a game atmosphere without (of course) injuring the guys playing in their place. Closer to the match they have to disappear, to give the playing 22 a bit of space, then on game day they are used for everything from ferrying bags to picking up the dirty playing gear after the game. <i>(Dec 3, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,8</i>)

>  Simon's been an unsung hero. He's had to carry a lot of the burden of a dirty-dirty [someone outside the playing 22]. <i>(Jul 16, 2004,Dominion Post,C12</i>)

>  He has to carry a lot of the burden as a dirty-dirty. He has done a lot of work for us in simulating opposition lineouts and he has run the reserves really well against the pack. <i>(Jul 15, 2004,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://xtramsn.co.nz/sport/]

>  [Caption] Afarto So'oalo, Con Barrell, Steve Lancaster and Andrew Mehrtens [were in] the 'Dirty Dirties' and watched the game from the stand during the Super 12 game played between the Crusaders vs the Waratahs at Jade Stadium, Christchurch, New Zealand, on the 5th May 2000. <i>(May 5, 2000,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: www.sportphotos.co.nz/display.aspx?image]

>  After breakfast the Dirty Dirties (squad members not playing on the weekend) met at 9.40am and we went and played badminton. <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: www.allblacks.com/index.cfm Jerome Kaino]

>  It's an interesting mix with uncapped players John Afoa, Jason Eaton and Isaia Toeava among the players who were effectively the "dirty dirties" last week. <i>(Nov 7, 2005,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: http://xtramsn.co.nz/news (Duncan Johnstone)]

>  Fearing a trap, Toeava said he didn't want to answer such questions before Oliver came to his rescue by saying they had enjoyed themselves as the non-playing dirty dirties in London. <i>(Nov 25, 2005,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,7</i>)

>  Greg Somerville - 6.32. Another player who spent most of his time among the dirty dirties. Did start in two games but failed to do anything startling. <i>(Aug 24, 2005,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: www.tvnz.co.nz/sport/ (David Long)]

>  [Caption] Highlanders'dirty/dirties' during the Super 12 semi-final played between the Crusaders and the Highlanders at Jade Stadium, Christchurch on the 18th of May, 2002 <i>(May 18, 2002,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: www.sportphotos.co.nz/display.aspx?image (nb this may be a recent caption on an old photo)]

>  First caps going to the Hurricanes pairing of Neemia Tialata and Chris Masoe, and domestic season dirty dirties Ryan and Smith strting, along with injury comeback boys Kelleher and Oliver. <i>(Nov 3, 2005,Internet - see Notes,0</i>) [NOTES: www.rugbyweb.co.nz]

>  For a group of dirty dirties, the tour is clearly over. <i>(Nov 22, 2005,TV1,News</i>)
