 <i>n.</i> <i>Dryopteris velutena</i> [[get cit: JD says ??]]

>  <b>FIELD</b> (And) <i>(1890,Ferns of NZ</i>)

### dirty paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm area carrying rough feed or weed.

>  Ewes can be used to clean up any dirty paddocks. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,219</i>)

### disc
 
 <i>v.</i> [also spelt disk]

>  I have ploughed 1,250 acres, hustled 1,000 and disced, drilled, harrowed, etc., a fair amount. <i>(May 28, 1928,NZ Free Lance,45</i>)

>  Should the land have been ploughed and worked down or disked and harrowed, all cultivation possible should be given while the fern is growing. <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Agriculture,234</i>)

>  About 50 acres of land has been turned over.. and now it has to be disced. <i>(Oct 1, 1937,Morrinsville Star,4</i>)

### disc harrow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> Up to the late 70's tine harrows were popular for preparing land for sowing, but during the 1870's the first Disc Harrows were imported from America. <i>(1956,History of Farm Implements,12</i>)

>  [Caption] At work with the disc harrows on an East Coast farm. <i>(Oct 26, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,46</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> One of their [sc. M.J. Hooper & Co's] most notable achievements was the production of Hooper Giant Disc Harrows. <i>(1984,History of Farm Implements,Appendix 21</i>)

### disc-harrow
 
 <i>v.</i> [also as verb to disc q.v.]

>  [Caption] Disc-harrowing with a bullock team. <i>(Jan 26, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,39</i>)

>  He was disc-harrowing in a paddock on the Saturday. <i>(Oct 20, 1917,NZ Truth,6</i>)

### discs
 
 <i>n.</i> [also spelt disks; see also disc harrow noun]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> "Never heard of anyone using six horses in the discs," he cried. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,38</i>) [NOTES: 1970 edition]

>  The soil should be stirred occasionally during the summer with disks and harrows until late February or early March. <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Agriculture,57/52</i>)

>  Where rolling has been left rather later, a cut with the discs will be helpful in preparing the land for subsequent rolling. <i>(1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60/415</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> A set of discs is a real bonus when it comes to dealing with the paspallum [sic]. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,8</i>)

### disengage
 
 <i>v.</i> (of police) take early retirement

>  Officers are leaving - 75 per cent of them "disengaging" (Police National Headquarters preferred term for taking their Police Employment Rehabilitation Fund, or PERF) - faster than they're coming in. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,35</i>)

### disking
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. disc verb]

>  In the early spring disk harrows should be used to disintegrate the furrow before reploughing, and further disking to break up the soil before summer dryness makes the work difficult. <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Agriculture,57/52</i>)

### Disneyland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Disneyland: Where a player goes when he takes "a heavy knock"; that is, his head has been stamped on. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Evening Post,7</i>)

### Disneyland Dad
 
 <i>n.</i> a father with access to his children at weekends and holidays

>  Some fathers who lost full custody became "Disneyland dads", taking children out for a good time during visits and neglecting to keep their behaviour in check. <i>(Dec 5, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

### Disputes Tribunal
 
 <i>n.</i> a judicial tribunal designed to determine small civil claims cheaply and informally. [Jim Cameron writes: Known as a <i>small claims tribunal</i> 1976-1988. Presided over by <i>referees</i>. Disputes Tribunals Act 1988.]

>  Some obsolete terms (e.g. entail, foreclosure, probation) are not noted as historical only. Even the Dictionary of New Zealand English (DNZE) (1997) is patchy in its coverage of legal terms, having for example no entry for disputes tribunal, family protection, incorporated society, Maori option, protection order, resource consent. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2(1) : 1</i>)

### dissolution kit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Do-it-yourself marriage dissolution (divorce) could become common practice if a recently released dissolution kit takes off. <i>(Feb 23, 1983,Evening Post,10</i>)

### distributor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> Ridgers could also be a separate implement, as were early distributors forfertilizer. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,67</i>)

### District Board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A copy of the petition and its annexes must be sent to the District Board [sc. of the Hospital] at least fourteen days before it is presented to the Governor. <i>(Oct 8, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### District Council
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The forced amalgamation of six small authorities into the Far North District Council in 1989 forced us for the first time in our history to co-ordinate our varying interests. <i>(May 1, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The broad scheme of the Bill is to set up district councils which will replace existing borough and county councils and may take over the functions of many ad hoc bodies such as catchment boards..pest destruction councils, and so on. <i>(1974,NZ Parliamentary Debates,394/4555</i>)

### District Court
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Magistrates courts were renamed district courts and their presiding judges district court judges in 1981, but the term magistrate in New Zealand denoted stipendiary magistrates rather than justices of the peace, who were and are usually referred to simply as justices or JPs. (District court has itself a history. Originally district courts were intermediate courts created in 1858; they were squeezed out between the Supreme Court (now the High Court) and the magistrates courts and were formally abolished in 1925.) The latest episode of this serial is the creation in 1998 of community magistrates to sit in district courts and exercise minor criminal jurisdiction. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### district high school
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) originally a rural primary school offering some secondary instruction; later a rural secondary school.

>  <b>BROOKING</b> Tate suggested that the solution to New Zealand's agricultural education problem lay with the District High Schools. <i>(1977,Massey - Its Early Years,19</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> Dylan Ormond had been at the district high, but only because even his mother and father knew that the expense of a city education would have been wasted on him. <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,240</i>)

### District Judge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Shortly after his appointment as a District Judge, his Honor went on circuit to Hamilton. <i>(Oct 16, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### district licensing trust
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes : Since 1989 this term has become largely though not wholly historical.]

### district nurse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> "I'm from north Hokianga... I was a district nurse there." <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,66</i>)

### district plan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Other distinctive NZE terms include district plan, regional plan, heritage order, access strip, and esplanade strip. There are dictionary entries for scenic reserve but not, for example, historic place, maritime park, or soil conservation reserve. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

>  The Mayor.. stressed that the audit's recommendations had not yet been included in the Council's District Plan. "They have been left out deliberately so that the Council can consult with all interested parties prior to the District Plan being finalised in approximately two years time"... <i>(May 1996,NZ Historic Places,58/5</i>)

### ditch and bank
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to form a fenceline or barrier by digging an embankment over which stock can not pass.

>  Did some ditch and banking in the morning ... <i>(Mar 29, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,30</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  In Tuesday's issue of the STAR I noticed a letter signed 'H.B.' in reference to the Maoris ditching and banking on a Sunday. <i>(Aug 21, 1885,Hawera Star,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a formation of a fenceline, embankment or barrier with the digging of a ditch.

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Fencing, ditch and bank with a hurdle fence on top. Cost 1/6 per perch. <i>(Dec 12, 1844,Letter,88</i>) [NOTES: in Kalaugher (1950) Gleanings]

>  We have now all but finished one paddock of about 400 acres, with a ditch and bank. <i>(1848,BLANK ENTRY,51</i>) [NOTES: in DEANS (1964) Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains]

>  <b>TAIT</b> Two ditch and bank fences and an old plough share were the only evidence of any previous cultivation. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,122</i>)

### ditch light
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  All locomotives have lights on top of their cabs, but Tranz Rail is adding "ditch lights" lower down that will flash when horns are sounded, making trains more visible to motorists. <i>(Mar 20, 2001,Dominion,1</i>)

### ditch, the
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the Tasman Sea (across the ditch: to or in Australia) [attributive use of across-the-ditch in first citation below]

>  After all, the New Zealand economy is not in bad shape compared with the United States or our across-the-ditch cousie, Australia. <i>(May 5, 2001,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Vine was across the ditch helping his son buy a house. <i>(Jan 24, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  In business and pleasure, the rough Tasman passage - more than ever before - is just "the ditch", no longer even a psychological barrier to movement. <i>(Feb 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Clark's agreement with Howard widens the Ditch. <i>(Mar 5, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Like many New Zealand companies it crossed the ditch because it couldn't grow much more here. <i>(May 5, 2001,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  So thank God for the bonds of affection that will endure between the hundreds of thousands of us whose friends and families straddle this ocean we used to call the ditch. <i>(Mar 29, 2003,Dominion Post,A14</i>)

>  Later in the year there'll be a major cartoon exhibition - with the provisional title, 'The other side of the ditch'. <i>(Apr 11, 2001,Capital Times,10</i>)

>  "Even Australians have to concede and go 'if we're going to make good film, then we've got to go across the ditch. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  [He] is the latest of a series of Australian executives who have jumped the ditch and landed at the helm of a New Zealand electricity company. <i>(Jun 25, 2003,Dominion,C4</i>)

>  My solution to unrequited love is to pick the eyes out of the cultural calendar, find cheap flights and look for a few weekends a year across the ditch. <i>(Jul 10, 2001,Dominion,15</i>)

>  The bulletin is broadcast, disconcertingly, from across the ditch. <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),79</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Cook Strait [cook straight sic in citation?]

>  [heading] <b>Across the ditch</b> (cook straight, that is) <i>(May 20, 2005,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,8</i>)

### ditch-digger
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a piece of heavy machinery used for ditching.

>  <b>KERR</b> A ditch-digger and a D8 bulldozer gnawed away at it. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,152</i>)

### Div
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) (as the Div) = Divvy

>  <b>O'Sullivan</b> But my father arranged it [the funeral] and rang around their cobbers from the Div... <i>(2006,Pictures by Goya,150</i>)

### div
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  None of the Bullstown "pubs" are paying heavy "divs." <i>(Feb 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,35</i>)

### diversion
 
 <i>n.</i> n. in criminal procedure, a system for giving a chance for a first- time criminal defendant in lesser crimes to perform community service, make restitution for damage due to the crime, obtain treatment for alcohol or drug problems and/or counselling for antisocial or mentally unstable conduct. If the defendant cooperates and the diversion results in progress, the charges eventually may be dismissed. Usually diversion may not be granted for a second offense. [definition from Californian based law.com dictionary, entered 8/10/2001 [http://dictionary.law.com/]]

>  Diversion is a system by which first-time offenders can be charged with a minor offence and escape without a conviction. <i>(Mar 15, 2004,Salient,No. 3/33</i>)

>  However Wellington Police Community Services Coordinator Detective Tony Moore disagrees, He sees youth initiatives like family group conferences and diversion as worthwhile. <i>(Nov 1, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),3</i>)

### Divide
 
 <i>n.</i> the Southern Alps [also Great Divide]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> &#65279;"Richard the Lion-hearted uncrowned kingof New Zealand, had cross the GreatDivide." "Last seen heading for theCass," said Jimmy Jude, and there was alaugh. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,18</i>)

>  The vista of the Divide peaks aheadprovides an incentive which can neverfail to spur him on. <i>(1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,III/64</i>)

>  At dusk I observed many of the dreaded"hogsbacks" rolling across the Divide. <i>(1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,III/64</i>)

### divider
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog that divides sheep in a carefully gathered mob [in a fit of impatience or over-enthusiasm.]

>  <b>STAPYLTON-SMITH</b> Mostly these dogs are of inestimable value, but at times, in an excess of zeal, they may become 'splitters,' or 'slewers,' or 'dividers.' <i>(1990,Other Side of Farming, The,201</i>)

### dividing range
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Resolved, That the Department be informed that the proposed road is inpracticable [sic], and recommending that a new road be surveyed on the diving range between the two roads. <i>(Dec 11, 1890,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Our guide shewed us another small valley three miles nearer the Lake than the one through which we had travelled, where he said there was a path leading to the valley of the Pakuratahi; with a little time devoted to asertain where a line may be carried, so as to avoid the highest points of the dividing range of hills, I have no doubt a road may be made so as readily to unite Wairarapa with the valley of the Hutt, at a cost perfectly insignificant, if we regard the extent of level and fertile land which it would be the means of rendering available, or the advantages it would confer on the first and principal settlement. <i>(Dec 22, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  The eastern boundary proceeds from the Mokutapu, thence along the summit of the dividing range of the Maitai and Pelorus for a distance of eight miles, from thence the southern boundary commences, and proceeds in a straight line to the southern angle of section No. 8 Suburban South, thence along the northern boundary of sections Nos. 19, 99, 76, 75, and 77, Waimea East district, to high-water mark. <i>(Nov 8, 1851,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,148</i>)

>  ".. Eighteen years afterwards, far on the eastern side of the big dividing range, and near the Hutt River, some bushfellers found the bones of the hapless lad." <i>(Aug 5, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

### diving petrel
 
 <i>n.</i> titi

>  .. there is a very pretty group including crested grebe, dabchick, grey duck, black teal, and flightless duck ; another taking in the mutton bird, whale bird, stormy petrel, night bird, blue billy, driving [sic] petrel, shearwater, and black petrel ... <i>(Sep 14, 1888,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  New exhibits at the Colonial Museum include .. a diving petrel from the Antipodes Island. <i>(Aug 29, 1900,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  He appends the following list of birds to be found on the Little Barrier in support of his statement .. diving-petrel ... <i>(Nov 16, 1892,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

>  DIVING-PETREL (<i>Pelecanoides urinatrix</i> Gmelin). <i>(1928,Chatham Islands,9/285</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PELECANOIDES URINATRIX. (DIVING-PETREL.) <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,313,1888 2nd edn 2/207 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 124. HALADROMA URINATRIX. Gml. Diving Petrel. Titi. <i>Pelecanoides urinatrix.</i> GRAY. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,45</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>Pelecanoides urinatrix</i> - Diving Petrel - Kuaka. Kuaka is also in the North Island a name given to the Southern Godwit - Limosa Novae Zealandiae. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,201</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Pelicanoides ... urinatrix</i> ... var. <i>chathamica</i> ... Diving Petrel. KUAKA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,87</i>)

>  Colin Miskelly hopes that one day he will be able to turn off the tape for good and listen to the real thing: the clamour of diving petrels, fairy prions, storm petrels and sooty shearwaters returning to feed their chicks... The first diving petrel chicks were transferred to artificial burrows on Mana, from North Brother Island in Cook Strait, in 1977. <i>(Jun 2002,Forest & Bird,304/8</i>)

>  Stereophonic seabird calls booming out across the ocean have played a major role in luring diving petrels back to Mana Island, where 14 pairs are now incubating eggs. <i>(Oct 2003,Footnotes,No. 20/5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Common Diving Petrel Pelecanoides urinatrix</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... <i>New Zealand Diving Petrel P.u. urinatrix</i> ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Kuaka Chatham Island diving petrel, Kerguelen diving petrel. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,106</i>)

### Division
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [ell.] an organisation of rural women [the Women's Division of the Farmers' Union (WDFU), later Women's Division of Federated Farmers (WDFF) most commonly referred to as Division, but from 1999 known as Rural Women.]

>  The work which is being undertaken by the Women's Division widens out and expands over all our country districts. The Women's Division is the only organisation in our country which purposes to help those who are sick and in need of help and to alleviate the hardships of country women and country children. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,Hawera Star,16</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> The Flock House boys and girls became the special care of members of the Division. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,11</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> Its Housekeeper Scheme .. was one of its early and most interesting attempts to lighten the load of the country woman; and the Division is apparently able in this way to keep some fifty or sixty women in continuous employment. <i>(1940,Women of NZ, The,183</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> This [social and political] extension of the orginal purpose of the Division has been by some members resented and opposed ... <i>(1940,Women of NZ, The,184</i>)

>  .. she was also a guest speaker at the Women's Division Federated Farmers (now Rural Women) conference. [Women's Division] <i>(Jul 8, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### do
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to thrive.

>  Lambs do not take to silage or hay and will not 'do' on these feeds. <i>(Jan 23, 1963,Weekly News,38</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> It looked wonderful and most of us gratefully turned the sheep out. The more observant, however, soon noticed that the sheep were not 'doing.' <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,52</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> "Put your rams out on the first of April,' he said. "The early lambs seem to be the ones that do." <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,48</i>)

>  .. the lambs were doing, so obviously the ewes were milking ... <i>(Jun 15, 1994,NZ Farmer,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stockmanship] to cause to thrive.

>  Ewes have been 'well done by' during the winter. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,174</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> Besides the usual pasture, roots and green feed given to the flock it is often profitable to 'do' the show sheep especially well. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,116</i>)

>  Flock rams noted for their good doing qualities ... 1950 J of Ag Oct 347 The successful management of a sheep run calls for .. an appreciation of the carrying capacity of tussock land to 'do' sheep well without either overstocking or understocking. <i>(Jan 1, 1929,NZ Farmer,56</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to muster a large area of hill or high country.

 <i>phr.</i>

### do a get
 
 <i>phr.</i> to leave in a hurry

>  Miss Taylor had been carrying on with a boarder, and he was told to do a polite get. <i>(Sep 21, 1907,NZ Truth,118/5/4</i>)

>  The defendant again got tired of the humdrum existence of domesticity, and once more `did a get', leaving his lady love lamenting. <i>(Jul 31, 1909,NZ Truth,214/7/1</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> I prepared to do a get <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,92</i>)

>  get, do a v. retreat, escape, abscond <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),9</i>)

>  get n. do a get NZ & Austral. sl. make a get-away, leave hastily. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,316</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]

### do a Kopu/do an Alamein Kopu
 
 <i>phr.</i>
