>  I don't actually believe the Derek Quigley for a minute is going to do an Alamein Kopu. <i>(Aug 18, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

### do a line with
 
 <i>phr.</i> (of a male) to court, make advances to (a female) [Selected Short Stories SARGESON 196 I was too far gone to care about almost anything except maybe whether I could do a line with Maggie.]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> He was doing a heavy line with the saddler's daughter <i>(1944,Troop Target,63</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I could do a line with Maggie. <i>(1946,That Summer,91</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> She was trying to do a line with George. <i>(1947,Big Game,88</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Lofty .... was `doing a line' with the governess <i>(1949,High Country Days,151</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> line: do a line with (a girl), court. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Silk stockings; and Jinner's strides are half-mast. Here and there a hint of lipstick. Pat's with a grouse new girl again. Must be doing a line with her. <i>(1965,Power of Joy,266</i>)

### do a skate
 
 <i>phr.</i> to hurry off; to decamp (cf. scale v.) [ca1938 (among schoolchildren)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>skate </i> phr. do a skate <i> to disappear hurriedly; aka</i> skate off; <i>eg `Potts figured there was trouble brewing and did a skate.' c. 1925.</i> - phr. <i>go for a skate</i> a fall, mistake, failure, blunder or dismissal; eg `If he doesn't shape up soon he'll go for a skate.' -<i> skate over/around</i> to avoid, move over or blur something disconcerting; eg `She's always skating around the question, never really answering directly.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,103</i>)

### do a Taranaki
 
 <i>phr.</i> to boast

>  <b>J WINCHESTER</b> I heard on the breakfast session of 2ZB a few days ago a song composed by Ken Avery which went something like: `I won't be doing a Taranaki/ If I tell you about Wairakei'. <i>(Oct 1963,Personal Communication</i>)

### do hard
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [stock management] to restrict the feed intake of stock.

>  Once the cows have been tupped they are all the better for being done fairly hard until 3 or 4 weeks before lambing. <i>(Apr 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,348</i>)

### do in
 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> lose, get rid of

>  John Thomas said he had sacked him for intemperance, which time John [Sullivan] had got another job and `done it in' for the same reason. <i>(Jan 29, 1910,NZ Truth,240/7/3</i>)

>  The watchhouse-keeper asked where the ring was, and accused said he had `done it in,' meaning that he had got rid of it. <i>(Dec 17, 1910,NZ Truth,286/6/2</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> [Compare do over. 192? K. Mansfield ? Ole Underwood [CHECK]]

### do it hard
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be angry, worried, upset (over sthg); to suffer (cf. hard doer); to find difficult or hard to bear [ca1920 He did it hard when his wife was dying.]

### do one's block
 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry, excited, upset (See [cf. do one's bun/loaf/scone; lose one's block](../D#do-one's-bun/loaf/scone;-lose-one's-block))

>  Hall smelt him, and could detect no odor [sic] of swankey, and concluded that the silent one had `done his block,' to use an ungrammatical vulgarism, and was ripe for the asylum. <i>(Apr 9, 1910,NZ Truth,250/6/1</i>)

>  A Christchurch bookmaker named Archibald Anderson `did his block' with the aid of hops on March 31, when he went temporarily insane in the Star and Garter Hotel, with a long beer glass clenched murderously in his right hand. <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/7/6</i>)

>  ... the clever chap who wrote the epistle would have no hesitation in speaking to this effect: `Dicken! I done me block right enough! I come home and the old finger hit the roof. Tike it from me, he's gotter rat.' <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,15</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Don't do your block, but do your best/ To learn your way about. <i>(1934,Journalese,209</i>)

>  <b>F.G. YOUNG</b> Savage started off and completely did his block; made a vicious attack upon those who supported the resolution and talked about challenging them to a debate ... a challenge ... which we will gladly accept. <i>(Feb 11, 1935,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Feb 11 F.G.Young to J.Roberts (Roberts Papers 1/12 in 1986 Gustafson Cradle to Grave 161)]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fritz landed a daisy-cutter and the transport driver done his block and took his hook. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The sergeant sounded really irritable. `OK, Sarge, don't do your block.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,311</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... a leader of men. He was tough and reliable and never did his block. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,114</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> And he never done his block about anything that went wrong. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,181</i>)

>  <i>block, to do one's (Coll. phr.)</i> lose one's temper. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> But, like most packmen, it took little to annoy him. He was credited with throwing fifty pounds of hard-earned money from the Waiau Ferry Bridge into the flooded river to see if it would float, and then did his block when he never saw the money again. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,37</i>)

>  <i>to do your block</i>: to lose your temper <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>CHARLES</b> [DO ONE'S BLOCK, lose one's head] He's done his block and lost his sense,/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,93</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> Croft tried ... to be hail fellow with everybody, but could sometimes ... do his block. <i>(1981,Hot Water Sailor,224</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To be overcome by fear or anger, and act irrationally on the impulse. .;`When he saw the Cop's car after him the silly goat did his block and tried speeding. It only made things worse for him; he never had a hope of getting away.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],265</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63) 1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 265]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Only a bloody rat ... Nothing to do your block over. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,90</i>) [NOTES: (Greaser's Story)]

>  <b>MANTELL</b> He wanted me to go up country to set another fire. I went ... [sic] and mucked it up. Walker did his block. <i>(1983,Murder to Burn,166</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> He really felt like doing his block and telling them just what he was thinking. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,39,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Don't do your block, Bill. <i>(1944,Troop Target,15</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> In the dark, my [disturbed] mind cries out. ... `Break it down,' reproves Pilling. `Don't do your block.' <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,49</i>) [NOTES: [Do One's block, ?go mad, lose self-control]]

### do one's bun
 
 <i>phr.</i> to lose one's temper (See [cf. do one's block etc](../D#do-one's-block-etc))

>  The most important of Army slang expressions, however, has been `doing the scone' with its variant `doing the bun', used for losing one's temper. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),II No XIX 24</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> `O.K.! O.K.! don't do your bun,' he answered. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,128</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Jock did his bun properly. `So my money's not good enough, eh mate?' he snarled at the driver. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,76</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> ... is to BLOW YOUR FUSE. This, is a temper tantrum but is less general and more directional than to throw a wobbly. One does one's bun at someone. These terms are more often used for adults while children are more likely to throw a wobbly. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,32</i>)

>  (Goldie Brown Collection) <i>to do your bun</i>: to lose your temper <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bun </i>phr.<i> do your bun</i> lose your temper; eg `Hey. no need to do your bun, Milt. You'll get your money.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,23</i>)

### do one's bundle
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> She was not the sort that anyone was likely to do his bundle over. <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,97</i>)

### do one's dash
 
 <i>phr.</i> to exhaust one's energies or capabilities; to do all one can, or is willing to do. [ca1920 W.H.B. Orsman]

>  <b>TURNER</b> It is tempting to wonder whether the expression `he's done his dash' meaning `he is played out', `he has done all he can' is connected with the gold-miner's dashing. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,152</i>)

>  do one's dash reach limit of one's abilities <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,121</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

### do one's hauhau
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> Everything's Maori up there. Most of them are Christians now, but on a Sunday you can still see some of the old chaps doing their hau-hau, getting down and barking like dogs. ... Thatmeeting-house is worth seeing. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,189</i>)

### do one's lolly
 
 <i>phr.</i> do one's head/block, become angry [1955 BJ Cameron Collection (TS July) do one's block, do one's bun, do one's lolly (v). To get excited, lose one's head.]

>  <b>GLEN</b> Biddy did not half do her lolly. The effort cost me a shirt, a torn pair of good working trousers . <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,33</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> lolly any sweets, short for lollipop. NZA. - chuck or lose your lollies to spew. - do one's lolly lose one's temper. NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,70</i>)

### do one's melon
 
 <i>phr.</i> = do one's block

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> the same thing [as do one's block] <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],266</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)]

### do one's nana
 
 <i>n.</i> become angry or excited, do one's block [?abbreviation of banana]

>  Sometimes, on dull days, I listen to that tape. It's a riot, of course - their crescendo-ing excitement interspersed with compressed homilies to each other, and Keith Quinn doing his nana in the background. <i>(Sep 5, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),29</i>)

>  His father did his 'nana' completely. <i>(Feb 19, 1995,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

### do one's scone
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>COOZE</b> The camp at Pahautanui was much as all military camps. Tedsons training, fatigues and `scone doing' from 6 am to 4 pm. <i>(1944,Kiwis in the Pacific,8</i>)

>  `Their [German] planes ....' `Don't do your scone, boy.' `Some bludger's got my tin hat.' <i>((21/0/1942),2NZEF Times,12</i>)

>  `Their [German] planes ....' `Don't do your scone, boy.' `Some bludger's got my tin hat.' <i>(Dec 21, 1942,2NZEF Times,12</i>)

>  `Don't do your plurry scone, Dig!' `Who's sconing ?' <i>(Oct 19, 1942,2NZEF Times,5</i>)

>  <b>SARGE</b> Don't do your scone. .... Don't do your block <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,134</i>)

>  ... another person's anger is usually falsified in some whimsical phrase, perhaps borrowed from another community - `took a dim view', `did his scone', `molte dispiac', `went off the beam', `off the deep end'. <i>(Sep 23, 1952,Landfall,23/210</i>) [NOTES: Pearson `Fretful Sleepers']

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I must say something or I'll do my scone properly. <i>(1958,Gun in My Hand, A,98</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> to become angry, lose one's cool [cf. do one's block etc.]

>  Don't do your scone, boy. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 17</i>)

>  [Caption] I'm NOT doing my scone. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZEF Times,II/90 : 12</i>)

>  He reckons the Doc. did his scone properly. <i>(Jul 19, 1943,NZEF Times,III/108 : 5</i>)

>  "Don't do your scone," Alex admonished. <i>(Jan 10, 1944,NZEF Times,III/133 : 10</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> Immediately pandemonium broke loose and scones were done in all directions. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,49</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Spandau Craig is really doing his scone about dereliction of duty ... <i>(1970,On the Ball,135</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `She's jake, boys, don't panic,' Ted said. `Don't do your scones. ...' <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,97</i>)

>  <b>HOWATT</b> Finally I could stand it no longer and I did my scone. I abused Peter Johnstone and told him what I thought of his tactics. <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,299</i>)

>  <i>to do your scone</i>: to lose control <i>to scone (someone)</i>: to hit them on the head 'ho;1981 Feb Avondale College (Auckland) Slang Words in Use (Goldie Brown Collection) <i>do your scone </i>- <i>do one's scone</i> lose one's temper; <i>- use your scone</i> advice to be sensible; <i>- off one's scone</i> mad. <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  Well the teacher did his scone and Patu did his porangi act. <i>(Nov 12, 1983,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <i>scone ... do one's scone NZ sl. </i>lose one's temper. [orig. uncert.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,682</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Before you commence to do the Editorialscone, let us hasten to assure you thatno offence report is intended. <i>(May 21, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/204 : 5</i>)

>  "Don't do your plurry scone, Dig!...Who's sconing? <i>(Oct 19, 1942,NZEF Times,II/69 : 5</i>)

### do over
 
 <i>v.</i> to treat (usu. a person) harshly, roughly or dishonestly; to thrash; to scold severely. a. ?????

>  <b>BURGESS</b> ... and since we are going to do these people over (his very words) I think we had better prevent him from doing us any harm hereafter. <i>(1866,Confessions,118,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The terror of Featherston camp he was. I tried to trim him down but he did me over proper. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,44</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> You're bloody lucky I don't do you over just for being such a nark. <i>(1960,Word for Word,255</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To beat up, perhaps savagely. .in 4;`His pals knew he gave information to the police, so theywaited for him and done him over. The cops found him lying on the pavement.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],269</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 269]

>  The Murphy's pigs are gonna do me [sc the Dog] over because they think I'mresponsible for the pig carcass in the river mud - but I'm not innocent.! <i>(Apr 6, 1984,Evening Post,20</i>) [NOTES: (Footrot Flats)]

>  <b>Beaton</b> KATE: They salted her tea at breakfast. ... They do it to all the new chicks. SANDY: Yeah! Christ! They did me over when I first come [sic]. <i>(1984,Outside In,24</i>)

### do the block
 
 <i>phr.</i> to promenade or parade the streets [1869. Marcus Clarke, `Peripatetic Philosopher,' (in an Essay on `Doing the Block') (reprint), p. 13:]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Block, doing the</i> v. lounging in the fashionable promenade. In Melbourne, it is Collins Street, between Elizabeth and Swanston Streets. In Sydney, `The Block' is that portion of the city bounded by King, George, Hunter, and Pitt Streets. It is now really two blocks, but was all in one till the Government purchased the land for the present Post Office, and then opened a new street from George to Pitt Street. Since then the Government, having purchased more land, has made the street much wider, and it is now called Martin's Place. <i>Ibid.</i> 37 <i>Block, on the (1) On the promenade above referred to. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,36</i>)

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> The gilded loafer `does the block' and elevates his snout/ To see the rough-dressed fossicker, white haired and broken down/ Who strays from out the lonely wilds for his Christmas booze in town. <i>(1894,Voices of Wave and Tree,15</i>)

>  `We may slacken pace a little now and again, just as the busy man, who generally walks quickly, has to go slowly in the crowd on the Block.' <i>(Jul 17, 1896,Argus, The,4/7</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... to do the block ... : these are phrases which come almost as easily to the New Zealand tongue as to the Australian. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Once or twice we [sc a boys' impromptu band] `did the block,' but I am afraid our collection was even more inadequate to our supposed needs than the Army's was to theirs. A Guy Fawkes appeal was much better supported. The Army was considered either irreverent or a pest. (1880-90; New Plymouth) <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,67</i>) [NOTES: [c1880-1890 New Plymouth]]

>  <b>WALKER</b> [The engaged couple] were `doing the block' - Hereford St. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,176</i>)

>  The writer of this was almost gathered to his fathers the other night when he met a fat Chow `doing the block' in the dour city. <i>(Dec 18, 1909,NZ Truth,234/5/4</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> You do the block in swagger style, in <i>such</i> a clever gown. <i>(1891,In Double Harness,54</i>)

### do the grand
 
 <i>phr.</i> make a great display

>  <b>CLYDE</b> I wish I had a run or two, / Like those of Dillon Bell, / Oh! I would do the `grand', / Like any other swell. <i>(1872,Te Kooti,39</i>)

### do the heavy
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make a fine show

>  Shall we do the heavy, and expend 3/- to visit the dress-circle, like swells as we are. <i>(1869,Comic Guide to Dunedin,39</i>)

### do the numbers
 
 <i>phr.</i> count one's supporters

>  National MP Gerry Brownlee denies doing the numbers for a leadership coup amid speculation over the future of party leader Bill English... Amid speculation he was positioning himself for a leadership bid, Mr Brownlee said he was not doing the numbers... <i>(Aug 9, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### do the Pacific Slope
 
 <i>phr.</i> (of a wanted criminal) to flee the country

>  <b>WALKER</b> Perhaps he [sc a missing man] accomplished the `Pacific Slope'. <i>Ibid.</i> 326 He [sc.the detective] has packed so many `confidence men' off to penitentiary ... that the others have done the `Pacific Slope' in various directions, chiefly towards Australia. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,292</i>)

>  <b>GILKISON</b> In the 'seventies and 'eighties many fraudulent debtors, embezzlers and rich thieves escaped from New Zealand before arrest, by doing what came to be known as the `Pacific Slope'. Someone discovered that the Sandwich Islands, which were then ruled by a native queen, had no extradition treaty with New Zealand and consequently rogues who were much `wanted' by the police in this country on reaching Honolulu could laugh at writs and law proceedings. <i>(1938,Early Days of Canterbury,133</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> `Poor old Tom! He's never been the same since his partner did the Pacific slope.' <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,42</i>)

>  <b>GORE</b> He never returned. Tetley had done what in those days was termed `The Pacific Slope', and was last heard of in South America, that haven for absconders and those anxious to shelve their responsibilities. <i>(1956,Levins,186</i>)

>  The employes [sic] at a certain flaxmill in the district (says the "Manawatu Times") awoke one morning to find that their overseer had "done the Pacific slope" with nearly GBP200 of their wages in his pocket. <i>(Jun 4, 1902,Tuapeka Times,3</i>) [NOTES: Reprinted from the Manawatu Times]

>  The Wanganui Chronicle says: The latest idea in "sweeps" is what is known as the "Pacific Slope" stakes. All the names of the prominent business men are entered, and the drawer of the next person who leaves his Wanganui creditors and crosses the Pacific, scoops the pool. We hear that the stakes are large, so it may be worth the while of the holder of them to do the "slope" himself. <i>(Nov 19, 1889,North Otago Times,4</i>)

### do up
 
 <i>v.</i> to spend (all one's available money). [see also do/do in]

>  <b>BRUNO</b> Now Auld Jock and Bluey, having successfully partnered a crown-and-anchor board in their Naafi, and having `done up' the feloose A.W.L. in Cairo Y.M.C.A.'s, had arrived back decidedly`onkus.' <i>(1944,Desert Daze,55</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> Jack does up all our dough on booze and I need it. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,48</i>)

### do you know anything?
 
 <i>phr.</i> an (?obs.) catchphrase from the King Country: a euphemism in the area when it was dry, for `where can I get some liquor?'.

>  <b>WINTER</b> A very common occurrence in the old days was to be asked in anxious tones, `Do you knowanything?' which, being interpreted into another kind of slang meant, `Can you put me on to a spot?'. <i>(1939,King Country, The,23</i>)

### do-it-easies
 
 <i>n.</i> tranquillisers [(prisoners')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Do-it-easies. (n) Tranquillisers. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

### do-ray-me
 
 <i>n.</i> money

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> `What you doin' out so late, small fry? You got no do-ray-me?' `Do-ray-me?' `You know, gold. What it takes. Green stuff, money, boy, money!' <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,64</i>)

### do-up
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also doer-upper](dig-dou.md#doer-upper))

>  Many recent downtown do-ups are Krukziener's. <i>(Jan 18, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  The small blue house was hardly wider than a walkway. A do-up in a "hot" suburb on a section best described as snug... The house was what real estate agents optimistically describe as a do-up, with a government valuation of $200,000. <i>(Sep 20, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  It's disappointing there's no bath but new manager Catherine Bone is keen to get in spa pools as part of a do-up. <i>(Sep 19, 2010,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,4</i>)

### do/do in
 
 <i>v.</i> spend (completely) [also do one's chips (spend all one's money)] (See [also do up](dig-dou.md#do-up))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> I'd never make for home again until I'd `done it in.' <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,27</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Maori shearer of the early sixties ... unfortunately ... proved a ready convert to the custom of drinking waipiro and `doing-in' his cheque at the nearest grog shanty. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,54</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> An it ran like a hairy goat an I did me chips. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,23</i>)

>  do in ... NZ & Austral. sl. spend completely <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,220</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 330]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> do, do in to spend all one's money, often recklessly; eg `Promise you'll come straight home, harold. You know if you go to the TAB you'll do in your entire pay packet.' - to defeat; eg `With Kirwan playing they'll do your team.' Both NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,37</i>)

### doab
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass),[doab](dig-dou.md#doab))

### dob
 
 <i>n.</i> a dollop; a small portion or lump of any soft, cohesive or glutinous material; common in NZ speech. [ca1940 Havelock e.g. a dob of mud, butter, plasticine, of porridge on one's plate.]

### dob in
 
 <i>v.</i> to inform on, to betray to authorities.

>  <b>Dave Armstrong</b> 'Well, I did tell the Maoris that if they didn't agree to my little "tiriti" ,' chuckled Janet, 'I'd ring up Fisheries and dob them in for poaching.' <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,116</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To deliberately betray, or, in a less serious situation, to deliberately reveal detrimental information concerning another - naturally to his disadvantage. .in 4;`They reckoned nobody had the slightest clue, but it looks as if someone was watching them. The police walked up to their tent while they were still asleep. Somebody dobbed them in alright.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],273</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust and NZ English [TS] 273]

>  `My father said are you going to dob me in to the farmer,' he said. <i>(Feb 22, 1984,Dominion,5</i>)

>  The pre-teen courier was dobbed in by a nine-year-old classmate and taken away for a chat with some friendly policemen who immediately recognised the `lucerne' for what it was. <i>(Oct 19, 1986,Sunday News,7</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> `Thank you, Sir!' said Winston, glad to be out and free, glad, too, to know who it was that `dobbed them in'. <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,103</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dob in to betray; to contribute funds; to put someone in trouble, perhaps oneself; eg `Watch your step with him, he'll dob you in for the hell of it.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,37</i>)

>  Unless people are prepared to `dob in' welfare cheats, Social Welfare Minister Michael Cullen has warned, demand will grow for the state to gain coercive powers it should not have. <i>(Mar 26, 1990,Dominion,18</i>)

### dobber
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) [cricket. Abbrev. of dibbly-dobbler?] (See [dibbly-dobbler](d-dif.md#dibbly-dobbler))

>  The two dobbers took wickets as well as drying up the runs. <i>(Oct 23, 2005,Sunday Star Times,B8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an informer

>  [Hdg] Dobber's name release prompts Winz review.... After being told that he was under investigation for benefit fraud, [he] asked the Department of Work and Income for his personal files. <i>(Jan 16, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>) [NOTES: Cross referenced from <i>WINZ</i>]

### dobbin
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The most advanced innovation on this line was the dobbin, a wheeled truck that would hold about a yard of material. It was run on wooden rails extending from the cutting face to the adjacent gully or filling and added to as the work advanced, ... [114] ... On reaching the end of its run the dobbin was either spragged or tipped by means of a cradle. <i>(1953,Chaslands,113</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> skep

>  Skep: Pronounced skip. Trolley for carrying loose wool in a woolstore or mill. Also called a dobbin. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,59</i>)

### Dobson fly
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The muddy edges are the home of the dobson fly larvae, toe-biters that burrow their way through the mud to lie safe from predators, until night when they emerge, nearly the largest of insect predators, to take their own toll on the life of the river's edge. <i>(Dec 13, 1999,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  Dobson flies, largest of the freshwater insects, will soon be taking flight. <i>(Aug 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

>  There would be other rises during the night, maybe some mayflies and Dobson flies, perhaps a smattering of stoneflies... <i>(Apr 2003,Dominion Post,F4</i>)

>  There would be other rises during the night, maybe some mayflies and Dobson flies, perhaps a smattering of stoneflies... <i>(Apr 12, 2003,Dominion Post,F4</i>)

### DOC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Department of Conservation [ORIGIN: acronym, also DoC]

>  A few Doc huts are the only dwellings. <i>(Sep 12, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> The early eighties was the period when the NZ Forest Service and Conservancy Departments were disbanded and replaced by 'new enlightened government departments' ie Forestcorp and DOC, who professed to put much emphasis on the weight of public opinion. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,38</i>)

>  The Department of Conservation (DoC) is opposed to the use of covenants in the tenure review process. <i>(Jun 20, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,4</i>)

>  ... tying the hands of Doc ... <i>(Sep 3, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: National Radio Morning Report]

>  And as United leader Peter Dunne says, the public image of DOC - fairly or unfairly - is of a body so purist about its conservation mission that it would prefer to ban all introduced animals and exotic plants from these shore and then start on the humans. <i>(Dec 6, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Thanks to all who filled out the reader survey included in the previous issue of 'What's Up, DOC?' <i>(Oct 1991,What's Up Doc,5 : 1</i>)

>  It seems that DOC is now an acronym for Department of Confiscation, with a policy based on "If I can't have it, you can't either". <i>(Jun 19, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Our biggest conservation successes have come whenever DOC has stepped outside the mainstream thinking and bent its own rules. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  'I don't think it would be legitimate for DOC to put funding towards looking for or protecting a bird [South Island kokako] that probably doesn't exist.' <i>(Aug 2009,Forest & Bird,333/24</i>)

>  When heavy rain sets in, and they are trapped in a DOC tramping hut,.. they have to endure more intimacy than they bargained for. <i>(Apr 19, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Further down the road there's <i>another</i> abandoned post office.... Its successor may fall into the hands of Doc, the dreaded Department of Conservation. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  It's probably not in danger from the likes of bearded DOC field reps. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,53</i>)

>  DOC staff are out patrolling the rivers now and will do so until the bait stop running. <i>(Nov 26, 1992,Evening Post,15</i>)

### DOC Estate
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [conservation estate](chi-con.md#conservation-estate))

>  They are in fact symptomatic of problems occurring throughout New Zealand at present as the DOC estate continues expanding with reckless abandon. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,14</i>)

>  There are 1.8 million hectares of land in the Department of Conservation estate on the West Coast, one million in beach forests. <i>(Apr 2000,North & South,82</i>)

>  The so-called DOC Estate is, in fact, the Public Estate administered by the Department of Conservation. <i>(Feb 1, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Land on the DOC estate has generally beenselected for its natural rather thanhuman history. <i>(Jun 1993,What's Up Doc,14:3</i>)

### Doc Shop
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) a nickname for a police or prison medical service. [(prison)]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> I slept well then visited the Doc Shop to see if I was medically fit for prison service. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,12</i>)

### dock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>dock [=] clustered dock: curled dock:broad-leaved dock [s]</i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE</b> A ready reckoner gave the content [of the log] in superficial feet, from which a deduction - a `dock' - was often made for defects in the log such as shakes ... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,39</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural)

>  He works hard top keep them low and reckons that over the past five years he has succeeded in docking an extra 1600 lambs through taking special care of the twins and triplets... <i>(Nov 13, 2003,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  Last week I docked the tails of eighteen lambs. <i>(Oct 22, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)

>  At this time of the year [sc. October] alarge number of lambs are beingcastrated, docked, and earmarked. <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,XLIX/221</i>)

>  The best time to castrate and dock lambsis when they are about three or fourweeks old. <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,XLIX/225</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> hospital

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Jim refused to go into "dock", as hospital was then termed. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,183</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to cut off the tail, esp. of a lamb

>  Fighting dogs' tails were docked to give their opponents on less body part to grab. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  I turned around to see him, haring off with the dogs after a ewe and I had to explain it had been docked a couple of years earlier. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mainly N.I] to cut tails and ear mark and castrate lambs and occ. calves.

>  When the lambs are being docked, note should be taken of any ewes that have not had lambs and these sheep marked. <i>(Oct 1890,NZ Farmer,387</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MAORI DOCK (<i> Rumex flexuosus </i>) is called also native dock, New Zealand dock, and rolling dock. It occurs throughout both islands on native and artificial pastures, showing no preference for moist country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,65,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> dock, <i> Maori [s] Rumex flexuosus </i> Sol. ex Hook.f. <i>Ibid.</i> <i> New Zealand dock [=] Maori dock [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Rumex flexuosus , a native dock whose dry stalk-network can be blown about by wind; =Maori dock, also called Maori dock, native dock.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MAORI DOCK (<i> Rumex flexuosus </i>) is called also native dock, New Zealand dock, and rolling dock. It occurs throughout both islands on native and artificial pastures, showing no preference for moist country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,65,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> rolling dock [=] Maori dock [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Rumex neglectus.

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i> Rumex neglectus </i> sea-shore dock <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i> Rumex neglectus </i> sea-shore dock <i>(1911,AJHR,C-13</i>) [NOTES: Report on the Dune-areas of NZ]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i> Rumex neglectus </i> shore-dock 2N <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> [a native dock] <i> Rumex neglectus </i> SHORE DOCK ... Hairless, short green herb, often flattened. ... coastal ... Native to: [mainly South and southern outlying islands] <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,164</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i> Rumex flexuosus </i> <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: Notes on the Plant Covering of Kennedy's Bush ... Canterbury]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MAORI DOCK (<i> Rumex flexuosus </i>) is called also native dock, New Zealand dock, and rolling dock. It occurs throughout both islands on native and artificial pastures, showing no preference for moist country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,65,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> dock, Maori [s] Rumex flexuosus Sol. ex Hook.f. <i>Ibid.</i> 52 native dock [=] Maori dock [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[check at Maori: transfer to here]]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MAORI DOCK (<i> Rumex flexuosus </i>) is called also native dock, New Zealand dock, and rolling dock. It occurs throughout both islands on native and artificial pastures, showing no preference for moist country. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,1967</i>)

>  <i> dock, Native [=] Maori dock [s] ... </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Rumex flexuosus </i> NATIVE DOCK ...Runa ... Leaves brown-green. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,164</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> dock, hooked [s] Rumex brownii </i> Campd. <i>Ibid.</i> 38 <i> hooked dock [s] Rumex brownii </i> Campd. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> dock, fiddle [s] Rumex pulcher </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>broad-leaved dock [s] Rumex obtusifolius </i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 <i> dock, broad-leaved [s] Rumex obtusifolius </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,8</i>)

>  <i>broad-leaved dock [s] Rumex obtusifolius </i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 <i> dock, broad-leaved [s] Rumex obtusifolius </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> climbing dock [s] Rumex sagittatus </i> Thunb. <i>Ibid.<i/> 22 <i> dock, climbing [s] Rumex sagittatus </i> Thunb. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,8</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> CLIMBING DOCK (<i> Rumex sagittatus </i>) South Africa perennial ... No other plants is like climbing dock - yet it gets confused with Gernam ivy. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,39</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> clustered dock [s] Rumex conglomeratus </i> Murr. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 <i> dock, clustered [s] Rumex conglomeratus </i> Murr. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> crisped dock [=] curled dock [s]</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 22 <i> dock, crisped [=] curled dock [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i> curled dock [s] Rumex crispus </i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 <i> dock, curled [s] Rumex crispus </i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,19</i>)

### dock a paddock
 
 <i>phr.</i> tail, earmark, and castrate lambs in a particular paddock

>  <b>DAVIS</b> .. while we were operating the same day Bill Waikai was docking his paddock over the boundary opposite us. <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,154</i>)

### dockdigging
 
 <i>n.</i> ? doing nothing in particular; walking around looking for a job (from the apparent aimless wandering of those working at digging docks from a paddock). [(obs.)]

>  <b>JENKYNS</b> It was the Dane's turn to stay in the house, so Gilbert and I set out `dockdigging' as it was called. <i>(1892,Hard Life,228</i>)

### docked
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  From many centres the tally of dockedlambs is very reassuring. <i>(Oct 23, 1912,Otago Witness,17</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> disabled (through loss of a limb) (phs merely a fig. use of v1. in a farming context). [(obs.)]

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> `I should have thought [your working-] days over.' `Why?' `Well you're docked a bit aren't you.' [to a one-armed man] <i>(1907,Return of Joe,19</i>)

### docker
 
 <i>n.</i> a tantrum; fit of angry struggling.

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> [1863 incident] I could ride, but not with the seat required to maintain one's position on a buck-jumper, and the grey could go in a `docker' when started. <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,165</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a worker who cuts off lambs' tails and testicles.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Docking in those days was vastly different to which it is today. It was a bloody job for both catchers and lambs. The docker usually ended the day with a face looking like a squashed tomato. When a lamb was caught it was carried to a bench and held in front of the docker before proceedings commenced. The docker noted the sex, then earmarked accordingly. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,90</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [timber milling]

>  Meanwhile without me there to carry out my duties as a docker, cutting the rough ends off the timber and classing it, the mill clogged up. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,25</i>)

### docket
 
 <i>n.</i> invoice

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> My duty was to pull metal frames out of the steel cabinets and then twist metal tags out of the frames, and afterwards to collect tags which were no longer wanted for dockets and to fit them in turn into their metal frame, and then into a steel cabinet. <i>(1995,Mum,71</i>)

>  It is a great pity that sharp differences of meaning explained in the publisher's publicity for such Anglo-New Zealand words as mall, docket, jack-up, social studies, etc. were not included in the dictionary proper. <i>(Mar 28, 1979,Otago Daily Times,21</i>) [NOTES: (G. Griffiths)]

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> While I'd no objection to seeing on a return docket sometimes a packet of cigarettes.., if I found there was not food for the family as well, I would then make out the order to the wife only and put 'No one else' on the bottom of it. <i>(1974,Sugarbag Years,152</i>)

>  For 10 years he'd kept the docket for a bag of chippies he'd bought for a meeting. <i>(1997,Mana,Autumn : 78</i>)

### docking
 
 <i>n.</i> removing a large part of a young animal's (usu. lamb's) tail to stop dung sticking to it, leaving behind the `dock', or part of the tail (with tailing the usu. term in NZ (OED2 dock v1. 1 b. 1491), and generates some special combinations)

>  It knocks the sheep about too much to doother than have one principal muster fordocking. <i>(Oct 16, 1912,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  It was the docking... It reminded me of the execution of Mary Queen of Scots. <i>(Mar 25, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,39</i>)

>  The docking, the crutching, the shearing, everyone's involved and we each had our own little jobs. <i>(Jan 2000,North & South,92</i>)

>  She said the farmers have been in here talking about docking one week, and the next, counting their stock losses. <i>(Dec 1992,Metro,15</i>)

>  Parliament just gave a first tick to Labour's Dianne Yates's bill to ban tail docking ... Depending on what the dog is likely to be doing, they argue, docking is actally a great kindness. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  Nearly 17 per cent. loss of lambs through docking is an extraordinary occurrence. <i>(Oct 22, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)

>  <b>A9</b> Docking and fencing are right up my street. <i>(Jun 16, 2003,Dominion Post</i>)

>  Tail docking, castration and dehorning are routinely performed - quite legally - on sheep and cattle in New Zealand. <i>(Mar 1, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  The knife could soon fall on the practice of docking dogs' tails.... A ban on tail docking was first put forward as part of the Animal Welfare Bill in 1999, but dropped after widespread opposition to the ban put the entire bill at risk. <i>(Jan 18, 2004,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  The castration and docking of lambs is animportant yearly procedure on farms wheresheep are bred. <i>(1920,NZ Journal of Agriculture,XXI/142</i>)

>  DOCKING. The operation of cutting thetail off the lamb is usually carried outimmediately before the animal isreleased. <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,XLIX/224</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> Lambs have two well-defined periods ofrisk, namely, immediately following andresulting from docking and castration. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,425</i>)

>  There are three common methods of docking tails: by using the knife, the searing-iron, or the rubber ring... The searing or docking iron must be at 'black heat', not red hot... Docking by the searing iron is quite efficient if carefully carried out. <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,199</i>)

>  Experience included dipping, dagging, crutching, footrotting and docking. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/43</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Resident Bert Halford, 89, took a break from docking to show us around. <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,1</i>)

>  Not only did it mean that the Bay had lost two-thirds of its front row, but also Cotter had lost his docking crew. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Their captain's comparison of this to another rural work habit involving docking irons was also rejected. <i>(Sep 5, 1992,Dominion,10</i>)

>  When docking time comes around many ofthe lambs are over eight weeks old,whilst others are very young. <i>(1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,XLIX/225</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the removal of lambs tails with either knife, searing iron or rubber ring. [Used mainly in the N.I.and is used loosely to include ear-marking and castration of male lambs.] (See [tailing](t-ten.md#tailing))

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> Docking we used to do out on the paddocks. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,100</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> 'Come up for the docking,' said Alf, who managed Otairi Station, near Hunterville. Docking is Northspeak for marking, but wherever it is you have to muster the sheep, yard them and do unspeakable things like dagging, drenching, earmarking, tailcutting and a considerable amount of castration. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,168</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WALLIS</b> In Awatere there had been sacks of grain and seeds, ... boxes of nails, docking knives, ... <i>(1963,Point of Origin,18</i>)

### docking area, docking yard
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As a young and silly lamb, without any proper knowledge of what is liable to happen in this hard old world, Hermit followed his mother into a docking area. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,124</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> The hessian, supported by standards, was used for the holding yard, the lambs never charging it as they do with netting. The docking yard was made of hurdles in the conventional way. <i>(1969,Molesworth,99</i>)

### docking board
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a flat board placed temporarly on a hurdle.[upon which lambs are placed for docking tails, castration and ear marking.]

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> The 'catch' then takes a lamb, passes his hands between its forelegs, seizes its hindlegs below the hock and places it sitting on the docking board. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,28</i>)

### docking chute
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a crush that holds calves and other stock for docking and marking.

>  Clearing sale: searing irons, elastrators etc, docking chutes, dehorners ... <i>(Jun 21, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

### docking cradle
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a metal cradle designed to hold lambs for ease of castration, tailing and marking.

>  Farm clearing sale: .. portable dagging plant, docking gates, cradle, scrim and standards. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

### docking gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a metal hurdle with a ledge or cradleon which to to rest lambs for docking.

>  Clearing sale: stumping jack .. docking gates ... <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,46</i>)

>  Farm clearing sale: portable dagging plant, docking gates, cradle, scrim ... <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

### docking iron
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an instrument used to sear the tails of lambs or calves.

>  Clearing sale: cow lifter, dehorners, docking irons. <i>(Jun 19, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

### docking muster
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a muster of ewes and lambs for the purpose of tailing, marking and docking.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Dogs that have never barked since the last docking muster ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,22</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Most were bearded, as they had arrived that day after the docking muster on the Ewe Country-twelve days' hard going. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,6</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> For such a man [a shepherd with control of his dogs] the first docking muster will hold no terrors. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,97</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> By now, most of the lambs have grown quite big, but there are always small ones as well as a number of wild 'long tailers' that were missed in the earlier docking muster. <i>(1976,High Country Sheep Station,26</i>)

### docking net
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long sheet of tarred, thick string netting used as temporary yards [to contain ewes and lambs prior to drafting for docking. ]

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> We had to run out docking nets on to posts that were in the ground ready to make the holding yards for the shepherds to yard into. These docking nets we used were marvellous things and they did not catch the wind like docking scrim, which became the norm later. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,23</i>)

>  Clearing sale: 3 rolls docking netting, assorted docking scrims and nets ... [docking netting] <i>(Jun 21, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

### docking points
 
 <i>n.</i> [plural noun or from phrase to dock points?]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Docking points: At a trial each dog starts his run with a total of 100 points. He will lose points (or get docked) for any error or undesirable feature in his work. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

### docking ring
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rubber ring used to remove lamb tails and to emasculate male lambs.

>  Two thousand docking rings, a wool sack, 3 mm moulded steel, a toi-toi plant, shade cloth .. made for the winning entry in one of the Mystery Creek Fieldays agricultural art wear competition. The docking rings were all sewed together by hand. <i>(Jun 2002,Manawatu Evening Standard</i>)

### docking scrim
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) fibrous material made from flax. [Used elsewhere but particularly widely used in New Zealand hill country farming for making temporary yards for docking and for funnelling animals into a race or loading bank.]

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> These docking nets were marvellous things and they did not catch the wind like docking scrim, which became the norm later. As they did not flap in the wind like docking scrim, it was easier to yard sheep up against them. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,23</i>)

>  Clearing sale: old wool press, concrete mixer, docking scrim, truck crate ... <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,27</i>)

>  Clearing sale: .. 4 snig chains, spinning jenny, docking scrim ... <i>(May 11, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,47</i>)

>  Farm clearing sale: .. portable dagging plant: docking gates, cradle, scrim and standards. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

>  Clearing sale: 3 rolls docking netting .. assorted docking scrims and nets ... <i>(Jun 21, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

### docking table
 
 <i>n.</i> [sawmilling]

>  The system of delivery line.. is duplicated in another carrying sawn lengths down from the breast benches to what is called the "docking" table. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(9) : 27</i>)

### docking team
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a number of workers who construct temporary docking yards, and earmark, emasculate and dock tails of young lambs.

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> As it was spring he was soon roped into the docking team. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,35</i>)

### docking yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an enclosure erected temporarily in a paddock, where lambs are docked/marked/emasculated.

>  If there is a small paddock at liberty, the ewes to lamb can be taken from the docking yard and put into it. <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1349</i>)

>  The catching pen of the temporary docking yards should not be too large. <i>(Aug 5, 1936,Weekly News,82</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> .. this entails using temporary docking yards on clean pasture ... <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,141</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> We would arrive at the entrance of a docking-yard with the mob well mothered-up ... <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,11</i>)

### doctor
 
 <i>n.</i> a cook on a ship, sheep station, etc. [(recorded early on the NZ coast, and prob. - well-known to early immigrants by sea).]

>  At 11 A.M. the Doctor and Jim Robinson from Wongooloa, with a Crew of the Atlantic's People. <i>Ib id.</i> 1842 Apr 29 The Doctor and Mate spent the day on shore. <i>(Jun 16, 1839,Piraki Log,89,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

>  [On the way to New Zealand] a fat black fish was seen swimming along following sheep which had been hung out by `the doctor' to wash. <i>(Oct 29, 1850,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/75,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> The old man's full of she-oak - .../ The `doctor's' boilin' stinkin' beef and drinkin' hell-fire rum. <i>(1900,Shanties,132</i>)

>  <b>EADDY</b> ... the Doctor, as all sailing-ships' cooks are dubbed ... <i>(1933,Hull Down,101</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of saws, to sharpen, set, repair, etc. as by a saw-doctor q.v.

>  An average of seven bandsaws a day, from mills around the Wairarapa, come to Fawcetts for doctoring. <i>(Aug 31, 1984,Dominion,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rum and milk as a `pick-me-up'.

>  <b>LUSH</b> After breakfast the Doctor gave me a `Doctor' - i.e. a tumbler of new milk well mixed with a glass of ... [sic] shall I tell? ... - a glass of rum. <i>(Nov 26, 1864,Waikato Journals,37,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> They drank as an `appetiser' before meals, and as a `digester' after meals; they drank to warm the `miner man' and to keep him cool. They opened the day with a `doctor' to stimulate their wakingenergies ... They ended up with a `nightcap' to welcome rest ... <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,516</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> Let's have a doctor Joe! Fetch that rum and milk. ... Raleigh expostulated that at so early an hour `doctors' did not agree with him. <i>(1895,South Sea Siren,206</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ? a pickpocket [(prisoners') ca1941-1945 Riverside Community]



 
 <i>n.</i> a person who castrates young farm animals (? pun on ?). [(farmers')]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The calf, powerless to move, would be ready for the doctor ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,100</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a West Coast publican [Not recorded elsewhere]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Doctor a West Coast publican; the original was Dr Schroder of the two-storeyed Central Hotel,Fitzherbert Street, Hokitika, since replaced by a single storey tavern. [G. Johnston] <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[Transf. to Anglo-maori kuki, cookee a Maori slave, one who prepares meals.]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... a chief was called a nob; a slave, `doctor'. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/318</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station or harvest-camp cook.

>  Doctor - Slang name for station cook. <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On many stations the cook was almost always known as 'the doctor.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the men's cook on (orig. South Island) sheep stations, thence to bush and other camps and gangs of workmen; occas. use of galley for the usual cookhouse on some stations, e.g. Dumfree, Marlborough.

>  <b>WALKER</b> Cook-shop for all hands ... the `doctor's' - cook's - residence. <i>(1902,Zealandia's Guerdon,55</i>)

>  doctor n. a cook; <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  Doctor. Slang name for station cook. Given as S. Australia in [1898 Morris Austral-English] but very commonly used here. [1951, p.365] This was a common term among sailors, and no doubt was introduced by them.] <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  Doctor.-Slang for the cook. This was a common term among sailors, and no doubt was introduced by them. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [In the 1860s] The ship term `bunk' was applied to the beds built against the walls in the men's huts. On many stations the cook was almost always known as `the doctor.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  doctor n. bush term for cook. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> the camp cook, or person running the camp cookhouse in the bush: ??a fig. usage from a nickname rather than a development of `cook' application.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I was known in the [bush fellers], camp as the `doctor', and the men came to me for things they needed from a little store I made a point of carrying. [things in general, or ? medicines] <i>(1898,Bushfalling Reminiscences,42</i>) [NOTES: [1898] A. Anderson's Bushfalling Reminiscences 1898 (1947) in Mulinder A Pioneer Family 42]

### doctor loaf
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BYCROFT LTD</b> Doctor loaf [a 2-crusted quarter] <i>(1934,Bread Baking in NZ</i>)

### doddy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an Aberdeen Angus cattle beast.

>  .. the 9,500 acres comprising Gwavas Station, Tokokino, make an ideal breeding ground for the big herd of Doddies, maintained here. Their [Aberdeen Angus] invasion of the broad acres of this rich province has long since been complete with the results that the herds of 'Doddies' continue to multiply. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1360</i>)

>  His observations have convinced him that the Doddies invariably kill about 2 cwt better than they look ... <i>(1925,NZ Farmer,1361</i>)

### dodger
 
 <i>n.</i> an (advertising) leaflet. [(printers' ? obsolesc)]

>  A country printer complains that when a church wants `dodgers' printed about an entertainment or a mission to fill empty pews ... <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/5/4</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> dodger leaflet <i>(1984,Blackball 08,243</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a station animal that evades muster. (See [hermit](hea-hom.md#hermit); [double-fleecer](dou-dyn.md#double-fleecer),[bushranger](bri-byo.md#bushranger))

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> .. we snared quite an assortment of dodgers - even half a dozen wild cattle caught napping well out from the bush edge. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,97</i>)

### dodgey up
 
 <i>v.</i> to improvise something artfully

>  `Someone says that the ramp from the yards won't be high enough to the top deck of the trucks. Looks like we'll have to dodgey something up.' <i>(Nov 1, 1983,Dominion,6/2</i>)

### Dodgy Brothers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dodgy Brothers dealers have been shakenout by the personal computer wars of thelast few years, although some will alwaysexist. <i>(Sep 21, 1992,Dominion Infotech Weekly,4</i>)

### doer
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse which runs or races well.

>  He's a good doer and might need another race to sharpen him. <i>(Sep 5, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,20</i>)

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> The Priest [a racehorse] is a good `doer.' <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,114</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> of a person, a character; humorous fellow; hard case; dag; see also hard doer (1916) [relevant cits copied to there]. [[?Move hard doer to here??]]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A doer is a jester, or and eccentric (cf OED Supp under Do n 2b). <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,88</i>)

>  doer, hard doer ... n. tough character, hard case. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  doer ... 3. N.Z. a thriving farm animal. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,329</i>) [NOTES: [1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 142 good doer]]

>  doer ... N.Z. & Austral. eccentric person (esp. hard doer). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,221</i>)

### doer-upper
 
 <i>n.</i> a house requiring DIY work

>  With prices in the inenr city reaching record levels and "doer-uppers" edging towards $300,000, some Disks - that's double income, some kids - are buying better-value-for-money properties in previ9ously "less desirable" suburbs. <i>(Jul 3, 2003,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  A lot of cheaper houses are doer-uppers and pretty rough, but what's wrong with that at entry level, if you're prepared to do a bit of decorating? <i>(Aug 28, 2004,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

>  It's like buying an old house that you know is a doer-upper but it turns out to be worse than you thought. <i>(Dec 7, 2005,Capital Times,11</i>)

>  A Seatoun doer-upper, which sold at auction last August for $462,000, has just sold again for about $20,000 more. <i>(Feb 11, 2004,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

>  [Hdg] A nice doer-upper... It's his bid to have this $2.4 million "asset of the people" put to better use in the hope that somehow this might justify spending an estimated $400,000 needed for renovations. <i>(Apr 14, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  It was the only house in the street that wasn't refurbished or well maintained in its original state, and it was for sale as a serious doer-upper. <i>(Dec 25, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),12</i>)

>  The council admits that the building is a bit of a doer-upper, with "a large degree of maintenance" required to make it waterproof. <i>(Nov 5, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  'Doer-upper' homes at the lower end of the market sell well. <i>(Mar 23, 2002,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Yet it also includes old workers' cottages in Aro Valley, which might be expected to sell as "doer-uppers", and tired houses often let to students in Mt Cook. <i>(Jul 5, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Cheaper "doer-uppers" are also selling fast, while mid-price homes - from about $300,000 - are the slower movers. <i>(Mar 23, 2002,Evening Post,1</i>)

### dog
 
 <i>n.</i> an inferior or badly made shoe. [(shoe trade)]

>  DON'T BUY `DOGS' [title] ?Bad shoes are often referred to in the trade as `dogs', but no disrespect to our four footed friends is intended. <i>(Mar 16, 1982,Karori and Western Suburbs News</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> low, contemptible person; an abusive term for an inmate [(prisoners')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> `You're a dog!' [the prison warder] shouted into my face. `You're a filthy bastard and a pig!' <i>Ibid.</i> 180 A tealeaf is just a cunt in my eyes, and you'd be about the lowest ... You're a peter thief and you're a dog. <i>Ibid.</i> 248 Dog. (n) Low contemptible person. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,32</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> SANDY: ARSELICKER! DOG! CUNT! <i>Ibid.</i> 109 Dog. Glossary an abusive term for a person. <i>(1984,Outside In,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bat

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In the simplest form of Tip Cat the players competed to see who could hit the cat farthest. The cat was a thin, six-inch stick with pointed ends. Their bat (or dog) was a round stick about the size of a cricket wicket (two and a half feet long). The cat was placed on the ground, tapped on its point, and then as it jumped up into the air, hit once more. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,156</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> . [work by cattle or sheep dog]

>  There are about 150 ways that an employee can earn demerit marks, and to escape them hemust wear wings and a halo, and be capable of miracles. So that a marked man is easily got rid of or disrated. One man complained at the meeting of the manner in which he had been `dogged'. <i>(May 13, 1911,NZ Truth,307/4/5</i>)

>  By the way, this gentleman got a severe hurt last week through being rushed by an excited cow that he was yarding up, and which had been dogged too much. <i>(Oct 3, 1890,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the Mongrel Mob

>  Gang affiliations give rise to terms such as <i>dog</i> for a member of the Mongrel Mob gang. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

>  Talking is 'barking', to be called a dog is a term of respect, and conversations are peppered with 'sieg f ..ing heil' as a greeting. Patched members are referred to as dogs. 'It's certainly not a derogatory term within the mob and it's used with quite a lot of respect,' he said. <i>(Sep 9, 2004,Press, The,A5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a connecting device

>  The iron "dogs" used to join the rail and sleepers during the construction of the line in 1928 are also intact. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/14</i>)

>  He unhappily got on the logs to knock out the "dog" that was holding it to the raft. <i>(Nov 29, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Canis familiaris</i>, the dog introduced by the pre-European Polynesian settlers, the Maori dog, native dog often so-called; kuri.

>  The country is certainly destitute of all sorts of beasts either wild or tame except Dogs and Ratts, [sic] the former are tame and live with people who breed and bring them up for no other purpose than to eat ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/277,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAYLEY</b> These were by far the finest set of Indians I saw during our stay at Charlotte Sound. ... They had many fine Dogs with them of the Fox breed. <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 June 4 BAYLY Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/209]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A good many dogs were observed in their canoes, which ... they kept tied with a string, round their middle; they were of a rough long-haired sort, with pricked ears, and much resembled the common shepherd's cur ... They were of different colours, some spotted, some quite black, andothers perfectly white. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/219</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> They have no larger animal than the dog, which is a native here, usually black and white, with sharp, pricked,up ears, the hair rather long, and in figure a good deal resembling the animal we call a fox-dog - the native name of which is Coraddee. <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,10</i>)

>  dog - goo'ree [kuri] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori wordssee also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284 (|= macron precedes) dog, go|o|ree [kuri]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [? phs related to put on dog `pretentious airs'.]

>  The authors, Messrs Alfred Hill and Arthur Adams, wrote `Tapu' years ago, and passed it on to Mr J.C. Williamson, who prepared for its first production in Australia, but other matters intervened, the opera was shelved until lately, when it was handed over to Mr Tom Pollard to `try on the dog,' as the saying goes, in the very town in which the authors lived and worked for many years. ... <i>(Feb 17, 1903,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: [Review of Alfred Hill's opera Tapu]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gudgeon that has a pointed thread and does not go right through. (See [gudgeon](gra-gyp.md#gudgeon))

>  To this hook is attached a second two-foot length with the pair of short pieces or 'dogs' mounted upon it at a point eight inches from the end. In operation the hook is placed about the [fence] post in the position indicated, and the wire drawn across the handle so that it is engaged by the 'dogs.' <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1326</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. every available gate was simply lifted off its gudgeons (known to Southerners as dogs) and pressed into service at the dog trial. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,33</i>)

>  Clearing sale: ... docking scrim, Hunter chain fence, gate hinges and dogs, wire strainers ... <i>(May 11, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,47</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the main character in Murray Ball's strip cartoon, <i>Footrot Flats</i>

### dog around
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to work [stock] with dogs.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> What with mustering, drafting and mouthing on the farm; then the 11-mile drive to the saleyards, then more drafting and sorting into age-groups and pen lots on the day of the sale, the sheep had been on the move and dogged around for more than a week. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,84</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### dog box
 
 <i>n.</i> position of disgrace; being offside [[A var. of doghouse.], also dogbox]

>  Other errant ministers Ruth Dyson and Dover Samuels were paroled back to the Beehive after a suitble stint in the dogbox. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  (Lodge cartoon) Rather funny you being in the cactus at home - I'm in the dog box with my wife too. <i>(Mar 9, 1953,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  A red-faced cricket writer is in the dogbox. <i>(Sep 24, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),24/4</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's in the dogbox with his wife, he won't be in, said Ted Clifford. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,155</i>)

>  What's this to do with errant husbands? is the question our Beaver-reader is certain many people will ask. `Well, they're either in the dog box or leading a dog's life,' he explained. `So there's no difference.' <i>(Oct 21, 1972,Marlborough Express,6/1</i>)

>  dog box n. N.Z. sl. disgrace; disfavour: in the dog .us box. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,329</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 220; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 143, DOGHOUSE]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cage mounted on rear of vehicle to transport working dogs.

>  ...1 Utility dog box holds 6-8 dogs,...1 dog box, netting gates... <i>(Nov 10, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  Clearing sale .. 1 dog box ... <i>(Nov 10, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,21</i>)

### dog collar
 
 <i>n.</i> (excessive) froth on a handle or glass of beer [ca1950]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a habitual criminal's indeterminate sentence. Compare Kath(leen Mavourneen).

>  <b>BURTON</b> This is an indeterminate sentence, and can only be given to a man who has ben declared an Habitual Criminal. ... Unless the `Act' is lifted from him by a judge of the Supreme Court it will continue in force for the rest of his life. Among convicts this particular sentence is known as the `dog collar' for obvious reasons. <i>(1945,In Prison,133</i>)

### dog daisy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MORELAND</b> Every place was covered with dog daisies; river-banks, roadside, everywhere an advancing army crept over the hills. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,135</i>)

### dog derby
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an annual winter festival event held at Coronet Skifield, Queenstown, in which shepherds and their dogs compete on a steep downhill course.

>  <b>HALL</b> One of the events is a dog derby. In this, farmers compete with their dogs on the skifield for quite a good prize - three or four days in the North Island. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,105</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> The idea was to send your dog up a hundred and fifty metres, through two poles then back to you again. Before the Dog Derby the dogs have to run up the ski field while their masters take the easy option and ride the chair lift. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,108</i>)

### dog doser
 
 <i>n.</i> an officer of the hydatids campaign

>  Dog dosers, or hydatids control officers as they were later called, orchestrated the whole event. <i>(Sep 29, 2004,NZ Farmers' Weekly,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a peripatetic worker who tests farm dogs for hydatids infestation.

>  <b>BEACH</b> I didn't know that day, when I came home from work, that the dog doser had been and dosed my dogs. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,73</i>)

### dog dosing
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) the act of drenching and purging a dog to obtain a sample of faeces to be examined for parasitic worms.

>  <b>TURNER</b> Dog dosing needs no further explanation in New Zealand. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,142</i>)

>  Dog Dosing: Act of drenching and purging a dog to obtain a sample of faeces to be examined for parasitic worms. This is to compel requirements. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the dosing and testing of farm dogs for infestation of hydatids.

>  <b>TURNER</b> Dog dosing needs no further explanation in New Zealand. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,142</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> "The time has come," the Chairman said, "to think of many things, Of Catchment Boards, dog dosing, and profits that have wings." <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,177</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Dog Dosing: Act of drenching and purging a dog to obtain a sample of faeces to be examined for parasitic worms. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

### dog dosing strip
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mention "down at the dog-dosing strip" to a more mature generation and there's a nostalgic sigh as they recall a slice of history and brilliantly executed campaign. ... Dog-dosing strips are part of the rural folklore. <i>(Sep 29, 2004,NZ Farmers' Weekly,13</i>)

>  Eight hundred voluntary committees set themselves up, discouraging farmers from feeding raw sheep offal to dogs and arranging to have all dogs dosed with purges and tapeworm poisons every six weeks at "dosing strips". <i>(Oct 28, 2002,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Drive a man mental it would - On the dog-dosing strip at Kuripuni I fell in love with you - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,162</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We're pretty solitary except when we throw a party. Me more so than Gray who at least [20] sees the other chaps at the dog-dosing strip. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,19</i>)

>  Dosing strip: an area of ground where official dosing by hydatids control officers is carried out. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

>  dosing strip n. (in New Zealand) an area set aside for treating dogs suspected of having hydatid disease. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,333</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 224; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 144]]

>  <b>AVERY</b> &#65279;By the Dog Dosin' Strip at Dunsandel,I fell in love with you. <i>(1974,Ken Avery Songbook, The,Unpaged</i>) [NOTES: &#65279;The hydatid hinderers, by the way, don'tlike the term 'dog dosing strip'; theyprefer to call it 'hydatids testingarea.']
