<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary dog.

>  Dog shepherds - dog shepherds are common in Canterbury, parts of Nelson and other places in New Zealand. <i>(Apr 1883,Northern New Zealand Settler,105</i>)

### dog tax
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A constable from Dunedin called to collect dog tax. <i>(Mar 23, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I remember in the [eighteen] fifties that men used to come to collect the dog tax, and if I remember right, the amount was 10/- for any kind of dog. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,99</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> I remember in the [eighteen] fifties that men used to come to collect the dog tax, and if I remember right, the amount was 10/- for any kind of dog. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,82</i>)

### dog tin
 
 <i>n.</i> a dog's feeding bowl

### Dog Town
 
 <i>n.</i> (derogatory) an early nickname for Port Chalmers (cf. the occasional PIGTOWN for Picton) [(coastal seamen's) ca1920 Pugh-Williams p.c.May 1951 Port Chalmers, perhaps because it comprises the proverbial `only one man and a dog' only.]

>  <b>BOWMAN</b> During the [1890] strike about 100 seamen unattached in Port Chalmers stayed in hotels and boarding houses, but a large number of them found residence in the "baches" in what was called "Sweat Rag Alley", consisting of a number of small baches or cribs in a double row where the Corporation Yard now is. Each bach housed three or four men and it was said that each man had a dog. From this fact, the area became known as "Dogtown". <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,53</i>)

>  To the uninitiated this may mean nothing, but, to those in the know, the quickest way to cause a riot and to raise an insurrection is to call an inhabitant of Port Chalmers a dweller in Dogtown. <i>(Jul 8, 1911,NZ Truth,315/7/1</i>)

>  <b>CRYER</b> There are two explanations for the term Dog Town. The first has its clue in the name of the town, Port Chalmers, which obviously had a lot of docks. As with the Isle of Dogs in London, there is a belief that in the old days the docks of Port Chalmers caused it to be referred to as Dock Town, and the word became corrupted. But there are also legends that.. there were always many seamen about, sometimes unemployed, and all of them were accompanied by their dogs. Most of these men lived in cottages in George Street which became known locally as Dog Town and the name spread to mean Port Chalmers in general. It's occasionally heard even today. <i>(2001,Curious Questions,63</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> And during the troubles [sc. an 1890 strike] , about 100 seamen from other ports stayed in baches in an area called 'Sweat Rag Alley' in Port Chalmers. I t was said that each resident there had a dog - and the name 'Dogtown' unfortunately stuck to the Borough for a long time. <i>(1973,Days of Fortune,42</i>)

### dog trial
 
 <i>n.</i> a competition to select the most skilful sheepog-and-handler combinations by means of set problems in sheep control. [(usu. pl.)]

>  Most dog trial people are pretty decent. <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Dominion,32</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Dog Trials: Need little description in New Zealand. However, it may be advisable to describe the various events. Affiliated clubs mainly hold three standard events - the Long Pull, the Short Pull and Yard, and the Huntaway. <i>(1949,High Country Days,194,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The expression is in frequent use at dog trials. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,369</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Incidentally, Kuripapanga was the scene of the first dog trial ever to be held in the North Island, and a hill there is still called Dog Trial Hill. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,63</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On arrival at Puketitiri, Frank found, that owing to dog trials being held that day at Patoka, Mr. Fitzpatrick, the hotel keeper was the only one left in the village with a car <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,163</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Dog Trials: In New Zealand most dog trial clubs hold four standard events - the long pull; the short head drive and yard; the zig-zag huntaway; and the straight hunt. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> Waimumu Dog Trials held in May, 1912, and April 1913. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,318</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dog trials test of dog skill in controlling sheep, described famously by John Gordon in annual TV series. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,38</i>)

>  Named by Joseph Romanos as one of the top 10 New Zealand women golfers of the last century, Fullerton-Smith was also one of only two female Masters of the Hunt in New Zealand - and she won a gold watch in the Ohingaiti Dog Trials. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  Sheep dog trials are tests of efficiency. The trial grounds are not training grounds, but testing grounds, and therefore not for the inexperienced man with a half-trained or badly-trained dog... Before the war there were about 130 sheep dog trial clubs in New Zealand. <i>(1948,NZ Journal of Agriculture,76/46</i>)

>  [Hdg] The Sheep Dog Trial. The Waitaki Collie Dog Club's annual meeting commenced on Thursday morning. <i>(Mar 20, 1890,Otago Daily Times,10</i>)

>  [Advt] National Dairy Show... LeapingCompetitions and Sheep Dog Trials. <i>(Apr 20, 1918,Feilding Star,4</i>)

>  [Hdg] Sheep Dog Trials. March 20 and 21.- Mackenzie Collie Dog Trials. <i>(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  The fifth annual dog trials.. were commenced.. yesterday. <i>(Jun 17, 1924,Otago Witness,47</i>)

>  The event of the year was my trip to the Springfield dog trials. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,31</i>) [NOTES: 1948 edition]

>  The Mata sheep dog trials were held on Bexhaven Station. <i>(May 16, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Dog trials, usually the highlight of the season, were now the order of the day. <i>Ibid.</i> 50 To Jim dog trials were just a happy outing. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,49</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Entries are pretty good and keen this year, in 1913 at the Ohura Dog Trials. <i>(1965,Open Country,10</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They became far too dog trial-minded - the schooling and perfecting of their dogs for that purpose had become of more consequence to them than the care of their sheep. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,121</i>)

>  Hew added to the farm storage, did other farm work in the district and was a keen supporter of the Burkes Pass dog trials. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/31</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> Trivia such as the Matemateora Dog Trials were relegated to the remaining 85 per cent of p. 1. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,8</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Everyone knew that the Brysons all dog trialled, so no one was surprised that there was a girl trialling. <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Dominion,32</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) contest for heading dogs and huntaways, held on set courses.

>  The Sheep Dog Trial. The Waitaki Collie Dog Club's annual meting commenced on Thursday morning. <i>(Mar 20, 1890,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The event of the year was my trip to the Springfield dog trials. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,31</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Another point of interest in this report is that the first dog trials to be held in the North Island were at Kuripapanga, at the foot of Gentle Annie, in 1888. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,147</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The Oamaru Times (now the Oamaru Mail) of 9 July 1869 carried a report of thirs annual Wanaka dog trial on 22-23 June of that year. This proves two things - that the Wanaka club held their first event in 1867 (six years before the Welsh), and that the time it takes clubs to get their results in the paper hasn't speeded up in 130 years! <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,28</i>)

### dog trial dance
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a party with live music and dancing, usually held in a woolshed or local hall at the end of dog trial contests.

>  <b>PATOKA REUNION COMMITTEE</b> Prior to the 1930s the dogtrial dances were a feature of the social calendar. [dogtrial dance] <i>(1985,Podocarp to Pasture,64</i>)

### dog trial stick
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stick less than a metre in length that is used to prompt sheep or dog in a dog trial contest.

>  <b>GORDON</b> After the dog, the only other assistance or weapon a triallist can have is what a British shepherd would call a crook, but to us is simply a dog trial stick. It is much shorter than an overseas triallist's crook - it can't be longer than a metre - and it can be used only to wave at sheep .. sheep can run into a trial stick. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,8</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The crook, or as we usually call it, dog trial stick, is very much the focus of unwitting attention and nervous release. [trial stick] <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,201</i>)

### dog trialist
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Until last year, Australian dog triallists competed in separate state-run competitions... Australian triallists don't have huntaways (those loud barking dogs) and, while in New Zealand the trials use a combination of human and dog, the Aussies have the dog do all the work. <i>(Sep 9, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He had been recommended to me by Bert Benbow, an old mustering mate and now a leading New Zealand dog trialist. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,157</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep-dog owner who enters dog trial contests.

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> The four males were taken over by keen dog trialists of the district ... <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,43</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Every dog triallist in New Zealand has trouble keeping three sheep together in the middle of a paddock out of the hook, the open enclosure that holds them for trialing. [dog triallist] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,147</i>)

### dog trialling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Vicki started dog trialling when she was about 12. <i>(Jul 8, 2000,Dominion,32</i>)

>  The show also features down-on-the-farm Kiwi entertainments, in keeping with the Bay's rural history, such as dog trialling. <i>(Oct 24, 1991,Dominion,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] running a dog when it is not necessary.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dog-trialling: wasting a mustering team's time by running a dog unnecessarily. [dog trialing; sheepdog trialing] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,25</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a competition held on a specific hunting or heading course, where sheepdogs compete to work sheep faultlessly within a certain time.

>  Sheepdog trialing is an activity that many non-trialing farmers have dismissed as being artificial and impractical. <i>(Jul 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,46</i>)

>  The trick: how to make an entertaining programme without sacrificing too many dog-trialing traditions, to produce a dog-trialing equivalent of one-day cricket. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Horse sports aside, New Zealand's two best-known rural contests are sheep shearing and dog trialling, both of which have emerged from the development of the most intensive sheep farming industry in the world. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,7</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Of all the rural activities that have had regular coverage in written form, sheep dog trialling is probably the best ... <i>(2001,Fresh Fields,198</i>)

>  The kitchen was one of the best - and warmest - places to be at this week's dog trialling event. <i>(Apr 4, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

### dog tucker
 
 <i>n.</i> meat for dogs (also figurative use)

>  At that stage we knew we were dog tucker,but I couldn't have gone around sayingwe'd lose, because negativity breedsnegativity. <i>(Oct 25, 1993,Dominion,9</i>)

>  <b>OVERTON</b> By a piece of bad luck we lost three sheep the first day...These were skinned and used for dog tucker on our journey. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,36</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The sheath-knife could have skinned a sheep, cleaned out a horse's hoof, or cut up the smelly dogtucker, but it was always sharp. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,177</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The next day they would be going pighunting for dogtucker, as well as moving stock. <i>(1986,Haliburtons of Glenfarg,1</i>)

>  Bee had killed one of the wethers for dog-tucker. <i>(1965,Open Country,45</i>)

>  The roundup was a kneejerk reaction that would see 220 horses disposed of at "dogtucker" value. <i>(Apr 23, 1993,Dominion,5</i>)

>  She can do anything on a farm except killdog-tuckers. <i>(Jan 14, 1995,Dominion,14</i>)

>  A joker rings him one day and says there's this horse, know anyone who'd be interested, dog tucker otherwise and not bad. <i>(Aug 18, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  And no, the mare that caused all the trouble hasn't ended up as dog tucker. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,19</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> Mavis Davidson.. recalled that army doctors took one look at [John Pascoe's] spindly shanks' and classified him as Grade CIII - 'unfit for military service', a category that as Pascoe overheard one woman tell another on a bus one day meant 'not fit for dog tucker'. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,195</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Dog tucker and mutton on a place that size will add up to 300 or more sheep. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) 1. an animal fed to dogs. 2. a sheep set aside for feeding to dogs, usually an animal of sub-standard quality. [also commonly used for any person or thing considered poor quality.] (See [killer](kee-kiw.md#killer))

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In the old mustering camps the dog tucker was usually wild pig. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,120</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It [the rifle] was ideal for knocking over an old horse for dog tucker or killing a beast for beef. 2. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,82</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Kennedy, with a few minutes to spare, had killed and hung up mutton sheep, but there were still the two 'dog tuckers' to be killed. <i>(1949,High Country Days,162</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> I've heard a head shepherd say to a man 'go down to the shed and put the red collar on those two dog tuckers in the yard." <i>(1951,NZ High Country,42</i>)

>  There are such expressive terms as to "bring in" referring to developing land out of the native for farming; 'dog tucker' for sheep kept for feeding dogs ... <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> If by the time you find her the bearing is dirty, badly town or bruised, you have a limited chance of saving her and dog tucker is probably the best option. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,88</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> I used to kill the dog tucker. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,158</i>)

### dog tucker country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is unproductive, run-out and of little use.

>  Dog tucker country is poor, lean, hungry land with a thin sole of grass ... <i>(May 2001,English in Aotearoa,10</i>)

>  Country that is not productive and does not carry much stock: dog- tucker country. [dog-tucker country] <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### dog tucker freezer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a freezer used in an out-building or laundry in which dog meat is stored.

>  Clearing sale..1 dog tucker freezer 22 cu ft 1996 Daily Telegraph March 23. 24 Auction on site .. Dog Tucker Freezer. <i>(Nov 10, 1993,Napier Daily Telegraph,21</i>)

>  For Sale: dog tucker freezer. <i>(Jul 21, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,41</i>)

>  Farm clearing sale: chain saws, dog tucker freezer, ass.tools ... <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,22</i>)

### dog tucker paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock where sheep to be killed for dog food are kept.

>  <b>GORDON</b> There are top looking rams that finish up in the dog tucker paddock because I know too much about them. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,101</i>)

### dog's cake
 
 <i>n.</i> [see cit.]

>  Mother would draw out the embers with a long scraper, and brush out the [153] oven with a big manuka broom dipped in cold water. ... After the bread came out, trays of apples ... would go in to bake, ... or a special `dog's cake' made of pollard, bran, suet, and butter-milk. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,152</i>) [NOTES: c.1940]

### dog's-nose
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Gandhi ... insisted on shouting seven dog's-noses for Horse in the bar of the Shamrock. <i>(1985,Horse,83</i>) [NOTES: c1962]

### dog-brag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> `And I wish you hadn't known what you say you've known. Only a lying, foul-minded swine like you would have made it up, anyway ... That's life for you, Birkett - dog-brag.' <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,129,1970 ed.</i>)

### dog-chipping
 
 <i>n.</i> the microchipping of dogs

>  Mr Tanczos, a failed leadership contender, argued that the majority of parliament was opposed to the dog-chipping legislation in one form or another. .. In the case of dog-chipping, Labour was relying on the Greens' assurances to shore up its own postition ... <i>(Jun 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### dog-flogger
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering stick.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dog-flogger: musterer's term for his stick. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,25</i>)

>  mustering stick: dog flogger. [dog flogger.] <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### dog-hard
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of work] physically exhausting.

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Day after day Bert struggled down there on the board but it was dog-hard work. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,35</i>)

### dog-hole
 
 <i>n.</i> a place unfit for human habitation

>  <b>MAY</b> The country was a dog-hole ... the forty-acre system a most villainous take-in. <i>(1869,Guide to Farming in NZ,42</i>)

### dog-kennel
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, tramping] a low bivouac or temporary shelter.

>  <b>PICKERING</b> By including the old tin musterers' huts, the 'dog-kennel' bivouacs, recognised rock bivs, rock overhangs and the odd sea-cave, accommodation of some sort has now run up to almost a thousand sites in the hills. <i>(1988,Hills, The,7</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Bivouacs are shrunken versions of huts and affectionately called dog kennels. <i>(1988,Hills, The,82</i>)

### dog-leg
 
 <i>n.</i> a fence constructed of crooked sticks and branches held together by their own crookedness.

>  Now our stockyard is different from most - the only substantial part of it being the gate. The rest of it is what is usually known as 'dogleg' and is composed of small logs piled up, with an occasional post to keep them upright. [dog-leg fence] <i>(1926,NZ Dairyman,137</i>)

>  the log fence and the dog-leg are the cheapest, and most common on new clearings. <i>(1883,Brett's Colonists Guide,18</i>)

>  In fencing we have a number of different methods, from the primitive dog-leg to the more handsome and substantial post and wire. <i>(1887,NZ Farmer,July 196</i>)

>  <b>DONEY</b> Dog-leg is a good fence and stands two or three years if put up properly. <i>(1891,Essays on Bush-Farming,18</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> In full dogleg(ged) fence; applied to a primitive kind of fence made of rough timber or poles, crossed spars, which are the doglegs, placed at intervals, keep in place a low rail resting on short posts, and are themselves fixed by heavy saplings resting in the forks above (Morris); but see cits.

>  Dog'.leg fence'. (d&omacr.g'.l&ebreve.g f&ebreve.ns'.).} A kind of rough fence, Dog'.leg (d&omacr.g'.l&ebreve.g), n. } made of posts and rails. Light posts (about 3 ft. 6 in. in height) are arranged in pairs, crosswise, so as to form a fork about 18 in. from the top. Each fork supports a rail, which is laid slantwise and plaited in and out among the three or four neighbouring supports, its lower end being driven into the ground. Sometimes the whole is capped with a horizontal rail, laid from fork to fork. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2021</i>)

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> The `dog-legged' type of fence ... was apparently a purely Canadian type ... made of stakes of varying length and the trunks of small trees. <i>(1935,Gael Fares Forth, The</i>)

>  There was no fencing wire in those days [in Auckland ca1865] so we had to make what is called a `dog-leg fence'. This was composed of stakes driven crossways in the ground and the long sticks laid with one end on the ground and the other end on the crossed stakes. <i>(1943,Memoirs of Pioneers</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The tiny flower patch surrounding the shack was protected by a dog-leg fence - that is a fence of crooked sticks and branches held together by their own crookedness. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,66</i>)

>  The dog-leg fence, found in North America only in the Maritime Provinces of Canada, reappeared in the Nova Scotian settlement at Waipu, and from there spread to some other bush settlements in the north. It was constructed by driving two crossing stakes into the ground, and in the notch of these placing a third rail which followed the direction of the fence ... It was claimed that it would keep out cattle, but the fence was never widely adopted probably because of its tendency to get out of repair easily. <i>(Oct 1965,NZ Geographer,147</i>) [NOTES: (Hargreaves `Farm Fences')]

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> So the farm gradually took shape. Small paddocks were fenced, first the crude `dog-leg' of roughly-split light posts, then post-and-rail and later barbed wire. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,45,1956 ed.</i>)

>  dogleg adj. applied to primitive fence made of rough timber. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  dog-leg fence NZ & Austral. zigzag fence made of logs supported on crossed poles. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,221</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> doglegged shape of a crooked fence; from the proverbially crooked nature of a dog's hind leg, now more familiar in the dogleg approach to some golf holes. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,38</i>)

### dog-money
 
 <i>n.</i> an official (Public Service) allowance for the upkeep of working dogs. [(deercullers') [Move to dog attrib.]]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> You'll get five shillings a week dog-money if your pup turns out any good. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,14</i>)

### dog-paddle
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> Bojay shook his [head] and started dog-paddling. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,161</i>)

### dog-poor
 
 <i>adj.</i> of sheep, very unthrifty

>  <b>PRESTON</b> But none of the blocks showed the least recovery; there was no second growth. In the Spring we had only 30% of lambs and the sheep were dog-poor. <i>(1978,Woolgatherers</i>)

### dog-skin
 
 <i>n.</i> the skin of the kuri or Maori dog, esp. as dogskin cloak, dogskin mat

>  ... in each of the Canoes were two or three Cheifs [sic] and the habits of these were superior to any we had yet seen, the Cloth they were made on was of the best sort and cover'd on the out side with Dog skins put on in such a manner as to look agreeable to the Eye. Few of these people were tattow'd or mark'd in the face like those we have seen farther to the south, but several had their Backsides tattou'd much in the same manner as the Inhabitants of the Islands within the Tropics. <i>(Nov 27, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/213,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> they had bits of dog-skin at the four corners of their cloaks.. The boghee-boghee, or shaggy-cloak, which hangs round their necks like a thatch of straw, was almost constantly worn by them ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/214</i>)

>  <b>WILKES</b> In his hand [Pomare] usually carries a short cloak of dog-skin, called topuni, shupuni, or patutu. <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/303</i>)

>  [The Maori men to the making] of their prized dog-skin, or Kiwi-feather, clothing mats ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,/III/8</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]



 
 <i>adj.</i> dogskin cloak, mat, robe

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> He stood in silence leaning on the end of his musket, a bill-hook bright as silver in his belt in front, and a handsome dogskin mat thrown carelessly over his shoulders, ... <i>(Mar 6, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,231,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILKES</b> He was very feeble, ... and clad in an old dog-skin robe. <i>(1853,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/307</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/307]

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The elder men were dressed in their best dogskin and kaitaka mats, some of them had their heads decorated with huia and kotuku feathers, not neglecting their mere[es acute] pounamu (greenstone clubs), and every other ancient emblem of chieftainship ... <i>(May 26, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 May 26 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 36]

>  He gave me a dogskin mat made by the Ngaticahounas [Ngatikahungunu]. <i>(Mar 3, 1849,Torlesse Papers,72,1958 ed.</i>)

>  The early voyagers and the first residents of New Zealand all agree in stating that the Maori dog was unknown in the wild state. It was a purely domesticated species ... valued ... for supplying the material for their much prized dogskin mats ... <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/219</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> .. Te Rangihaeata rose, a tall gaunt savage in appearance, clothed in a dogskin mat. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,42</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Apart from greenstone heirlooms, etc., the most valuable gifts that can be bestowed include the dogskin cloak, the korowai cloak with black edges, and the kiwi feather cloak. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,15</i>)

### dog-skin mat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> The elders in their fly-blown dog-skin mats / yearn for their smoky whares <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,61</i>)

### dog-slow
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  [A NZ yachtsman from Seoul olympic preliminaries speaks about racing;] ... think you're getting up in the water, but find you're going dog-slow. <i>(Sep 15, 1988,TV1</i>) [NOTES: News]

### dog-tired
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [used earlier in Brit. but widely in rural N.Z.] exhausted, like a working sheepdog after a long day's work in the heat.

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The men, and often the children too, sometimes don't arrive home till breakfast time, hungry and dog-tired. <i>(1971,Historic Sheep Stations SI II,31</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> I have seen us leave the station to get home at seven in the evening, dog-tired. Then we would have to feed the horses, have our tea and let the horses out later on. <i>(1998,Goodness Gracious Me,134</i>)

### dog-training paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area where dogs are run around sheep to train them for trial courses.

>  <b>KERR</b> On a warm afternoon these days you might find three old gents in my six-hectare dog-training paddock. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,201</i>)

### dog-trial man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a keen participant or follower of dog trials.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Frank is a son of old Seymour Lambert, once a leading North Island dog-trial man. This was Dudley Bushe, another noted Gisborne dog-trial man ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,199</i>)

### dog-trialing
 
 <i>n.</i> as dog trialling

>  The trick: how to make an entertaining programme without sacrificing too many dog-trialing traditions, to produce a dog-trialing equivalent of one-day cricket. <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### dog-tucker
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  He [a horse] had the most unusual formline in yesterday's Te Rapa racebook - BLORF - short for brought down, lost rider not in the first nine, ran off and fell. Laugh as much as you like, saysWilson, Blorf isn't ready to be dog-tuckered. <i>(Jul 20, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIS</b> They wandered about on deck with the superior yet slightly baffled expressions of stud merinos by some horrible mistake drafted in with a mob of dog-tucker rams. <i>(1964,Watersiders,41 ed.</i>)

>  The dog tucker shed stood on four legs beside the killing shed. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

### dog-tucker tree
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tree in which stock carcases are strung for safe keeping for dog food.

>  Did you rattle the cowboy along this morning? If so, it's quite probable that by tomorrow the whole district will have heard how you murdered him and hung him up the dog-tucker tree. <i>(Sep 27, 1962,NZ Farmer,53</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> This went on for a month or so, until one day we returned from fly camping to find the owner in residence and our arch-enemy [a bull] hanging by his hocks from the dog-tucker tree. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,115</i>)

### dog-walloper
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mustering stick.

>  mustering stick : dog-walloper. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) musterer or shepherd

>  <b>PETER</b> A musterer is often known as a dog-walloper. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,6</i>)

>  shepherd: a dog-walloper. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  They refer to themselves as .. lizards or dog-wallopers ... <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### dog-watch
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The Post Office likewise has its own phrases: `clear down', `six to clear', `dog watch'. <i>(1950,Post Office English Course [TS],14</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> After this he went to the West Coast and worked in the Blackball mine on what was called `the dog watch' because it was an all-night shift. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,273</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> All day and all night there were miners on [128] the move: front shift, back shift and dogwatch. <i>(1980,Canterbury Tale, A,127</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The younger members called themselves `The Dog Watch.' The debate in the daytime was conducted by the elders. The Dog Watch took over after the older member' day's work was done. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,214</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  No.25 81 The late, late shift (usually 11 p.m. - 7 a.m.) for despatchers. A naval term - thought to have been coined by those who thought that particular watch to be `a dog of a job'. <i>(Jul 1983,Metro</i>)

### dog-whistle/dog-whistle politics
 
 <i>n.</i> (politics) a concealed, coded, or unstated idea, usually divisive or politically dangerous, nevertheless understood by the intended hearers. [orig. Australian, now used worldwide]

>  Are you reading my mind Peter? That's exactly what this Jewish New Zealander was about to say. And there are more gay men in NZ than Jews, I'm sure. .. The whole idea of a "mainstream New Zealander" is bogus anyway .. except as dogwhistle code for "real New Zealander". Which is why Jordan's annoyed. And so am I. <i>(Jun 27, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Just Left: Jordon Carter 27/6/2005 http://jtc.blogs.com/just_left/2005.06/gays_aren't_main.html]

>  This, say the critics, is pure dog-whistle, the subtle appeal to racism, sounded at a pitch that is heard only by the right ears. <i>(Jul 10, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  ... what are sometimes called dog whistle politics ... <i>(Jul 5, 2005,Radio NZ / National Radio,Nine to Noon</i>) [NOTES: Linda Clark interviewing Jane Clifton]

>  Critics say the pair - who run Crosby/Textor, a public relations and polling company with branches in Sydney, Melbourne, Canberra and London - specialise in "dog whistle politics" - fearmongering that preys on racial disquiet and prejudice ... Some say [they] did not help the Conservatives, whose share of the vote rose less than 1%, and that the dog-whistling backfired. ... <i>(Aug 14, 2005,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  That is a dog-whistle National does not want: one that sends the dog after rabbits instead of the ewes. <i>(Jun 28, 2005,NZ Herald,A11</i>)

>  The net also plays into the Tories' hands by facilitating the "dog-whistle" campaigning at which Michael Howard has become increasingly adept. Just as a dog-whistle is inaudible to humans but is heard by every canine within miles, Howard has been beaming messages at targeted groups-anti-abortion campaigners, and people hostile to immigrants, gypsies and asylum-seekers-which, if broadcast in the normal way, might repel the majority of voters. <i>(Apr 10, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: [the Observer, 10 Apr. 2005] quoted on www.worldwidewords.org/turnsofphrase/tp-dog2htm Not a Kiwi quote]

### dog-whistler
 
 <i>n.</i> (usually politics) dog whistle a concealed, coded, or unstated idea, usually divisive or politically dangerous, nevertheless understood by the intended hearers.

>  On race, however, Brash has proved a diligent dog-whistler. <i>(May 1, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

### dog-worrying
 
 <i>n.</i> sheep-worrying by dogs 1899; here indexed as sheep-worrying. [1899 Dept Ag Rep 2 Stock Inspector's Report (Griffiths Collection) Perhaps an idiosyncratic slip]

### dogable
 
 <i>adj.</i> suitable for a dog

>  My house has its dogable area out the back, already fenced off. <i>(May 3, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### dogbox
 
 <i>n.</i> the office boy, the gopher

>  When the Arnetts started, each was the dog-box boy. <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a type of railway goods wagon or a substandard compartment in a railway carriage. [(?obs.)]

>  <b>MILLER</b> Our crowd moved off from Headquarters in the railways goods waggons known as `dog-boxes' ... <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,2</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Jan 19 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tramps and Trenches 2]



 
 <i>n.</i> The `guard's storage compartment at the rear end of a unit or railway carriage (the 1970 cit. (as does the 1969) may refer to a special box or cage, or space for carrying dogs in the guard's van).

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> `The dog in the carriage with you? ... He'll have to go in the dogbox.' ... Because there was not enough room for us all in the dogbox, ... we were allowed to stand in the guard's van.] <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,31</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> The guard put the two dogs in the dogbox, and Stewart climbed aboard the train for Garston. <i>(1970,Tat,93</i>)

>  A TRENTHAM schoolboy was last night undergoing surgery ... Lower Hutt police said last night it appeared the boy had tried to get into the dogbox part of a carriage while the train was moving. <i>(Apr 23, 1981,Dominion,1/5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> poor or cramped quarters, lodgings, or room; a small sub-standard house. Compare dog-house. [ca1952 (during a Parliamentary debate, referring to small substandard houses) [I have never heard the term applied directly to a DOG-KENNEL]]

>  Mr Connolly, MP, Dunedin, said: `Houses for 700 pounds? - substandard housing, that's what it is.' Interjector: `Dog-boxes, I'd say.' <i>(Jul 4, 1952,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1952 July 4 2YA Wellington: Parliament Broadcast]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dogbox cramped quarters, originally the guard's van cage; in the dog box out of favour, in disgrace; eg `I see Jim's staying at the pub again. In the dogbox with the missus, eh?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> prison official's office [dog informer (spec. prisoners')] (See [dog informer](../D#dog-informer))

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> DOG BOX (n) Prison official's office. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

>  <b>Newbold</b> Dog-boxes are just steel cubicles with perspex windows and bars, so that the screws inside them were as much prisoners as we were. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,90</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [(obs. rural)]

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Later, traction engines [for threshing] ... had their own galley and cook, and it was a great sight to see engine, mill, water-cart and dog box - a two wheeled hut. The boss and Water Joey slept near the machinery to be handy to get up steam in the morning, and then get the cookshop going. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,90</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> wooden box-like cover over the engine of an openboat

>  Nearly all the [Otago fishing-] boats are fitted with good cabins and weather-dodgers, the open boat with only a dog-box over the engine having fallen into disuse. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/100</i>)

### dogfish
 
 <i>n.</i> [[?earlier name = ?].]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 128. SCYLLUM LATICEPS. Dumeril. Dog Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i> Scyllum laticeps</i> .. Dog-fish. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,115</i>)

>  5. <i>Ponui Passage</i>. - Mullet, kanae, dog-fish, stingaree. <i>Ibid.</i> 24/204 24. <i>Puysegur Point</i> - Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <i>Cephaloscyllium laticeps</i>, Dumeril. The dog-fish is very common. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/552</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]



 
 <i>n.</i> = spotted stargazer

>  <b>WILSON</b> DOGFISH: ,<i>see Stargazer</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 208 SPOTTED STARGAZER Similar to his cousin the Common Stargazer but thicker and with more distinctive, spotted markings on body and fins. ... Other names are Catfish, Monkfish and Dogfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,44</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER [<i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i>] ... Other names include monkfish, catfish, dogfish, and flathead. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,20</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUEW & MORELAND</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER ... <i>Other names: Geniagnus monopterygius</i>; catfish, monk, dogfish; kourepoua (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,263</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> . (See [northern spiny dogfish](no-nzx.md#northern-spiny-dogfish))

>  Conservation Department marine ecologist Clinton Duffy said it was believed that at least five species of dogfish were being described as northern spiny dogfish. <i>(Jul 14, 2005,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> All the coves of this bay teem with fish of various kinds, such as cuttle-fish, large breams, ... small grey breams, small and large barracootas, flying gurnards, horse-mackarel, dog-fish, soles, dabs ... <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,114</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [We] indeed caught some fish [near Long Island] which are common to Europe, viz. the horse-mackarel, the greater dog-fish, and the smooth hound.* [fn] Scomber trachurus, squalus canis, & sq. mustelus, Linn. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/181</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> On these stages were placed a quantity of the dog-fish and sting-ray, which were drying there as a supply against the winter; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/322 Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our hakes, tench, bream, snapper, haddock, elephant-fish, pollock, salmon, gurnards, pipe-fish, parrot-fish, leather-jackets, cole-fish, John Dorys, sword-fish, cod; various kinds of skate and cat-fish, sting-ray and dog-fish. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/269</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The Shark is rarely seen near the coast, though small <i>Dog-fish</i>, from <i>one to three feet</i> in length, are very common, and considered excellent food by the natives <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,160</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> The natives are busy fishing, and one can scarcely go near their Pas on account of the horrid smell of the Kawai, dog-fish, and small sharks, drying by thousands in the sun for winter use. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,159</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Great numbers of large dogfish prevail. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,52</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Dyer says the Dogfish (a small kind of Shark) makes bathing dangerous. He was chased by one about 4 feet long having gone into the water for a Pigeon which had been shot and dropped. <i>(1853,Journal of Henry Sewell,1/149,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> A large party of natives from the South ... brought a great quantity of dried fish, especially dog fish, ..., also some nuts of the `Karaka', Corynocarpus lavigatus. <i>(Mar 3, 1853,Traveller under Concern,84,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> After this we came to a long row of poles, on which was hung an immense supply of dried shark (the dogfish split in two), besides baskets of ku-me-ras (sweet potatoes), ... <i>(1884,Our Maoris,26</i>)

>  <i>Dogfish</i> of more than one species furnish good edible food. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/136</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  dogfish. The name used in Australia and New Zealand for different kinds of fish of the shark family. b. <i>Emissola </i>spp. TH <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),129,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> GUMMY SHARK OR DOGFISH (<i>Emissola antarctica</i>), Manga of the Maoris, grows up to 3 1/2 feet in length ... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,61</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> They caught a very large Dogfish. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/269 The day before, the <i>greater Dog fish</i>, (<i>Squalus cogris</i>), well described by Mr Pennant in his Br. Zool. Vol. III. was caught, & likewise a new kind of Codfish, [[ed. fn] <i>Gadus bachus</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>., p.120.] it has 2 dorsal-fins, & one beard under the Chin, a dark back, but is allover reddish, & even under the belly & on the fins. I had got such another <i>red Codfish</i> some days before ... <i>(Apr 4, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/246</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SQUALIDAE Includes two species of <i>Squalus </i>, the brown (<i> S. blainvillei </i>) and spotted (<i> S. acanthias </i>) dogfishes; one species of <i> Scymnodon (S. plunketi </i>), which grows up to 5 ft long and is also known as the brown dogfish; one species of <i> Centrophorus (C. squamosus)</i>; one species of <i> Centrophorus (C. squamosus)</i>; one species of the long-snouted <i> Deania (D. calcea)</i>; one of <i> Scymnodalatias </i>, a genus in which the spine is missing; two luminescent sharks of the genus <i> Etmopterus </i>; and two of <i> Centroscymnus </i>. Of these squalid sharks, <i> Squalus </i> is the most commonly caught. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,199</i>)

>  <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>. Synonyms: <i>Galeus antarcticus</i> and <i>Galeorhinus antarcticus</i>. The smooth-hound, or gummy (<i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>) ... is one of the small sharks commonly taken by trawlers around new Zealand, and popularly termed `dogfish.' The family is distinguished by the blunt pavement-like teeth - hence the popular name `gummy,' in use in Australia, where the species is also common. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/263</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  71 <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>: Common dogfish, pioke (Auckland), rigg (Canterbury). <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The</i>)

>  <i>Galeorhinus australis</i>. Synonyms: <i>Galeus canis</i> (in part); <i>Galeus australis; Mustelus australis; Eugaleus australis</i>. The southern tope, or school shark, <i>Galeorhinus australis</i>, is one of the small so-called dogfish commonly taken by trawlers around New Zealand. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/259</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

### dogfish shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [the group of squaliformes; while there are NZ dogfish species their names are not necessarily NZ and 'dogfish' itself is universal]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Spined Dogfish Shark <i>Koinga lebruni</i> (Vaillant) (Syn. <i>Squalus kirki</i> Phillipps,<i> S. canis</i> Forster, and <i>S. maculatus</i> Richardson) ... Spiny Dogfish Shark <i>Oxynotus brunienis </i>(Ogilby) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,398</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 398]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] dogfish shark, spined or spiny 83-6 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### dogged
 
 <i>adj.</i> equipped with, possessed of working dogs. [(musterers')]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The gang that year [1932-1935] was the most capable and strongly dogged I have ever worked with. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 Early November found me riding up country again newly dogged and horsed. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,57</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Old Jack Kidd ... had the place when I was first there. He wasn't very strongly dogged and, of course, that didn't help matters. His mainstay was a dog called Wad - <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,30</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of musterers and shepherds] served with dogs.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The gang that year was the most capable and strongly dogged I have ever worked with. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,57</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Early November found me riding up country once again, newly dogged and horsed. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,89</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One thing I can say is that the musterers of today are better dogged and are more capable in the actual handling of stock .. they were a strongly dogged and capable gang. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,121</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I struck what was probably the strongest and best-dogged gang I've mustered with at Mesopotamia. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,47</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A first-class musterer, always strongly dogged ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,27</i>)

### dogger
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep used for dog-tucker.

>  Killed 3 muttons and 3 dogers [sic]. <i>(1896,Glens of Tekoa Station Diary,65</i>) [NOTES: in MCRAE (1963) Glens of Tekoa]

### doggie
 
 <i>n.</i> a saveloy (? hot dog).

>  <b>PARK</b> <i>(1949,Poor Man's Orange</i>) [NOTES: check NZ context]

>  <b>BOOTH</b> [The pie-cart owner] clattered a cup and saucer ... `Like a doggie to go with it?' Darlon nodded and two minutes later he had a saveloy imprisoned in a soggy bread roll and the steaming coffee in front of him. <i>(1961,Long Night,99</i>)

### doggin
 
 <i>n.</i> a cigarette butt, dog-end [WWII Italy]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> By crikey ... wouldn't it be great to collect all the cigarette butts chucked away at the old cinemas back home? Remember all the `doggins' round the lounges and entrances? <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,146 or 170 (pb ed).</i>)

### dogginess
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a state of sheep fleece-wool in which the crimp is wide and shallow and often has a distinctive shine.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Fibres responsible for dogginess have been traced to follicles that show a grossly enlarged outer root sheath just above the follicle bulb. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,49</i>)

>  The importance of dogginess as a fault of course depends on the manufacturing performance of the wool and the characteristics of the fabric made from it. The association between very high yolk production and dogginess in New Zealand wool is apparently an artificial one. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,51</i>)

### dogging
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) shepherding.

>  <b>KERR</b> My first dogging job was at Glenlappa Station, from 1943. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,45</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and shepherding] the hassling of sheep by over-eager dogs.

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Some huntaways are continuous barkers and can cause unnecessary dogging and bunching of stock .. Ken Spence warns against all unnecessary dogging and bunching of sheep. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,39</i>)

### doggo
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) physically exhausted, dog-tired, deadbeat.

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> Usually, we'd be absolutely doggo and went to bed early. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,72</i>)

### doggy
 
 <i>adj.</i> lacking a defined staple crimp [(woolgrowers')]

>  Doggy: Lacking a defined staple crimp. Normally applicable only to Merino wool. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleece wool] having a wide and shallow crimp, often with a distinctive shine.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The most outstanding feature of doggy wool is a wider and more shallow crimp than is normal for wool of equivalent fineness. A secondary feature of some doggy wools is a very lustrous appearance. So far doggy wool has attracted notice only in Merino wool and wool from breeds with Merino in their ancestry. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,48</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Doggy: Lacking a defined staple crimp. Normally applicable only to Merino wool. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> Doggy - Plain, run-out staple showing little or no character or breeding. Applies to Merino and Merino crosses. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,17</i>)

### doggy man
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd or musterer.

>  It is my practice, and I think it is the practice of most 'doggy' men to very carefully study the characteristics of a pup before beginning on him. <i>(Aug 19, 1936,Weekly News,85</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Eventually it was decided that the musterers could have the 'penner-up' and also draw reinforcements from the doggy men across the river at 'Craiglea.' <i>(1946,High Country Days,59</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The local pub was booked out to the last bed; doggy men all, several of the boarders hailed from local stations. <i>(1949,High Country Days,185</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Doggy men out in the paddock applaud a long pull or a 'short head.' <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,179</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) man who follows or participates in dog trials with fervour.

>  At one time the Trials were confined to sheep men only, but close settlement, also low profits of sheepfarming, have driven a large number of our doggy men into the cow bail. <i>(Apr 1932,NZ Farmer,255</i>)

>  I would like you to know that your notes are very much appreciated by all doggy men. <i>(Sep 13, 1951,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

### doghair
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kemp grows for only a certain time, and is then shed ... There is in some South African sheep an occurrence of `kempy' fibres with even thicker medulla than in ordinary kemp, and these are known as `doghair' and `gare' ... <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/182</i>)

### doghook
 
 <i>n.</i> Dog-hook is also occas. used (e.g. by watersiders) for bale-hook, a small steel hook with, usually, a wooden handle at right angles to the curve of the hook (see Web3), and this is its sense in b. below.] a. (bushmen's) use) An iron bar with a bent prong [i.e. a `dog' or iron claw] for securing or hoisting a log, etc; see also [?what] [?canthook, ?chain jack]. [(obs.)]

>  <b>Z.W.WELLS</b> Tools hid in the bush under the first pine log west of my stream: Auger, truck irons, axe, mattock, doghook, hoe, Tools. <i>(May 4, 1863,Diary,51,1970 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to hang on very firmly or tightly

>  I don't take much stock of texts, mum, but I reckon that one over your head is the real straight griffin, and a fellow ought to hang on to it with both hands and a doghook. <i>(Sep 30, 1905,NZ Truth,15/7/8</i>)

### dogman
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer or dog trialist who is an enthusiast or expert about sheepdogs.

>  And this is how quite a long association started with the three top NZ dog men. <i>(Jun 8, 1994,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> If you ever ask a real dog man about his dog, and the animal's at hand, he'll never look at you, just at his workmate while talking of his time together. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,27</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> When a dog man meets another dog man, usually each knows the other by reputation - their strengths and weaknesses, style and skill. Old dog men can talk good sense and not cover much old ground talking about dogs until the candle gutters or the whisky gives out. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,85</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Bill Elliott from Glenlappa walked in with his head shepherd, and he and I talked about dog men. He was talking about a shepherd he knew ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,85</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> .. he was also a top dog man, who had won lots of trials ... [dog man] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,140</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> one who gives directions to a crane-driver, often riding on the crane's dog-hook, or on materials lifted [chk award].

>  on crane mishap in Auckland [Crane-drivers, like driving a remote-controlled helicopter] All helped by men on the ground called dog-men. <i>(Jan 28, 1987,TV1</i>) [NOTES: News]

>  [HEADLINE] Crane ballast fall hurts dogman [CAPTION] A young construction worker on the truck suffered head injuries from the fall. ... Part of the block fell on to the rear of the truck, missing the injured dogman by about two metres. <i>(Feb 22, 1990,Dominion,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Another well-known hillman was Jim Binnie, a man born and bred of dog men. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,57</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> In the back country, men have not only to be dog men - to some extent, they must be horse men too. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,30</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Today with her family off her hands she is filling the role of shepherd and at the time of my visit she and West were the only dog "men" on this 6,000-acre place. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,237</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [dog tucker](dig-dou.md#dog-tucker))

 <i>n.</i> prepared dog food done up in a roll

For the next fortnight, Wellington City Council's animal contractors DSS Management will reward dogs doing the right thing with 630 grams of dogroll. <i>(Nov 4, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### dogshit
 
 <i>n.</i> But this is poss. merely the use (or poss. an extension) of a term of common abuse; or of dog-->, a prison term for an informer. [(prisoners')]

>  <b>BEATON</b> DI: ... I just said, she's different to the usual run-of-the-mill dogshit, that's all. <i>Ibid.</i> 42 DI: ... Someone told her [the new inmate] I could score, so I helped her out. ... MA: Bit risky, weren't it? DI: Nah, she's clean. You know how I feel about dogshit. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 HELEN: ... Tell me something, Ma, what's dogshit? MA: It's a name the Boss uses. HELEN: Yeah. What's it mean? MA: Junkie. HELEN: So? I'm not the only one in for using. <i>Ibid.</i> 109 Dogshit: an abusive term, used by Di to mean middle-class, well-meaning educated junkies. <i>(1984,Outside In,42</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### dole-bludger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> 'Dole bludgers,' says Jimmy... 'No hope for no-hopers.' <i>(1995,Mum,224</i>)

>  The myth of the Kiwi dole-bludger has been exposed by statistics. <i>(May 31, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  People would have categorised my humouras kind of dole-bludger sit around and donothing humour. <i>(Apr 24, 1996,City Voice,5</i>)

>  The mythh of the Kiwi dole-bluidger has been exposed by statistics which show the percentage of unemployed Australians in New Zealand is just about the same as the percentage of unemployed Kiwis in Australia. <i>(May 31, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  When I was working as a journalist in Sydney in the 1980s (when Kiwis were labelled "dole-bludgers"), statistics came out showing that immigrants always contributed far more to the economy than they ever cost it in social services. <i>(Dec 14, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> The odd-job man, old bludger, worn-out soak/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,80,1949 ed.</i>)

>  Strewth! Forget about Kiwi dole bludgers lying on Bondi Beach - the Aussies are claiming and spending our student loans here. <i>(Sep 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A14</i>)

>  dole bludger <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  dole-bludger n. Austral. sl. a person who draws unemployment benefit without making any attempt to get work. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,330</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  The term `dole bludger' was not, for instance, uttered even once from the [National Party] conference floor during the recent party get-together in Dunedin. <i>(Aug 20, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),136</i>)

>  The mermaid was evidently inclined to the liberal or left-wing spectrum for she never used the term `dole bludger' except in the most ironic tones. <i>(Jun 1984,Landfall,150/148</i>)

>  <b>WEDDE</b> `Some young pisshead ...[sic] dope fiend ... dole bludger ... sex pervert ...how did they know he wasn't a Courtenay Place bus-shelter sherry boob-head?' <i>(1986,Symme's Hole,70</i>)

>  When every vote was needed to obtain power there were no such things as `dole bludgers', either - only potential votes. <i>(Jan 11, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  [Hdg] Wrong signal for bludgers... The Government... is abolishing the "work for the dole" scheme begun by former employment minister Peter McCardle. <i>(Nov 14, 2000,Dominion,8</i>)

>  It is inconceivable that Northland MP John "Hone" Carter would have called talk-back radio pretending to be a Maori dole bludger in 1995 if the thousands of Maori in his electorate had been able to show their displeasure at the next election. <i>(Aug 10, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  With unemployment sitting at its lowest point in nearly two decades, it becomes fairly hard to generate widespread public outrage about dole-bludgers. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### dole-bludging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Although there is less talk of dole-bludging these days there is still an unrelenting criticism from pockets in the community. <i>(Dec 24, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  The peril that dole-bludging presents in New Zealand today is hardly of that order - particularly as the government provides for a degree of cheating in that area. <i>(Feb 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  No compensation let there be / to mothers on the DPB / No compensation ever paid / to Maoris waving their Treaty. / Give us strength; make our hearts stout / To cut the general super out / from the whole dole bludging band / God defend New Zealand. <i>(Jul 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

### dolie
 
 <i>n.</i> a person on the unemployment benefit.

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Thirty bob? ... Is that all your dolie old man can afford, thirty bob?' ... to the well-off it seemed that all who worked for wages [in the 1930s] were dolies or red-feds, whatever that was. <i>(1965,Power of Joy,213</i>)

### dollar
 
 <i>n.</i> as in New South Wales, a Spanish dollar; see also holey dollar.

>  <b>KENDALL</b> And could I now part with dollars, .. <i>(Feb 26, 1822,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1822 Feb 26 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 186]

>  <b>F.MATHEW</b> [The Governor's] distribution of presents [to the Maoris] gave ... much dissatisfaction; and one vagabond who actually begged a Dollar of him had the impudence ... to bring it back, and at Captain Hobson refusing to accept it, threw it in the River. <i>(Feb 16, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 16 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 53]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Spanish dollar is the favorite coin, as in all barbarous countries, where the distinction in coinage is known; ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/183</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> After shaking hands he [John Tuawaike] pulls out his watch, and, having satisfied himself on this point, he pulls out a dollar, and orders the steward to fetch him a bottle of wine. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: -1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 234 [?Aust col or US]]

>  <b>H. WILLIAMS</b> It appears that Satan through the means of these his agents has been very industriously circulating the idea of our intention to seize the Chiefs in a short time, ... and that for those who receive our instruction we are to receive dollars according to the rank of the individual. <i>(Apr 21, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,308,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [??Sydney dollar]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Down to Wellington we travel, M.H.R. and M.L.C.,/ Where we blow the hard-earned dollars of our bleeding counteree./ <i>(Jun 15, 1892,Auckland  Star</i>) [NOTES: 1892 June 15 Auckland Star in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 82]

>  ... she ought to be worth some dollars. <i>(Sep 30, 1905,NZ Truth,15/1/5</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> Where's the gold, the dollars. <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,117</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Dealing with the tendency of the Maori to refer to all money as `dollars' - a practise persisting to some extent with the Pakeha today - ... [fn] Some New Zealanders use the term `half-dollar' for `half-a-crown'. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,63</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(? rare, obs)[check to see that TS and 1960 edition agree: TS or MS have 16?].]

>  <b>1/84</b> We succeeded in hiring a small canoe for the sum of ten [? sixteen] shillings or four dollars as they [? Maoris] call it. [check this quot against MS: ?16: my orig TS versio has 16 shillings] <i>(Feb 23, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> before decimal currency, the sum of five shillings; esp. half-a-dollar `half-a-crown' (cf wheel); the fig. pl. use `money' is rare.

>  <b>SCANLON</b> Could you lend me a dollar till tomorrow? <i>(1925,Much in Little,31</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> A job at a dollar a day fighting for your king. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 You can have a job at a ha'penny a day blocking the swamps, or a job at a dollar a day fighting for your King. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,70</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> The collection amounted in gold to [pounds]20/10/-, dollars [pounds]3/4/-, half-crowns [pounds]5/2/6, shillings [pounds]16/12/-, sixpences 16/6 - total [pounds]46/5/-. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,148</i>) [NOTES: [a collection taken up in Northland in 1841]]

>  We use a colloquialism for half-crown and five shillings and call them a half-dollar and a dollar even now. <i>(Oct 8, 1963,Dominion,4/4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `... What you boys want, Curly?' `Dollar.' ... `Yes. Dollar it is,' ... `A whole five bob?' <i>(1964,Watersiders,51</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The present colloquial ... use of dollar, usually in half a dollar, `half a crown' in New Zealand, but in Australia, where half-crowns are not in use, two shillings, should help the natural adoption of the new official term <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,133</i>)

>  The crown was called a bull, a cartwheel, a caser, five bob, and most familiar of all a dollar, the latter dating from the time when the rate of exchange was four US dollars to the pound. The only instance recorded of Cockney rhyming slang term for a coin is Oxford scholar, the derivation from dollar being obvious. The half crown was called a half-dollar, a half-bull, half-caser, two-and-a-kick, and two-and-a-buck. In the latter instance buck stands for sixpence, though apparently this term was rarely used by itself for that coin denomination, but almost always in association with a number of shillings. The florin seems to have had fewer English nicknames, only two bob being recorded as being in use in New Zealand. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,15</i>)

### dolly
 
 <i>v.</i> to crush auriferous quartz with a dolly, an apparatus for crushing and extracting gold

>  <b>HUNT</b> I dolly in it too if I come across quartz reef. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,47</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(?obs.)]

>  <b>MANNERING</b> The only way I could sit him was to have a `dolly' in front and crupper behind ... (A `dolly' was a stout stick wrapped in a sack and held with straps on the dees of the saddle.) <i>(1943,Eighty Years,42</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> It may have been that the white `hungry' appearance of much of the quartz on the surface discouraged earlier prospectors and they did not bother to test it by `dollying' and washing a few samples. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a homosexual partyner [(prisoners') Cf Mrs, Darl.]

>  Female offenders of all ages were tattooed almost exclusively with tattoos that marked homosexual relationships they had with their young `Mrs.,' teenage `Darls,' or adult `Dollies,' ... <i>(1970,Genetic Psychology Monographs,No.81/92</i>) [NOTES: (1968) 1970 A.J.W. Taylor `Tattooing...' in 1970 Genetic Psychology Monographs No.81/92]



 
 <i>n.</i> a tub with revolving blades or paddles (the `dollies' proper) for washing various materials. [(fellmongers')]

>  Sheep and lamb skins are conveyed into another building, called the fellmongery, and after being washed in a `dolly' they are painted on the flesh side ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/165</i>)

>  The pelts are first washed in pits or `dollies', with paddles revolving, in order to thoroughly remove as much as possible of adhering detritus. <i>(Aug 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/158</i>) [NOTES: (Wright)]

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Generally there has been little change in fellmongering practice since 1892. Skins are still cold-water washed in `dollies' or paddles, deburring machines are no longer used, but the de-wooling paint is applied on a moving conveyor instead of on the piled skins. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> methadone

>  Amongst addicts methadone is known as dolly or done ... <i>(Apr 17, 1986,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Close Up programme]



 
 <i>v.</i> [(obs.)]

>  ... dollying dirty duds ... <i>(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,10/4/4</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> As there is barely room in this passage for [the sheep] to swim up and down, they `dolly' one another, and rub all the dirt out of their fleeces. <i>(1886,Glimpses of Maori Land,128</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In other words when you drop a dolly of a catxch in a lower grade cricket match, which costs your team the game, you only have to suffer with 10 other players. <i>(Apr 30, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a curing vat, containing agitating paddles, in a fellmongery.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dolly: ii. a curing vat, containing agitating paddles, in a fellmongery. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,26</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dolly: i. a ewe. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,26</i>)

>  a ewe - a dolly. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### domain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RIVES</b> I took a constitutional in the domain before lunch but owing to the flatness of the country there is little to be admired. <i>(Mar 7, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,10</i>)

>  <b>WESTON</b> ... the Domain ... <i>(1890,Ko Meri,114</i>) [NOTES: ?where]

>  The whole is admirably set off by Hagley Park (400 acres in extent), the Domain and Botanical Gardens (79 acres), Lancaster Park, the Town Belts, and other public and private gardens and plantations. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,55</i>)

>  And two pimply boys/ Whisper tales/ Of intercourse with schoolgirls/ In playgrounds and domains. <i>(Sep 1952,Here & Now,30</i>)

>  <b>McCORMICK</b> Only two patches of bush had been preserved ...: one stood ... within sight of our home; the other ..., known as the Domain, covered the banks and papa cliffs for a mile or so on either side of the river. <i>(1959,Inland Eye,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a public park; often in local use the Domain, a public park, recreation ground; public reserve; originally A green area or belt of public land set aside by the New Zealand company. [1839 [to be checked with NZ Company Papers 3/1Secretary to Smith 1 August 1839]]

>  The water in question is obtained from ponds excavated in a hollow in the Domain. <i>(Dec 7, 1872,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The lovers rambling in the Domain... A merry Christmas to you all! <i>(1890,NZ Observer,Xmas : 8</i>)

>  The gallant veteran.. is William Buchanan, of Te Aroha, where he is better known than the Domain or the baths. <i>(Jul 19, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  Lee manages to maintian her formal poise throughout, despite being rquired to link items from a quirky variety of Asian landmarks such as.. the Auckland Domain, a wind-blasted West Coast beach, and the middle of some commercial strawberry fields. <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Our Domain Board (if there is such a body) have surely gone to sleep, judging from the state of the domains here, which, to say the least, are simply disgraceful. <i>(Sep 25, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  A long, narrow bridge led to the memorial hall, garage, grocery store, domain. <i>(Apr 29, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  <b>Miller</b> Radiating from this well-planned centre [of Wellington] were to stretch one hundred and [44] ten thousand fertile acres, divided into hundred-acre farms linked by roads, and the town sections were to be separated from the outer rural district by a broad belt of green domain. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,43</i>)

>  The transfer has resulted.. in less being done for the Domain than ever. <i>(Jan 15, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  You ascend a wide grassy avenue lined with pohutukawa which was once planned as a road into the Domain. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,33</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Down on the Domain half-a-dozen straddled the cross-bars of the football goals, dreadfully uncomfortable and nervous of failing, but possessed of an unparalleled view of the Southern Cross as it swung its silver nose into the sunlight and headed right across the dreamy green saucer that was Te Kamo. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,134</i>)

>  I feel I should head to the Domain for my Christmas biscuit, glass of orange and the start of this well-travelled tour. <i>((25/0/1993),Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  I feel I should head to the Domain for my Christmas biscuit, glass of orange and the start of this well-travelled tour. <i>(Dec 25, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?Orig surrounding Government House, Auckland, ??but generalised.

>  H. Government Domain. ... as it is only five acres in extent, and is situated in the very centre of the town, overlooked from every part, it is utterly unfit for a Government Domain. -->1842 Aug 12 Map of Auckland in GBPP 1842 (No.569) [facing] 136 TEMPORARY GOVERNMENT HOUSE AND DOMAIN <i>(Oct 1842,GBPP,(No.569) 185</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Oct F.Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 185]



 
 <i>n.</i>
