>  (E.W. Stafford) I am also going to get trees, seeds & plants sent out for the Govt domain and by the way the receipts for pasturage there shd. not be carried to the Genl Acct but be placed to the credit of a special fund for keeping up the Domain. <i>(Jun 19, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/467,1960 ed.</i>)

>  A large coarse grass, common in woods and warm gullies, not hardy in the Government Domain; eaten by cattle; ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/301</i>) [NOTES: (Report on Grasses)]



 
 <i>n.</i> surrounding Government or Parliament House, Wellington

>  [Legend] H. Government Domain [approx where Parliament Buildings now are] <i>(Aug 12, 1842,GBPP,(No.569) facing 185</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Aug 12 Map of Wellington [drafted F.Mathew 1841] in GBPP 1842[Legend] H. Government Domain [approx where Parliament Buildings now are]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  (A.S. Atkinson) ... Read the papers & did Maori, went for about half an hour in the Domain & found a fantail nest (young ones) & a riroriro's with two young ones & an egg. I am inclined to think the egg is a pipiwharauroa's (white, with purple spots at the larger end). Splitting firewood in the afn. I found a fine large centipede - a weri - body brown, with the articulations very plainly marked, legs a pale blue ... <i>(Oct 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/645,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> In the afternoon we went for a walk in the domain, there were a good lot of people there but no swells. <i>(Jun 12, 1864,Journal,238</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> Presently on my road to town I fell in with two Maories. I was sitting on a hill side in the [Auckland] Domain when up came my two Antipodean aboriginals who ... shook hands ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,20</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> I had strayed into the Domain, or Botanical Gardens; and the acclimatization gentlemen of Auckland have the pleasure of knowing that they have provided, in that cool grove a ... concert ... <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,187</i>)

>  <b>HULL</b> At easter time we held our sports/ Upon the wide Domain, sir,/ <i>(1907,College Songs,18</i>) [NOTES: 1903 in 1907 Hull College Songs 18]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Bede Collins (no longer, unless by courtesy, Sister Collins) was attending Anzac Day service in Auckland. There was a church service before the parade and laying of wreaths at the foot of the Cenotaph, which would follow in the Domain grounds, ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,236</i>)

>  <b>STEAD</b> [Akd] I was walking in the Domain pushing my baby Christopher in his pram. <i>(1984,Some Other Country,129</i>) [NOTES: 1965 Stead `A Fitting Tribute' in 1984 Some Other Country 129]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> But presently, as I took my usual short cut through the Domain, I saw the car ... had pulled up not far ahead of me. <i>(1975,Man of England Now,159</i>)

>  Auckland possesses a vast, open stretch of tree-fringed village common called The Domain. <i>(Jul 2, 1983,Evening Post,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [1986 a U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c. Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987) Then ... you can find a pleasant site beside the road or in a domain (park).]

>  <b>TEXIDOR</b> The Domain in front of the hotel was packed out with cars and lorries. <i>(1987,In Fifteen Minutes,159</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Texidor in 1987 In Fifteen Minutes 159]

>  <b>EVANS</b> [In 1913] T. Middlemiss gained a tender of 10s. per acre for eight acres to `digger plough' the domain. At a working bee in August [1913] tussocks and other plants were removed. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,172</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> When the train arrived, most people made straight for the [Te Aroha] Domain to secure a shady spot for their picnic lunch. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,99</i>)

>  <b>McDERMOTT</b> ... when you read `domain' think `park' ... <i>(1976,How to Get Lost & Found in NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> a domain is a locally administered PARK usually in the COUNTRY. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,33</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Ken wanted to stop at the store by the domain. ... The grass of the domain was silvered by fine rain, and the trees stood motionless, ... <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,65</i>)

>  domain ... 5. N.Z. & Austral. a park or recreation reserve maintained by a public authority, often the government. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,330</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 222; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 143]]

>  It was a hundred small towns. You used to drive through them ..., anywhere in the North Island. Fair Square grocery ... A stringy little park labelled TOWN DOMAIN. <i>(1984,High Country Weather,5</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> It seemed to me that the farm was the centre of gravity of the district - a stone's throw from the school, the tiny post office ... in one direction and five minutes' walk from the store, domain and tennis court in the other. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,104</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [Dannevirke] has pleasant domain gardens - ducks and deer, elm and ash trees. <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  A bridge crosses a small stream which tumbles down from the Domain duck pond. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,33</i>)

### Domain Board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tommy Wells ought to be acknowledged as the owner of Cambridge Domain. He has virtually himself been the Domain Board for thirty years. <i>(Feb 13, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The area was surveyed and handed over [in 1913] to the newly constituted Domain Board as a sports area. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,172</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> As an amateur actor and producer he shone in local productions, but he will be best remembered for his work on the local Domain Board. <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,201</i>)

>  The Domain board is defunct. <i>(Jan 15, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

### dome
 
 <i>n.</i> the usual NZ term for a stud-, snap-, or spring-fastener. [ca1935 Havelock]

>  <b>FRAME</b> The pair of little blue glass slippers ... which now housed buttons, domes, needles, hooks and eyes. <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,39</i>) [NOTES: (Royal Icing)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> Dome. The SNAPS that you commonly find on garments in North America and don't find in some expected placed in New Zealand. Would you believe pajama flies? <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,33</i>)

>  dome NZ & Austral. press-stud. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  The main shortages are in Mum's darningbasket - elastic, pins, domes, andbuttons. <i>(Jun 25, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/209 : 2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACKENZIE</b> dome central area of Mt Eden from which the cell wings radiate <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>) [NOTES: (Lag's Lexicon)]

### Dominican
 
 <i>n.</i> Ellipt. for Dominican gull.

>  There was a luscious `Paua' or Mutton-fish decaying on the beach, a great screaming of hungry Dominicans overhead, . <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,482</i>)

### Dominican gull
 
 <i>n.</i> = black-backed gull

>  ... the Karoro, or Black-backed Gull, in the wild and dreary solitudes of the Ocean Beach. ... named Larus Dominicanus (Dominican Gull) from his likeness to the mendicant brothers of that order ... <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,482</i>)

>  193. <i>Larus dominicanus</i> Lichtenstein, 1823 <i>Southern Black-backed Gull (Karoro [Dominican Gull]</i>) Circumpolar ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,53,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Dominican Gull Larus dominicanus</i> LICHTENSTEIN, 1823 ...The New Zealand population has increased this century. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karoro, southern black-backed gull, kelp gull. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,221</i>)

### dominie
 
 <i>n.</i> a schoolteacher. [(Southland-Otago Scots ?obs.) ca1900 R. Mason quoting Mrs Christie: ref. Southland at the end of 19C. `We always used to call the teacher the dominie.']

>  <b>LEE</b> True enough, in these days the Headmaster in school has become merely the Headmaster and is no longer the Dominie or even the `Heedmaister,' ... <i>Ibid.</i> 82 The Dominie would detail a few big boys to apprehend some of the culprits. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,14,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The sight of an eight-year-old schoolboy shambling into the class-room ... was too much for the dominie's sense of humour, ... <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,26</i>)

### Dominion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I estimate it at about two millions, or ... one-twelfth of the sheep in the Dominion. <i>(1916,TrNZI,48/156</i>)

### Dominion Day
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Yesterday, September 26, will stand in the records as the first Dominion Day. <i>(Sep 27, 1907,NZ Mail</i>) [NOTES: 1907 Sep 27 NZ Mail (Wellington) in 1985 Bromby Eye Witness History 90]

>  dominion day n, usu cap both Ds 2 : Sept 26 observed in New Zealand as a statutory bank holiday celebrating the anniversary of the coming into dominion status in 1907 <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,672</i>)

>  Dominion Day birthday shared Eighty years ago tomorrow New Zealand became a dominion and Frances Flett Dominion Daker was born at Arrowtown. <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Evening Post,5</i>)

### Dominion goose
 
 <i>n.</i> = colonial goose

>  <b>J.F. CAMPBELL</b> Dominion Goose [fn] Formerly Colonial. <i>(1943,Postscripts of Crowbar,78</i>)

### Dominion pudding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> ... she'd just opened the pudding bag to show off a big puffy white dominion pudding. [c 1930s] <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,114</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Dominion pudding raisins and batter, first appearing to celebrate our Dominion status in 1907. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,26</i>)

### Dominion sausage
 
 <i>n.</i> the orig. patriotic name during WW1 and after of German sausage (also Belgian sausage), now usually luncheon sausage ca1914. (Compare English Spam; and compare the history of `German measles' in WW1.)

### Dominion status
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Just as Massey had in 1907 opposed the adoption of the title `Dominion' ..., now, in office, he declined to participate in the formulation of the concept of `dominion status' ... Massey consistently denied that the Dominions were, for international purposes, sovereign states. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,238</i>)

### Dominion-wide
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> Twenty-five years ago I was chairman of a Dominion-wide representative conference on population called under the aegis of the Wellington Chamber of Commerce. <i>(1951,Confessions,107</i>)

### Dominyong
 
 <i>n.</i> Dominion

>  It's grim in the old Dominyong, all right. <i>(Aug 30, 1943,NZEF Times,III/114 : 6</i>) [NOTES: Probably a take-off of Peter Fraser, the then Prime Minister]

### dommyknocker
 
 <i>n.</i> [Get bommyknocker--> from D. NORTON from Joy Cowley; also as dongyknocker.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> dommyknocker a stick to beat other kids with, aka bommyknocker; origins unknown, though a `dommie' was a nickname for a Norton `Dominator' motorbike and thus for any motorcyclist, while `bommie' is a bombora or submerged reef beyond beach breakers, of interest only to Australian surfers, and their guests. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,38</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dongyknocker a club, cousin of dommyknocker, invoking the concept of a dong or knock, familiar among possum poppers, or trappers. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,26</i>)

>  Keith Bauer aged 6 knows BOMMYKNOCKER, which occurred in a radio children's story for something to knock your enemies over with: a knobbed stick: check also the term a fisherman's PRIEST. Winifred Bauer also knows the children's term BOMMYKNOCKER <i>(Oct 19, 1988,Personal Communication</i>)

### Don Clarke
 
 <i>n.</i> a powerful or well-directed kick.

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Then a hefty Don Clarke special connected with Hec Jepson's rump ... <i>(1981,Fish Factory,172</i>)

### Don R
 
 <i>n.</i> Despatch Rider

>  He reveals a desire for full coverage in a section on the "Don R's" - the despatch riders who rode their trusty motorbikes across every kind of terrain. <i>(Apr 23, 2003,Capital Times,6</i>)

### don't argue
 
 <i>n.</i> a strong fend-off

>  Hand off: Grant McQuoid gives a don't argue to Taranaki halfback James King on Saturday. <i>(Aug 30, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>) [NOTES: Caption]

### don't give me that stuff
 
 <i>phr.</i>

### don't stand there like a burnt stump
 
 <i>phr.</i> i.e. do something, move! [or similar wording]

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> Don't stand there a burnt stump. Come back up here. <i>(1976,Travelling Man,87</i>)

>  <b>TUI FLOWER</b> When someone says, `Don't just stand around like a burnt stump,' it means, do something! <i>(Jan 14, 1978,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1986 B.G. Ostmann U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c. Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987)]

### don't you believe it
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

### donah
 
 <i>n.</i> a girlfriend; sweetheart; sheila.

>  [Lad of the smart set speaks] Girls were never punctual - even `Donahs.' <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,1/38</i>)

>  He was trailing a spoony couple and become so excited when viewing the manner in which the young fellow kissed his `donah.' <i>(Aug 12, 1905,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  Dancing Donahs. [headline] <i>(Oct 20, 1906,NZ Truth,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i> an act of sexual intercourse. [rhyming slang]

### donatia
 
 <i>n.</i> [1919 NZ Plants & Their Story COCKAYNE Donatia novae-zelandiae]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Donatia novae-zelandiae</i> <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]

### done
 
 <i>v.</i> to donate (blood) [ORIGIN: abbreviation, "Having worked in the Auckland Blood Transfusion Centre, I can vouch for the frequent use of the verb "to done" which rhymes with "stone", most commonly heard in such contexts as "I have been doning [blood] for years". [Marian Barnes]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farm stock] old or ill.

>  Old done ewes will, I think, be almost unsaleable .. the sooner they are boiled down the better. <i>(Feb 3, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,12</i>)

>  A sick sheep: a sheep that is done. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### done like a dinner
 
 <i>phr.</i> thoroughly defeated etc. (See [done like a dog's dinner](dig-dou.md#done-like-a-dogs-dinner))

>  On the ball, warts and all, getta life, fishwife On the box seat, sitting pretty, what a winner Dinkie Di. She'll be right, a box o' birds, tonights the night Stone the crows, and done like a dinner. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,No 51, p19</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> 'We're going to be done like a dinner by this guy,' said Eddie. 'We're going to be arseholed out and our feet won't even touch the ground.' <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,215</i>)

>  "I was done like a dinner," Mr Atchison recalls. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

### done like a dog's dinner
 
 <i>phr.</i> soundly defeated [cf. ODE for (like a) dog's dinner]

>  <b>McGILL</b> done like a dog's dinner comprehensively defeated; eg `Last year just about every other province was done by Auckland like a dog's dinner.' ANZ. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,26</i>)

>  "We've been done like a dog's dinner," says Rugby League Park convenor Denis O'Reilly. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Capital Times,3</i>)

### dong
 
 <i>n.</i> a blow, punch, hit. [ca1935 Havelock schoolboys.]



 
 <i>v.</i> to hit, punch, bash; cf donk, sonk, sock. [(mainly schoolchildren's slang ?onomatopoeic) ca1900 C.R. Carr Dunedin schoolchildren ca1935 Havelock schoolchildren]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Dong. To strike; to punch: New Zealanders' and Australians': C. 20. Perhaps ex the dong emitted by a bell when struck; perhaps a blend of ding + dot. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,233</i>)

>  [Slang of the N.Z.E.F.] `To dong' and `to knock cold' are plain; ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>COOZE</b> I'll dong him one in the eye. <i>(1946,My Little State Home in Suburbia,34</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> `What are you writing?' `My twin she dong me on the boko.' `What is `boko', Whareparita?' <i>(1958,Spinster,25</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Joe had the hind legs [of the pig] and Toss had the ear and wouldn't let go, and Joe was trying to dong him with his mustering stick and swearing and shouting ... It was a proper Donnybrook for awhile ... <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,72</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> What's biting ya? Ya'd never rush outa the boozer in the old days. Too proud to drink with ya old cobs? A man oughta dong ya. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,45</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> I'll dong you if you say it any more. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,133</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `A joker ought to dong you; you know that?' <i>(1960,Word for Word,255</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> <i>(1963,Spinster,183</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> So I donged her on the head with the breadknife. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,77</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `I'll dong you, boy!' he shouted. <i>(1968,Sydney Bridge,6</i>)

>  dong v.t. hit, usually over the head. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

>  `I got donged on the nut and then I was sitting up in the team dressing room,' he said. `I think I must have bitten someone's finger as they tried to do something with my mouthguard.' <i>(Jul 27, 1983,Dominion,1</i>)

>  `Someone came out and donged him with a billiard cue.' Another bystander said he saw the man hit with the cue and the hammer and being punched and kicked . -->?As he came, I caught him, my feet in his stomach. <i>(Jul 17, 1983,Dominion,5</i>)

>  dong NZ & Austral. sl. 1 n. heavy blow. 2 v.t. hit. [imit.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,223</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 144]]

>  [a woman gang member] could be sitting in the pub ... and some woman would come up to me and dong me in the face. <i>(May 1986,More Magazine,231</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> dong to strike or punch, perhaps from the sound of the word: `If you don't piss off quick smart, I'll dong you one.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,38</i>)

>  Despite requests from his children to 'dong the rats', David does not intervene, believing the sacrifice is worth the valuable public awareness conveyed by the image. <i>(Nov 2002,Footnotes,No. 18/8</i>)

>  I donged a guy one Saturday. He was walking and he thought he would rush through as I put the arm down. So he got an egg on the head. <i>(Sep 10, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [??dateline ... it strikes me that if the man who performed the feat of carrying that great awkward saw in, had been with Hillery [sic] and Lowe on their first Himalayan trip, Everest would probably have been donged sooner than it was.]

>  I read in the paper the other day of a man who was fined $100 with court costs $35, and was then donged $3.50 GST on the court costs. <i>(Oct 25, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

### dong-beater
 
 <i>n.</i> masturbator

>  <b>GEE</b> Why do I always think of sex? ... Guilt. Presbyterian drug-beater's [??dong-] guilt. A dread disease. A killer <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,147</i>)

### dong-wallop
 
 <i>n.</i> (coarse slang) penis (extension of dong) [Also dong-walloper ca1935 Havelock schoolboys. ca1950 Wellington]

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> When jokers cut themselves there is a nurse [employed by the Works] to do up cuts, etc.; so others try to make out they have cut their cocks, and the terms [associated with] this are to cut your dong-wollop, kidney-puncher, bean-tosser, hawk-sword, family maker. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,29</i>)

### donga
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The land [at Anzac] rises gradually in [126] a series of hills and ridges ... It is broken by deep nullahs and dongas. and is covered by thick scrub, ... <i>(Aug 26, 1915,NZ Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Aug 26 NZ Herald (Auckland) in 1985 Bromby Eye Witness History 125-126]

>  <b>ANZAC</b> ... over dongas and gullies. [<i>Ibid.</i> p2 refers to the `men who had seen service with the Maorilanders in South Africa'] <i>Ibid.</i> 153 ... in the bottom of a ten foot donga. <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,123</i>)

>  <b>AUSTIN</b> ...held up ... by m-g [check] fire from a donga. <i>(1924,Official History NZ Rifle Brigade,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [?poss. from golfing usage]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The O.C. said when the Japs land we'll chase them into the donga, we'll chase them into the matagouri and he really believed it because he was in the first war. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,225</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a depression in land; a (small) narrow gully, or dry water course, used by NZ military since the Boer War. [Mashall's use of "on" rather suggests he is confusing it with <i>kopje</i>, a small hill.]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Like a Havelock boy ending up getting shot on [sic] some Boer War donga. <i>(1999,Harlequin Rex</i>)

>  <b>HAWDON</b> [Trooper Barry quote] `some of our scouts were down a donga and could not get out in daylight.' <i>(1907,New Zealanders & the Boer War,64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a dip in the course; a shallow gully or hollow. [(?mainly Wellington, golfers') ca1948 p.c. K.J. Cullinane (Featherston).]

>  Tee shot finishing in `Donga' may be brought back on to fairway without penalty. <i>(1960,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: 1960 Hutt Municipal Golf Links: Card of Local Rules. No 7:]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (Transf.? from 2) Pothole (in road). [ca1952 Ohingaiti hotel bar, patron speaks. You've got to watch out for the dongas in the road.]

### donger
 
 <i>n.</i> a heavy wooden club (for stunning fish with. [(mainly fishermen's)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Netta saw the dong hanging from a piece of wire and the donger on a loop of leather. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,84</i>)

>  [Fishing Col] Within seconds a beautiful blue [Mao Mao] was threshing in the bottom of the boat and Johnny was reaching for the cray and his donger ... Doubling the crayfish over he pounded the claws into a pulp against the gunwale and the shredded pieces streamed away in the drift. <i>(Apr 4, 1963,Evening Post,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a penis

>  <b>BENTLEY & FRASER</b> During choir practice at Otorohanga/ The bell-ringer displayed his new donger;/ Some thought it much thicker/ Than the curate's or vicar's,/ And everyone said it was longer. <i>(1985,Grand Limerick Tour, A,[No.]40</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> donger penis, by association with preceding entry. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,38</i>)

>  <b>LAY</b> `How's the donger?' `Bit of suturing, but still in one piece.' <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Then the big boys of the wholesale vege business moved in and I went down the donger, didn't I. I went bloody broke, virtually." <i>(Sep 19, 1998,Dominion,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TRAIL</b> I did not fully understand what this dawn ritual was all about and was horrified to discover that some of the trapped rabbits were still alive and had to be donged on the head. I was to be the donger while he was busy skinning the dead ones. <i>(1984,Child of the Arrow,87</i>)

### donger-knocker
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [dommyknocker](dig-dou.md#dommyknocker))

### donie
 
 (/douni/) <i>n.</i> excrement; poo [(children's) ca1935 Pahiatua neighbourhood: F. O'Brien]

### donk
 
 <i>n.</i> a donkey or mule

>  <b>WAITE</b> [caption] `DONKS.'/ These big mules of the N.Z. Divisional Train were bred in North America. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,33</i>)

>  <b>MANSON</b> And it's just the most glorious day possible, ... and all the villages alive with blue-gowned fellaheen marketing; dear little donks and groaning old camels .. <i>(1960,Doctor Agnes Bennett,75</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Agnes Bennett in 1960 Manson Doctor Agnes Bennett 75]



 
 <i>n.</i> a var. of dong [(primary schoolchildren's) ca1935 Havelock schoolchildren `I'll donk you (one)' or `I'll give you a donk.']



 
 <i>n.</i> an engine [Spg donk' (?airforce, fishermen)]

>  It was not until 1963 that those dependable old donks made way for 95 hp Gardiners, which pushed the <i>Echo</i> across the strait at seven and a half knots. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/45</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Better pull the motor and gear-box out and get them both done up, Jamie. I've heard your donk' in the mornings and it's running rough. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,121</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Young Bradshaw's the boy who is flying this plane,/ She is as full as a tick and it's pouring with rain./ Things are not going good - his donk's on the blink* -/ Then he puts up a blue and she dives in the drink./ [fn] *his donk's on the blink: his engine is giving trouble (airman's slang) [Charles had served in the RNZAF in WW2] <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,89</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a penis [ca1938 Havelock schoolboys]



 
 <i>v.</i> to hit, wallop, punch [(schoolchildren's) ca1935 Havelock e.g. I'll donk you (give you a donk) if you don't watch out.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> pl., esp a racehorse. Compare moke.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> However the station still has a few old donks at the homestead. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,76</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> She was always doing her money cold on the donks. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,71</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `There's no such horse as Kiwi Song.' `Too right, there is. Just a young donk but not a bad pacer at that.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,224</i>)

### donkey
 
 <i>n.</i> Spec. use of donkey-engine; or fig. as doing the `donkey work'; hauler [(forestry)]

>  HAULER. A portable engine equipped with drum and cable, and powered by steam or internal combustion. syn DONKEY <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a deer [(deerhunters' joc.)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> donkey deer, to those who stalk deer. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,26</i>)

### donkey bird
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for a penguin

>  Woe betide [visitors] should at the same time nesting penguins have tenancy of the under-floor space; for the night calls of these birds have earned for them the soubriquet "donkey birds." <i>(Feb 1947,Forest & Bird,3</i>)

### donkey deep
 
 <i>adj.</i> deeply immersed

>  "Donkey deep" may have been used with a crude meaning by New Zealand soldiers in Egypt, but then many expressions acquired temporary unsavoury angles in wartime. <i>(Sep 14, 2005,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  If Helen Clark used the expression "They are donkey deep in it" as you reported (Sept 9), is it appropriate that our prime minister should resort to using obscenities when attacking the opposition? <i>(Sep 14, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> immersed, up to one's neck (in a thing) [ca1951 Dunedin (from a Southland man) p.c. R. Mason]

>  I'm `donkey-deep' in fractions/ Since I got the ha'p'ny rise/ I'm on the `skin' in decimals,/ And my brain is all afire/ To try and make the increase spread. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,67</i>)

>  The T.M.'s are donkey-deep in cricket. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/49 : 12</i>)

### donkey lick
 
 <i>v.</i> to defeat decisively (in sport).

>  In the expressive idiom of New Zealand rugby the All Blacks `donkey licked' Transvaal. ... Their victory, decisive as it was ... <i>(Aug 8, 1960,Dominion,13</i>)

### donkey room
 
 <i>n.</i> a staff cafeteria [as where the `donkeys', or workers congregate]. [(watersiders')] (See [also donko](dig-dou.md#donko))

>  <b>DEVBANNY</b> Lafe and Holly ranged themselves in the queue in the `donkey room' [to draw their wharf-pay for the week]. <i>(1926,Laura Divine,257</i>)

>  ... the mission was still hoping to move into the upstairs section ... This was formerly the watersiders' dining and meeting area and was known in the old days as the `donkey room.' <i>(May 17, 1975,Evening Post,2/9</i>)

>  I remember how a bus would distribute cafeteria staff around what were known as "donkey rooms" (or cafeterias), the farthest of which was at the overseas passenger terminal. <i>(Dec 5, 2001,Dominion,28</i>)

### donkey vote
 
 <i>n.</i> voting by preference for alphabetical order of candidates' names on the ballot paper.

>  ROAD safety compaigner Herb Bickley is hoping to win more than the `donkey vote' at the general election on August 15. <i>(Aug 2, 1987,Sunday News,27</i>)

### donkey's ages
 
 <i>phr.</i> a variant of <i>donkey's years</i> [also heard as donk's ages. Jim writes : "I have not found this variant in any dictionary, but it is not uncommon in informal speech."]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Been teaching English for donkey's ages. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,225</i>)

### donko
 
 <i>n.</i> a room in a workplace set aside for worker's smoko, lunches etc; also occas. crib room. [(industrial workers')]

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> Up the stairs to the "donko" to wrap the sacking known as "sneakers" around his boots. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,38</i>)

>  He sits in the donko beside the rotary milking shed on the 235-hectare family farm backing on to the Feilding golf course and explains why [he buys in extra stock feed]. <i>(Mar 15, 2012,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  May I suggest the inclusion of the word `Donko' in any subsequent edition of your New Zealand Dictionary. The word, which appears to mean `the lunch room or similar facilities for workers in a factory'. The word appears to have fairly wide usage in New Zealand and I have spoken to several Australians and found them unaware of it. It do not know the origin of the term and would be grateful if any of your etymologists can enlighten me. <i>(Apr 28, 1981,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: A.R.Stone letter to editor HNZD]

>  Can you tell me the origin of the word `donko'? My correspondent assures me it is a New Zealand term for `the tea room of a factory or work-place'. The report comes from Wanganui. ... Is it a place where you donk or dunk biscuits in your tea? <i>(Aug 21, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon]]

>  ... in the woolstores. Smoko was held in the donko, where we'd adjourn after working like billyo. <i>(Apr 14, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### donnie
 
 <i>n.</i> Donnybrook

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `There was a donny down at the Foresters tonight', he said `Couple of them had this bloke down, one of them was belting him with a bottle.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,257</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Some gate-crashing bush-whackers had picked on one of their blokes and it looked like a real donnie coming up. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,164</i>)

### donnybrook
 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ usu. a brawl or fracas.

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And in his big fist he had got a shillalah,/ As if it was Donnybrook 'stead of Parnell./ <i>(1862,Auckland Vocalist,4</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> `A good feed to-night - a short, tight sleep - an early march in the morning - another fight about the break of day. Donnybrook will be nothing to it.' <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,134</i>)

>  ... as proceedings wore on, wordy arguments developed into fisticuffs, and there was a general `donnybrook' as a fitting climax to an eventful day. <i>(1914,History of North Otago,103,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> There was one between a stockman named Thomas Todd and an Irishman named Patsy which nearly led to a Donnybrook. <i>(1937,Thomas,52</i>)

>  <b>WALSH</b> Doctor and Matron were having a great old Donny-brooke. <i>(1947,Fourth Point of Star,20</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Joe had the hind legs [of the pig] and Toss had the ear and wouldn't let go, and Joe was trying to dong him with his mustering stick and swearing and shouting ... It was a proper Donnybrook fora while ... <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,72</i>)

>  <b>Rev. R.W. CANNELLY</b> I was told one of our [St Bede's College] `football' (Rugby) matches developed into a `Donnybrook'. <i>(Nov 26, 1957,Letter</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There was a bit of a donnybrook but how it finished Erik never knew ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,168</i>)

>  With this intention I made tracks through the crowd. When I got into Queen Street I found that a donnybrook had already started ... <i>(May 1987,Metro,148</i>)

### Donovan
 
 <i>n.</i> a potato - but check. [(obs.)]

>  Planted, with the assistance of Mr.Ogilvie, a quantity of Kidney donovans. <i>(Aug 29, 1842,Piraki Log,144,(ed. Anson 1911) ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass))

### doobie
 
 <i>n.</i> a marijauna cigarette

>  The law lets me drink myself to incapacity and smoke to my heart's (dis)content so why can't I pass the doobie? <i>(2004,Salient,8/33</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a pipe (See [dudeen](dou-dyn.md#dudeen))

### doodackie
 
 <i>n.</i> a thingumebob. Compare doodah, doohickey, hoodackie, etc.; hoojackie. [ca1940 (esp. children's)]

### doodackied
 
 <i>n.</i> Spruced up; dolled up (? connected with `all doed up').

>  <b>WALSH</b> Anyway ... Matron must have seen John waiting here, all doodackied up [to meet his girlfriend]. <i>(1947,Fourth Point of Star,12</i>)

### doodah
 
 <i>n.</i> Fig., a swell; a townie; a non-labourer

>  He buckled to himself, but, noticing how few hands were engaged, he ran to where the shovels were kept and carried an armful to where a number of city clerks, doodahs with the inevitablebrief-bag - probably containing property they had pinched from their employers that evening. [re Kilbirnie Tunnel cave in: Sentence is incomplete in the original - no subordinate clause verb.] <i>(Mar 2, 1907,NZ Truth,89/3/6</i>)

### doodee
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [dudeen](dou-dyn.md#dudeen))

### doodia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Doodia media. </i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> DOODIA, SCENTED DOODIA <i>Doodia caudata</i>. ... DOODIA, TAILED DOODIA <i>Doodia caudata</i>. <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>LOGAN</b> <i>(1875,Ferns of NZ</i>)

### doodlum-buck
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game, toodle-em-buck q.v. [aka doodle-em buck, doodlum buck, toodle-em-buck]

>  There were fancy stalls, produce tents, butchers' shops, skittle alleys, a shooting gallery, the good old game of "Doodlum Buck," bowling alleys, prize quoit boards, etc. <i>(Apr 14, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,5</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Pitching games were Pitch and Toss, Quoits, Ninepins, Egg Cap and Toodle-em-buck. In the game of Toodle-em-buck (Richmond ([19]19) a piece of broom a foot in length with a button on top of it stood upright in a circle of about two fet in diameter. Players pitched another piece of broom at this upright stick from a set distance in an endeavour to knock it over and send the button out of the circle. The game was played to the cry of `Roll up, Tumble up, come and play the game of Toodle-em-Buck'. There is still occasional mention of Egg Cap (Horoeka, Alexandra). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/471</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Another universal stone-throwing game was Ducks and Drakes (K-8), or Skipping Stones, which consisted simply in `skipping' flat stones across the water. feet in diameter and a button was placed on top of the stick. Players pitched another piece of broomstick of the same size at the upstanding piece in an attempt to knock it over and send the button outside the circle. The game was played to the cry of `Roll up, tumble up, come and play the game of Toodlle-em-buck.' <i>Ibid.</i> 172 An odd pitching game was Toodle-em-Buck (K-28) (Richmond, Nelson, 1919), in which a piece of broomstick a foot in length was placed upright in a circle about two <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,167</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. .. <i>Canterbury:</i> Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips,Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag;... <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

>  [Gambling] which disgraces horse-racing, is benignly smiled upon by grandmotherly legislators who know as much of betting and turf gambling as they do of three card monte, or the ways of doodlum-buck. <i>(Nov 6, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  Soldiers' Day Attractions... Cocoanut shys... Doodle-em Buck. <i>(Apr 13, 1916,Stratford Evening Post,3</i>) [NOTES: My uncle used to often used the phrase "The great old game of doodle-em buck" apropos of almost anything which I suppose he must have picked up from a visiting side-show.]

>  <b>BRUCE</b> Peter Poski ... with his crown and anchor game and his confrere with his game of `Doodlum Buck', ... <i>(1932,Early Days of Canterbury,152</i>)

>  The various sports, under the superintendence of the committee, according to the programme, consisted of target shooting, target shooting with shot, quoit matches, wrestling, three-legged racing, vaulting with pole, putting the stone, egg racing, running flat leaps, standing high leaps, 300 yards handicap racing, pigeon shooting, hurdle racing, boys' racing, and hop, step, and jump; and last - though not least - "Doodle'em Buck" and the wheel-of-fortune. <i>(Dec 28, 1871,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  THE SKILFUL GAME OF DOODLUM BUCK: A POPULAR FIGURE AT ALL RACE MEETINGS AND SPORTS. <i>(Apr 5, 1905,Otago Witness,38</i>)

>  Shooting Gallery, Doodlum Buck. <i>(Apr 25, 1891,Timaru Herald,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a game [See (synonymous) doodlum buck]

### DOODS
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Dykes Out Of Debt, a Lesbian organisation

>  DOODS celebrates its 10th birthday this month... Originally standing for Dykes Out Of Debt, it was established partly in response to a funding crisis faced by the Amazon lesbian softball and soccer teams. <i>(Jun 26, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a doodackey, a thingumebob. [ca1953 H.S. Gajadhar]

### dook
 
 <i>v.</i> to shake hands

>  <b>MEEK</b> DOOK: To shake hands. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

### dool
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream and ca1945 Varsity undergraduates. Don't be such a dool.]

### Doolan
 
 <i>n.</i> a Roman Catholic; A Catholic, esp. an Irish Catholic (occas. Irishman); see also Micky Doolan. [ca1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We'd gang up on kids who chucked off at us - like the boys from the Catholic home. They'd call us [from the Presbyterian Boys' Home in Dunedin] `Pressbuttons'; we'd call them `doolans', `doolies', `Mickey Doolans' - or worse. <i>(1984,Island to Island,87</i>) [NOTES: [c1935]]

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> A holy medal, eh? Those jokers all doolans you know. <i>(1952,Brown Man's Burden,116,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Little Gift for Harry']

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And in peace the civilians must have something to hate too. The Dutchies or the Pommies or the Doolins or somebody. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,60</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I don't let any black bugger push me around. Or any Doolan either..<i>Ibid.</i> 36 `I'm not a Doolan, Ivan.' I know you're not. But if you're ever looking for a Church, don't by-pass the onewith the big stone steps ... <i>(1985,Horse,29,C.1962 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Name and the Game)]

>  <b>PEARSON</b> They had called him `the Doolan bugger' - Doolan on their tongues meant Irish Catholic. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,63</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Webster had brutally reminded him that the original Crusaders had all been Doolins. <i>Ibid.</i> 195 There is a catholic college in the town now and we [in our rugby team] coulduse some of those ... kick everything in sight Doolins. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,92</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Not that she isn't worried enough about Eric, that's my steady in Wellington, him not being a doolan and all that. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,57</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> doolans: Catholics. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> `Yes, I'm a Doolan,' I pacified him. `At least I was baptised one.' ... It was only a short walk to the Catholic church ... . <i>(1974,Loner, The,100</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> SMITHSON: `The New Roman Missal'! Good God. Are you becoming a Doolan now? <i>(1974,Swan Song,14</i>)

>  <b>LAWRY</b> This fellow measured far short of what they wanted for their daughter, and anyway, he was a `doolan'. <i>(1980,Good Luck & Lavender,77</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> SKULLY. Once a Doolan always a Doolan. You've got holy water on the brain. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,39</i>) [NOTES: (1968) 1982 Baxter Collected Plays (Wide Open Cage) 39]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> doolan Roman Catholic <i>(1984,Blackball 08,243</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Spotted him for a Doolan right enough. Only several removed [sic: =?removes] from the priesthood. <i>(1985,G'day Country,102</i>)

>  Doolan NZ & Austral. sl. derog. Roman Catholic. [Irish name] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,223</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> doolan Roman Catholic, usually expected to be Irish; presumably from Doolan being a common name here last century. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,38</i>)

>  The Roman Catholic church (attended by `Micks' or `Doolans') was outside this ranking ... <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,127</i>) [NOTES: (Meikle)]

>  The dial tone suggested my luck with the Doolans had run out. <i>(Nov 18, 2004,TV3,17</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> Catholic

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Horse had not been in a Doolan church before. <i>(1985,Horse,42</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Quick and the Dead) 42]

>  To draw a familiar analogy, `white flight' is a bit like that old business where 15% of the population shamelessly segregated their kids from the godless 85% and put them in the care of nuns, brother, friars and other sundry medievals to fill young heads with the harmlesspapist prejudices of a pre-darwinian unenlightenment. As the Post might have dubbed that social malaise, `Doolan schoolin''. <i>(May 23, 1986,National Business Review,19</i>) [NOTES: (Warren Mayne)]

>  <b>SLIGO</b> ... the Doolan priest who was always hanging around the convent, as everybody knew ... <i>(1987,Final Things,8</i>)

### doolie
 
 <i>n.</i> a Catholic. [ca1935 at least.]

>  The classics are ignored by St Patrick'sCollege band Downstream Doolies. <i>(Aug 5, 1993,Contact (Wgtn),3</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We'd gang up on kids who chucked off at us - like the boys from the Catholic home. They'd call us [from the Presbyterian Boys' Home in Dunedin] `Pressbuttons'; we'd call them `doolans', `doolies', `Mickey Doolans' - or worse. <i>(1984,Island to Island,87</i>) [NOTES: [c1935]]

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) dags.

 <i>v.</i> pigeonhole without action. An allusion, resumably to lack of action on the 19** Report on Social Services which was referred to at the time, because of its size, as being mainly useful as a doorstop.

>  Isn't it more likely that its just going to be door-stopped? <i>(Sep 10, 1996,Radio NZ / National Radio,Kim Hill</i>)

### dopestick
 
 <i>n.</i> a cigarette. [(?smart slang: ? obsolesc) ca1969 Heard in the Victoria University of Wellington, Staff Common Room. **OED: dope-stick slang (see quot. 1918: examples are N.Z.).]

>  Cigarettes! smokes, fags, weeds, dopesticks - they are known by many strange names. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,132</i>)

>  <b>SMYTH</b> Pass me those dopesticks, there's a dear. <i>(1930,Wooden Rails,168</i>)

### dopey
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a ewe suffering from sleepy sickness, twin-lamb disease or preganancy toxaemia] lethargic.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> dopey or dozey: a ewe which loses interest in life is said to go dopey. Usually she has been suffering the lack of a steadily rising nutritional plane during her pregnancy and often is carrying twins. Her disease is thus sometimes termed twin-lamb disease. More correctly it is pregnancy toxaemia. [dozey] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,26</i>)

### dopie
 
 <i>adj.</i> of sheep, suffering from dopiness q.v. [(station and farming)]

>  Dopie. A sheep which loses condition and interest in life, is said to go d[opie]. (C.) <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

### dopiness
 
 <i>n.</i> a deficiency disease; a form of bush sickness.

>  The worst soils for `dopiness' as the local farmers call the disease [sc. bush-sickness]. <i>(1929,TrNZI,59/412</i>) [NOTES: (Aston) [Te Kuiti district]]

>  Recent investigations ion New Zealand testify to ... the value of top dressing of pastyures with lime and superphosphate where sheep are affected with dopiness diseases. <i>(Nov 1932,Discovery,357/2</i>) [NOTES: [OED2]]

>  The known deficiency diseases in New Zealand apparently mainly due to the lack of some element in the food of farm animals are:- `Bush Sickness' or [mistakenly] Iron deficiency. `Dopiness' or Calcium deficiency. `Waihi Disease' or Phosphate deficiency. `Goitre' or Iodine deficiency... Bush sickness is always found in the volcanic district on coarse rhyolitic pumice soils and `dopiness' on soils derived from fine volcanic dust or mud showers. A soil on which `dopiness' develops is a leached loam and is found on deteriorated lands, i.e., lands which originally were highly fertile due to the ashes from the `bush burn,' the land subsequently becoming gradually less productive ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,81</i>)

>  It is at lambing-time that the effect of grass-grub ravages is most severe. Ewes are low in condition and have missed the `green bite from the pastures ... The normal green-feed area ... is inadequate. The result is a high mortality in young lambs, an increased susceptibility of the ewe to `dopiness,' ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/323A</i>)

>  Some green, succulent feed just before and at lambing assists in the prevention of diseases associated with lambing - dopiness (twin lamb disease or ante-partum paralysis) and milk fever. <i>(Jul 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33/3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a state of lethargy in sheep suffering from milk fever, sleepy sickness or an associated disease.

>  .. antepartum paralysis or "dopiness" of ewes is a nutritional disease definitely associated, as a rule, with ewes falling off in condition at the approach of lambing. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,111</i>)

>  The troubles which are most likely to affect the ewes are (i) ante-partum paralysis, or as the farmer more frequently calls it 'dopiness' 'twin-lamb disease' or 'sleeping sickness' ... <i>(Sep 1, 1937,NZ Farmer,36</i>)

>  All good sheepfarmers know that 'sleepy sickness' ('dopiness') in ewes can be prevented by proper feeding at the right time. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,64</i>)

### dopo
 
 <i>n.</i> in joc. address, `dope [(WW2 Italy)]

>  She'll be back at the strip-tease stuff, dopo. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a tar-stick for tarring animal wounds.

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i> a large amount; lashings [(?tramping) [OMIT THIS]. ca1940-1950 (?informant) -->`Great dorgans of graunch' is interpreted `a hell of a lot of work']

### dork
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) defeat

### dorkess
 
 <i>n.</i> the feminine of dork

>  <b>RENeE</b> But oh no, some dork - or dorkess, let's not be sexist here - decided that that would be too easy. <i>(1998,Yin & Tonic,127</i>)

### Dorkland
 
 <i>n.</i> Auckland

>  Where are you from, ya fag?" he asks. "Oh? Dorkland!" he jeers. <i>(Jul 18, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),27</i>)

>  The couple claim to miss nothing about Auckland ... "We call it Dorkland now," Mr Weston laughed. <i>(Oct 18, 2006,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

### Dorklander
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "I'm known down there as a Dorklander, not an Aucklander." <i>(Mar 24, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  South Island apres ski - it can mean trying not to look too much like a Dorklander over a mulled wine (sadly, a dead giveaway) at the iconic Cardrona Hotel. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  He's trying hard to ignore the shouts of "dorklander" and "jaffa" from the bar. That's how they curse a northerner in the Mainland. <i>(Oct 10, 2001,Capital Times,10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Dorklanders get Blues in the bedroom. <i>(May 16, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B15</i>)

>  It was unfortunate this letter be followed by a ridiculous offering..which only lends to help the rest of us understand the true origins of the term "Dorklander"! <i>(Apr 23, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B6</i>)

### Dorper
 
 <i>n.</i> a South African breed of sheep

>  The Dorper sheep breed isn't exactly bald but has a mixture of wool and hair which keeps their bottom nice and clean... The Dorper breed was developed in South Africa in the late 1930s by crossing Blackhead Persian and Dorset Horn breeds. They are solid white or white with a black head and don't require shearing. <i>(May 14, 2003,Capital Times,15</i>)

>  New Zealand's latest sheep import, the wool-less dorper from South Africa, is the fastest-growing sheep breed Queenstown farmer Bill Loft has seen. <i>(Oct 30, 2002,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

### dorty
 
 <i>adj.</i> anything fine, excellent; beaut [(children's catchword) 1923 Pukekohe p.c. R. Mason (1953) Dorty was a very popular catchword among children for a short time.]

### dory
 
 <i>n.</i> an edible marine fish, recognised by the curious `thumbprint' markings on its sides [French dore[e-acute] gilded] (See [also oreo dory](on-ore.md#oreo-dory))

>  <b>RICHARDSON</b> 38. <i>Capros australis</i> Richardson, Zool. Tr. 3. This is probably the Dory mentioned by Polack. <i>(1842,Travels in NZ,2/211</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Dory also belongs to New Zealand, but is very rarely captured. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,625</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... a number of periodical fishes ... and these include frost-fish. barracuda, horse-mackerel, king-fish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  Of the edible [pelagic] fishes ... such as the frostfish, barracouta, horse-mackerel, kingfish, dory, warehou, mackerel, and garfish; while only the ling, hake and haddock ... are among those of more southern types ... <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>AYLONG</b> <i>JOHN DORY</i> (Kuparu) <i>Zeus faber (Zeus japonicus)</i> ... very similar species occur in all other temperate seas. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,180,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] dory [TS] 1034-42 DORIES 1024-54 ... dory, John 1038-43 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### dosing strip
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) an area formerly set aside for the treatment of dogs, against hydatids, sheep measles, etc. [(farming)] (See [dog-dosing strip](dig-dou.md#dog-dosing-strip),[dog dosing](dig-dou.md#dog-dosing))

### dosing strip/dog dosing strip
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area in a local rural locality where sheepdogs are dosed and tested for hydatids.

>  <b>MAF</b> Dosing strip: an area of ground where official dosing by hydatids control officers takes place. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,24</i>)

>  <b>PATOKA REUNION COMMITTEE</b> The present dog dosing officer Ernie Saker has made many regular visits to the farms and dosing strips in the area for the last twenty-five years. <i>(1985,Podocarp to Pasture,32</i>)

>  <b>CANDY</b> Haven't I always repulsed all those snarling dogs at the dosing strip? <i>(1988,Every Living thing,115</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> dog dosing strip: once ubiquitous hydatids depts where dogs got vigorous medicine to stop hydatids worm eggs getting back to sheep and humans, made famous in Peter Cape's ditty 'The Dog Dosing Strip at Dunsandel.' <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,25</i>)

### doss-down
 
 <i>n.</i> Noun: more often used now than the verb; a shakedown; a rough, makeshift bed; the NZ use is mainly doss-down (cf shakedown @n). [[Form first recorded in NZ.]]

>  <b>CARRICK</b> [The bed] was accounted a luxury ... compared with the doss-down the digger in pursuit of his calling was accustomed to. <i>(1892,Romance of Lake Wakatipu, A,16</i>)

### doss-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HISLOP</b> It was a great place for a doss-out. <i>(1944,Pure Gold,117</i>)

### dossing-out
 
 <i>n.</i> usu. of a vagrant, sleeping out-of-doors; also doss-out, an out-of-doors sleeping-place.

>  His offence, if it can so be called, consisted in `dossing-out', or in begging food to keep body and soul together. <i>(Jul 3, 1909,NZ Truth,210/4/4</i>)

### dotterel
 
 <i>n.</i> [pohowera, see plover.]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 64. CHARADRIUS BICINCTUS. Jard. Dotterel. Pokowera <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,24</i>)

>  Amongst birds he has obtained [at the chathams] are ...a Dotterel, differing from the common Dotterel of this country ...; a sea-bird, called by the whalers the `Blue Billy', the beak of which is singularly shaped, and of a blue colour, hence its trivial name; the Nelly <i>Ossifraga gigantea</i>) ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/64</i>) [NOTES: (Travers Notes on the Chatham Isds)]

>  62. <i>Charadrius bicinctus</i>, Jard. and Selb. Dottrel [sc]. - One of the earlier breeders, whether on the tussock-clad plains or on river-bed spits and flats; ... <i> not</i> in Yate NZ 1835 p65 for Tuturiwata (Griffiths Collection) <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,507</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  CHARADRIUS BICINCTUS, <i>Jard.</i> - Dotterel. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/126</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <i>Pluviorhynchus obscurus</i> Gm. Dotterel. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/76</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp.343-382) 358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Charadrius bicinctus</i>; a small New Zealand and Australian bird of the plover family, with two bands of colour across its breast, found on shores and riverbeds; also called double-banded dotterell, New Zealand dotterel, tuturiwhatu.

>  It is also the habitat for many birds, including the banded dotterel, which is threatened with extinction, and the black-fronted tern, which breeds here. <i>(Aug 14, 2000,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  ... the banded Dotteril flies around with warning note ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/67 (Potts Birds) CHARADRIUS BICINCTUS. Banded Dotterel. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/48</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CHARADRIUS BICINCTUS. (BANDED DOTTREL.) ... Native names.- Tuturiwhata {, Tuturiwhatu,} and Pohowera. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,210,1888 2nd edn 2/3 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The Banded Dottrel (<i>Charadrius bicinctus</i>). <i>(1921,Tutira,ch xxiii 207</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BANDED DOTTREL (<i>Charadrius bicinctus</i> ), or Tuturuwhatu is a characteristic little bird of the sand dunes, river beds and tussock clad plains.... It is widespread in New Zealand and occurs also in Tasmania and Australia <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,81</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> The banded dotterel (<i>Charadrius bicinctus</i>) runs very fast, as if on wheels. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,261</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BANDED DOTTEREL <i>Charadrius bicinctus Other names</i>: Double-banded Dotterel, Mountain Plover, Tuturiwhatu, Pohowera</i>. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,122</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Piopio ...Ochthrodromus bicinctus; banded dotterel. <i>Ibid.</i> 282 ... Charadrius bicinctus; banded dotterel; a bird. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,135</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The birds I noticed were the bell-bird, the blight-bird, the yellow-headed tit, the ground lark, the little parakeet, the banded dotterel, and the native snipe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,275,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Ochthodromus ... bicinctus </i>... below white with a band of black on the chest and another of chestnut on the [80] upper abdomen; ... Banded Dotterel. POHOWERA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,79</i>)

>  .. the men from `down under' ... <i>(Jan 1, 1905,NZ Truth,20/3/3</i>)

>  While looking for nests of the banded dottrell Mr. R.H.D. Stidolph flushed a pipit or ground lark from its nest. <i>(Nov 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  Pairs of banded dotterel make their nests early in that month [September]. <i>(Feb 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,56</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  61. <i>Charadrius obscurus</i>, Gml. Dusky, Red-breasted, or Mountain Plover, Big Dottrel [si], Tuturiwatu, Paturiwhata, Tituriwhatu-pukunui. - This excellent gamebird formerly bred on the Canterbury Plains ...; since farms and settlements have occupied the waste lands of the plains,for the most part it has retired to the mountains of the back country. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,506</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Charadrius obscurus</i>; also called dusky plover, tuturuwata.

>  ...observing endangered New Zealand dotterel breeding by the Takapuna motorway ramp... <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,26</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> CHARADRIUS OBSCURUS. (NEW-ZEALAND DOTTREL.) ... Native names.- Tuturiwhat{i and Tuturiwhatu}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,208,1888 2nd edn 2/1 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Charadrius obscurus</i>, Gmelin. (New Zealand Dottrel [sic].) <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/191</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Rako, ...Pluvialis obscurus, New Zealand dotterel. <i>Ibid.</i> 463 Tuturuatu, n. Pluvialis obscurus, New Zealand red-breasted dotterel, and Thinornis novaeseelandiae, the shore plover. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,322</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Dusky Plover or New Zealand Dotterel (<i>Charadrius obscurus</i>) [Tuturuwata]. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,44 fn</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [The banded dotterel's] relative the New Zealand dotterel, ... a larger bird, chestnut-breasted, nests on sandy beaches, mainly in the north. I know it best on the open high country of StewartIsland. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,261</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> New Zealand boasts four endemic waders not found elsewhere in the world. These are the black stilt, now limited to a few pairs nesting on high ground in the centre of the South Island; the shore plover, confined entirely to a small island off the Chathams, where its numbers are fairly strong; and the New Zealand dotterel, red-breasted in summer, rather curiously nesting in two discrete groups, one in the Hauraki Gulf, the other far away at and around Stewart Island - <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,181</i>)

>  <b>SALE</b> The New Zealand dotterel, while not rare, is classed as an uncommon bird and it is virtually unknown over a large part of New Zealand. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,143</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> RED-BREASTED (NEW ZEALAND) DOTTEREL <i>Charadrius obscurus</i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,125</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> North of Duck Creek ... is the place to see the red-breasted New Zealand dotterel, the size of a slim bantam hen but not as fussy. <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,204</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  63. <i>Charadrius ruficapillus</i>, Temm. Red-capped Dottrel [sic]. - This wader is now included in the list of New Zealand birds. [Is of Australian provenance] <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,508</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

### double
 
 <i>n.</i> a bet placed at the same time on two horses to win (often spec. races). cf doubles chart; cits needed.

>  A local boniface had a ticket on the winning double at Trentham on Wednesday and received nearly 250 [pounds] for his investment. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/2/2</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `It isn't a call-out, Mr Pilgrim, it's an invitation. He said to tell you he struck the double and they're having a hooley.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,71</i>)

>  He.. had admitted that he had been taking "doubles."... He had never admitted "running any doubles." <i>(Feb 5, 1924,Otago Witness,34</i>)

>  <b>BIRD</b> A man known as 'Jacko' ran a double on the two contests, 1/- for GBP5. <i>(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,130</i>)

>  Just as L'Allegro surprised an army of punters.. at Ellerslie in 1932, so did he amaze the optimistic double punters who took on every available shilling on offer about the Jonathan-Star Artist combination. <i>(Mar 14, 1934,NZ Truth,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to deceive; to use trickery (on) [ca1950 still used]

>  <b>ROCK</b> . come the double on me ... <i>(1880,Colonists,60</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep which has missed a shearing. [(farming)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Double or double-fleecer Double: Sheep which has missed shearing once or more. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When he took over [the station], the only domestic sheep were `roughies' - long tailers and doubles that had multiplied from those which had escaped the wind-up muster four years before. <i>(1973,Big Country,86,1977 ed.</i>)

>  It was away back where roads were unknown, ... and where a few hundred sheep annually evaded the shearing muster, and remained among the scrub and flax bushes to develop into`double-fleecers'. <i>(Apr 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,48</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We lived on wild pig ... and the fat wild sheep and double-fleecers that could be raked in from river cliffs; ... <i>(1926,Tutira,224</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a two-ounce packet of prison tobacco. [(prison)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> DOUBLE (n) Two-ounce packet of prison tobacco. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> twice the standard nip; also called double-header, doubler. Contrast Brit. large.

>  `Bloody oath, Mat what good luck!' ... `Shout you eh?' `Too right you can. Double whisky.' <i>(1984,Some Other Country,182</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Hulme `Hooks and Feelers' in 1984 Some Other Country 182]

>  <b>HULME</b> `Too right you can. Double whisky.' And I get that from him and a jug and another couple of doubles and another jug. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,79</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `We've got two doubles down him. Neat. ...' <i>(1981,Fish Factory,78</i>)

>  <b>HALEY</b> All I had to do was to pour one glass into the another and I'd have my double. ... `Double whisky,' I said. <i>(1989,Transfer Station,41</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> of locks or doors, to be double-locked

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> double locks put on the double so that they cannot be opened with ordinary keys <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [ell. double-fleecer or double-decker] a sheep that has missed at least one shearing.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When he took over, the only domestic sheep were 'roughies' - longtailers and doubles that had multiplied from those which had escaped the wind-up muster four years before. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,86</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> short form of double-bank, also occas. as n.; see also dub. (See [double-bank](dou-dyn.md#double-bank))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> San Severino and coming down the track on the company bike with Mick doubling me on the bar. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,187</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> A bike came past: a big boy doubling a girl on the crossbar. <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,57</i>)

>  Philip [aged 5] ... was being doubled on the bicycle by Bonnie Helen Robertson. <i>(Oct 30, 1966,Sunday Times (NZ),44</i>)

>  <b>GROVER</b> I was the only one who had a bike, and I couldn't double Con and Camille together ... <i>(1967,Another Man's Role,20</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The agent ... came out to the farm [on the motorbike] and doubled me back to town. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,84</i>)

>  <b>KOEA</b> Once I did let my legs dangle too close to the wheel as I was doubled somewhere on the back of a bike ... <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,67</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an event in a dog trial contest with two dogs and six sheep.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. he [the dog]'s a bit lively yet but he had just won a double with him and with age he should be a top dog. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,191</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> All were heading dog competitors and half would compete in a singles course (one dog working three sheep) and the rest in doubles (two dogs and six sheep.) <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,20</i>)

### double All Black
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Double All Black One of the highest honours that a New Zealand sportsman can receive is to represent his country both at rugby and at cricket. The following is the list of double All Blacks who have represented New Zealand in these games: <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/33</i>)

>  Apart from the rather curious term double All Black, recorded in DNZE from the 1960s and meaning a man who has represented New Zealand at both rugby union and cricket (even though our cricketers have never been called All Blacks), there were no other secondary formations for another 75 years, when the previously overshadowed soccer code in New Zealand enjoyed its finest hour as the national team against huge odds qualified for the 1982 World Cup in Spain <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2(1) : 5</i>)

### double banking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> DOUBLE BANKING: One horse team added to assist another. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,186</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 186]

>  ... my three leaders were hooked in front of Tommy's team. Having given him a pull to the crest, they were brought back, ... This practice was known as double-banking, ... [West Coast, c1900] <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,130</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> splitting a train in half to enable one engine (otherwise insufficiently powered) to draw each section up a hill.

>  <b>McGILL</b> We might make Wharanui in a couple or so hours. Unless ... we stopped on the hill. ... When it did, you had to split the train in half, double-banking. Took one half at a time. <i>(1985,G'day Country,119</i>)

### double beat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the taking of two areas or beats on a muster, e.g. the top and middle beat.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> But I was in my twenties and double beats were nothing to me ... <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,1</i>)

### Double Brown
 
 <i>n.</i> a brand of beer

>  <b>LACEY</b> He's as sweet a sight as a line-up of frosty DB Double Browns on a mid-summer's day. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,24</i>)

### double crush
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [crush](con-cyp.md#crush),[crush pen](con-cyp.md#crush-pen))

>  <b>BOWEN</b> In a double crush (i.e. two pens) a good long gate, of approximately four feet width, should [121] be used at the end of the dividing fence to lead the sheep from either pen into the race. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

### double cut
 
 <i>n.</i> [shearing]

>  The fleece, being free from double cuts, is worth considerably more. <i>(Nov 27, 1890,Otago Witness,11</i>)

### double dipping
 
 <i>n.</i> the practice of deriving income from more than one source, at least one being a State benefit or government superannuation. [Jim writes: In NZPOD but not DNZE. Not in Collins. NSOD tags it as <i>NAmer & SAustral.</i>]

### double dump
 
 <i>v.</i> [Poss. move these dumps to dump n and v??]]

>  dump, to double dump v.t. compress wool bales, as by hydraulic pressure; dumper wave that collapses suddenly in the surf, tossing surfer on the hard sand; whence, to dump, and getdumped. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

### double dumping
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A powerful hydraulic press forces two bales into the space formerly occupied by one, and they are bound with steel bands, or wires. (This process is called `double dumping' or `dumping'.) <i>(1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,67</i>)

### double eyes
 
 <i>n.</i> spectacles; glasses. Compare four-eyes.

>  <b>FOSTON</b> `Where's my specks, mother?' Jacob's double eyes were soon brought. <i>(1921,At The Front,144</i>)

### double fleece
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) fleecewool that has been growing for two years.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Looking up he could see about twenty-three double fleece sheep pulling out through a saddle right up in the shingle. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,39</i>)

### double footed
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> They were scientific, too, these fences. ... six posts to the chain, six battens between each, strainers double footed and stayed; eight tightly strung wires, the top barbed. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,177</i>)

### double G
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a nickname given by political opponents to Sir George Grey, from joining his initials with an alleged penchant for double-dealing.

>  ... the great landed estates have originated in Sir G. Grey's 5/- an acre regulations. ... The result ought to be a caution to working settlers against such friends as double G. <i>(Dec 27, 1877,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/440</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

### double garage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The double garage has been accorded the status of temple. <i>(Feb 15, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### double gate
 
 <i>n.</i> [(farmers')]

>  Double gate: Two gates from the race in a sheepyard, which allow the sheep to be let into any ofthree pens, so that a mob can be drafted into three lots at once. Double gates were introduced into Canterbury about 1868. (C.) <i>(Oct 7, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

### double happy
 
 <i>n.</i> a large firecracker (and figuratively a large nuclear explosion) [(mainly children's) Pl. spg usu. double(-)happys. ca 1930s (DEH)]

>  It takes the double-happies-in-a-tin approach to guest selection... <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  Alistair.. would be riding down Victoria Avenue with Double-Happies strapped to him and being exploded on his BMX. <i>(Dec 6, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Wrapping a Double Happy in cotton wool we jammedit in the mouth of a full bottle of turps, lit the fuse and ran off to watch. <i>(Oct 30, 2002,Dominion Post,5</i>)

>  BURGLARS who took $2000 worth of fireworks from a Kilbirnie fruit shop left only one packet of double happys [sic] on the shelves. <i>(Nov 1, 1984,Dominion,5</i>)

>  It was quite good, but didn't compare with a Mighty Cannon in a tin can. It was round the store [in the ?1960s] we would have our first late October battle with lighted Tom Thumbs and Double Happys. <i>(1985,G'day Country,62</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> Guy Fawkes night, double-happys exploded in her metal letter box. <i>(1985,Gilmore's Dairy,9</i>)

>  `I had a fair idea it was lightning. There was a roar like a double happy (fire cracker) going off inside a rubbish bin' <i>(Feb 17, 1988,Press, The,9</i>)

>  Does it mean that the protestors approve Communist Chinese doctrines, the thoughts of Mao - and the Nanking nuclear double-happy. <i>(Jul 25, 1972,NZ Truth,2/1</i>)

>  [Hdg] A night to make you double happy... This year's 15-minute fireworks hatbour spectacular explodes into life at 9pm on October [sic] 5... <i>(Nov 4, 2004,Wellingtonian,4</i>)

### double long
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A "double long", or "doppio espresso" has two shots of coffee for a full cup. <i>(Jan 22, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

### double plugger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  What'll be next to your bed when you waken in the morning?.. Foot falcons or dubble [sic] pluggers. <i>(Mar 15, 2004,Salient,No. 3/11</i>)

### double shearing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the shearing of sheep twice in twelve months.

>  Double shearing was begun originally because of trouble with blackberry ... <i>(Nov 7, 1963,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  Ted Lawrence has tried double shearing in order to avoid the hay/wool clash, but found it wasn't worthwhile, as the lambs still had to be shorn in December. <i>(Dec 10, 1964,NZ Dairy Exporter,59</i>)

>  The difficulties of acquiring labour, due to high wages offering in mines and sawmills, make double shearing a problem for most farmers. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,29</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> With lambs and double-shearing 35,000 sheep were shorn last year ... <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,161</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. everything is double-shorn and including lambs over 30,00 sheep go over the board. [double-shearing] <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,186</i>)

### double unit house
 
 <i>n.</i> [1957 Dec p.c. J.H. Robb Some suggestions on N.Z. usage [include]: DOUBLE UNIT HOUSE, a semi-detached house.]

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... state houses (owned and let by the government) are occasionally double-unit houses. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,172</i>)
